**The ***reason of the image of God*** was this.** ***Man was founded to the image of God.*** But this image extinct and corrupted, no one could more rightly restore to the whole than the ***eternal and essential image of God the Father.*** But that this be rightly done, it was fitting that He be both God and man — that is, of the Father from eternity, and then image of God, such as man is. And on this cause He, as ***eternal Son of God***, willed to be born also ***Son of man*** from the Virgin Mary.

**Human fragility requires a Mediator of whose faculty and will one may not doubt, and to whom one may dare to accede in one's anguishes.** For if the Mediator were only man, we should doubt of His faculty and power — whether He could hear and help us. If He were only God, we should doubt of His will — whether He would certainly wish to hear us wretched men. The Mediator therefore is ***omnipotent God***, and man ***our brother***, who both can and wills to hear, aid, and deliver us — ***God and man***, whom in each nature — divine and human, or ***assuming*** and ***assumed*** — as [***omnipotent***] on account of the ***hypostatic union*** and ***communication of divine idiomata*** (truly, as it were, human — of the assumed and glorified or exalted nature) — we ***worship, adore, invoke, preach, and celebrate.***

#### Whence the Doctrine of the Communication of Idiomata Arose — Arius and the Council of Nicaea

**It has been said hitherto that in Christ two natures — ***divine*** and ***human*** — are ***inseparably united in one Person***, without confusion of natures and properties.** This doctrine the devil has impugned in all times — chiefly through Arius, Nestorius, and Eutyches. Of Ebion, Cerinthus, Artemon, and Samosatenus we shall not now say — since ***Arius*** renewed the heresy of all these in the year of Christ 322.

**He was first a Deacon of the Alexandrian Church**, and defended the heresy of Meletius denying that lapsed [Christians] were to be received into manifest crimes: therefore Arius was expelled from the Church by Bishop Peter, whom, having been consumed at Alexandria by glorious death for the name of Christ, ***Achilles*** succeeded. He received Arius, and set him over the theological school. But when Achilles was dead, Arius aspiring to the Bishopric, and taking it hard that ***Alexander*** was chosen, from desire of vengeance impugned the doctrine of Alexander concerning the ***Son of God co-eternal and consubstantial with the eternal Father***, asserting that ***the Son is not truly and by nature God***, nor is co-eternal with the Father, but had a beginning, and was founded from nothing.

**And he used these formulas of speaking**: [***ἦν ὅτε οὐκ ἦν***] — ***"there was when He was not."*** And again explaining himself: [***καὶ πρὶν γενηθῆναι, οὐκ ἦν***] — ***"and before He was begotten, He was not."*** ***"Was Father when there was not yet Son."*** Item he said that He was [***ἐξ οὐκ ὄντων***], ***"from things not existing"*** — that is, ***from nothing founded, the highest creature.*** In this his sentence Arius drew many Bishops and others, and there arose public and great discord.

**Constantine the Great**, who had already restored peace to Christians, wished lovingly to compose that controversy, and he himself wrote letters to Alexander the Bishop, and to Arius the presbyter, admonishing both to salutary and pious [***ὁμοφροσύνην***], showing what scandals to the Gentiles they should give if they were not concordant. But Arius persevering in his blasphemy, Constantine sent ***Hosius Bishop of Cordoba*** to each, that he might compose the case and dissension. But the fire increasing, he instituted a ***Council at Nicaea*** in Bithynia. There came also presbyters, and legates of Bishops who on account of old age and diseases could not themselves come. There were present also Monks and Deacons, among whom ***Athanasius*** — still constituted in the class of Deacons — was eminent. Laymen also learned and pious came. And the ***Emperor himself*** came to Nicaea, and to the holy Fathers, sitting in the middle on a lesser seat than theirs, made of gold material, having given thanks to God for that assembly of friends and fellow-servants of his (for so he himself had spoken), commanded them to inquire what Arius said, and to judge whether he wandered from the true sentence.

**The first sentence** was given by ***Eustachius Bishop of Antioch.*** And the rest of the Bishops, when they heard the dogma of Arius read — that Christ was not God, but founded and made by God, diverse from the paternal nature (which blasphemy they write he had drawn from some books and papers of Origen not yet published and covered with silence) — with just and pious indignation ***rose from their seats***, and ***tore up the schedules of Arius***, and decreed that Arius with his sectaries was to be cast out from the assembly of the orthodox by public heralding. And of the Son of God they decreed by unanimous consent — a few excepted — that He is:

- [***ὁμοούσιος***] — of the same essence
- [***ὁμοδύναμος***] — of the same power/dignity
- [***συναΐδιος***] — of the same eternity

**with the Father.**

There was written also the ***Nicene Symbol*** up to these words: ***"Whose reign shall have no end."*** For here the Symbol ceased, and what follows concerning the Holy Spirit was afterwards first added in the ***Council of Constantinople*** against ***Macedonius Bishop of Constantinople***, who had named the Holy Spirit ***a creature***, as it is written.

The Emperor Constantine approved and confirmed the sentence of the Bishops, with an edict added — that all be damned by that [edict] who contradicted the orthodox Fathers. Constantine himself also subscribed, and called himself ***fellow-minister of the Bishops.*** Arius, however, [***αἱρετικόν, χριστομάχον, θεομάχον, πονηρόν, ἐχθρὸν τοῦ Θεοῦ***] he named. The Arian Bishops also — ***eleven*** — from fear of the Emperor, without delay subscribed, dissembling that they embraced the Nicene Symbol. But ***six*** with Arius underwent the punishment of exile.

#### The Three Chief Causes Treated at Nicaea

**The chief causes, of which the [Nicene] Synod showed the testimonies from the sacred Scriptures, are to be considered by us. There were ***three causes*** which pertained to the doctrine.**

**The first cause was the ***repetition and damnation of the impious dogma of Samosatenus***, who elected as Bishop of Antioch (in which place the name of Christian was first heard, Acts 11), when he had been in office 4 years, recalled the heresy of Artemon — and in favour of the Arabian woman Zenobia the Queen, transformed the doctrine of the Messiah into Judaic opinions.

**The second cause was the ***damnation of the Arian dogma***, which was nothing else than the contagion of Ebion, Artemon, and Samosatenus — as ***Alexander Bishop of Alexandria*** rightly wrote. For Arius had taught:

**1.** ***Christ is not God, but a creature.*** And when he was convinced by the orthodox from the sacred Scripture, so that he was compelled to affirm ***Christ is God***, he conceded this by that reason by which ***Angels and all the saints are named gods and sons of God.***

**2.** But when he was further compelled to the confession that ***Christ is true God from the true God***, he conceded also this, with his blasphemous interpretation concealed — that ***Christ is true God made from the true God***, since ***everything made of God is true*** — that is, ***Christ is a creature.***

**3.** When this fraud was detected, he was driven by the Word of God to the confession that ***Christ was before the creation of all things***, or before the whole world — which Arius also conceded, wishing indeed nothing else to be understood than that ***Christ was founded before all other creatures***, and was [***κτίσμα***] or [***ἔργον***], [***πρωτότοκος πάσης κτίσεως, ἀρχὴ τῶν ὁδῶν Κυρίου εἰς ἔργα αὐτοῦ***].

**4.** But when the orthodox Fathers showed that ***through the Son of God all things were founded***, as John evidently attests — Arius conceded he had said this also, by this sentence: ***Christ first and before all things was created, and afterwards through Christ all things were founded.***

**5.** Again when the pious said that ***the Son of God is begotten, and was not made***, Arius conceded the same also — understanding ***begotten*** in that manner by which all believers born of God are named ***sons of God***, but ***not made among the other creatures***, but ***made before all other creatures.***

**6.** But when the orthodox affirmed that Christ is [***ὁμοούσιος***] to the Father, [***καὶ ὁμοφυὴς***] — consubstantial, co-eternal, and of the same dignity with the Father — here the Arians ***fell silent***, and were unwilling to say either [***ὁμοούσιος***] or [***ὁμοφυὴς***] — much less [***συναΐδιος***] — just as also ***Servetus***, burned at Geneva, when he was now about to die, ***refused to invoke the Son of God as eternal God.***

**And this was the second cause of which the Nicene Synod showed the testimonies.**

**The third cause was the ***damnation of the error of Novatus and Meletius***, who taught that ***the lapsed returning to penance were not to be received.*** On this matter the Emperor, jestingly reprehending the arrogance of Acesius Bishop of the Novatians, said: ***"Acesius, apply a ladder, and ascend alone to heaven."*** By this ***irony*** he signified that ***he was feigning falsely that he and his own were*** [***καθαροί***].

**It happened also in that Synod that ***libels*** offered against some Bishops the Emperor neither read nor allowed to be examined**, but ordered them to be preserved sealed, until the public cause should be dealt with. Then he ordered the ***fasciculus of private accusations*** to be brought forth at the exit of the Synod, and still sealed to be cast into the fire, with all the Bishops looking on, saying: ***"Even if I myself should see some Bishop delinquent, I would cover him with my own purple — nor would I suffer the crimes of priests to be borne into the public, since men's minds are alienated by such scandals from the doctrine."*** Thus therefore the most pious Emperor sanctioned [***ἀλάλητον***] of private offences, and exhorted the Bishops to guard concord in the propagation of true doctrine and in the whole of life. Thence he called the holy Fathers into the city, a banquet being prepared at which he himself was present. Afterwards he sent each into his own parish, affected with honours and magnificent gifts. This was the outcome of the Council of Nicaea, which lasted more than three years.

#### Post-Nicaea — Arius's Death and Persistence of Heresy

**A little afterwards Constantine — by the deceit of Constantia his sister, instructed by an Arian priest, persuaded that Arius did not think evilly — recalled him, and ordered the case to be examined again at Tyre.** But at length, when the case of Arius became gradually more plausible, and the courtiers made much of him, ***God Himself punished the blasphemous soul with a death like that of Judas.*** For when at Constantinople Arius was to be publicly received into the communion of the Church with solemn and courtly pomp, ***Alexander Bishop of Constantinople***, the orthodox one, at evening entered the church, prostrate on the ground he deprecated God through the whole night, that He should not suffer ***Arius the wolf to enter His sheepfold***, or should ***first remove him from life.***

The next day, when from the court of the Emperor (some report Constantine himself, others the son Constantine) Arius by the courtiers and his faction was being magnificently led to the Church — by sudden consternation of soul and sorrow of belly he was seized, and turning from the way sought the ***necessarium***, and sitting on the ***latrine***, his intestines, liver, and spleen being poured forth, ***by defecation miserably perished*** — a foul man oppressed by a foul death. The multitude, having recognised his death, dispersed. And ***Eusebius Bishop of Nicomedia***, the Arian, departed overcome by shame and confusion — and again the ***authority of those rightly-thinking*** began to be greater.

**After the death of Constantine the Great**, that same Eusebius renewing the dogma of Arius, alienated ***Constantius*** from the orthodox — Athanasius being driven into exile. And many Bishops began to propugn the blasphemy of Arius who before through fear had been silent. And the ***Synods of the Arians*** also were frequent, corrupting the Nicene Symbol — at ***Sirmium, Ariminum, Milan, Seleucia, Constantinople, and Antioch.*** In the Synod of Seleucia many received the word [***ὁμοιούσιος***], which before the Synod of Sirmium had decreed to be placed in the Symbol in place of the word [***ὁμοούσιος***], that thus by this cothurnus the Arians might be placated and acquiesce. And they write that ***Hosius Bishop of Cordoba*** was so acted upon, that he subscribed to that decree. But soon, the deceit being recognised, they were repudiated by the orthodox.

**In the Synod of Antioch**, which was very turbulent, and did not wish to admit even the word [***ὁμοιούσιος***], ***Acacius the Arian Bishop*** used this argument:

> ***The Father worships no one*** — or as he himself was accustomed to the word, [***οὐ σέβει τινά***].
> ***The Son worships the Father*** — or [***σέβει τὸν Πατέρα***]. So great was the impudence and profanity of Acacius, that he thus spoke.
> ***Therefore they are dissimilar.***

**Similar to this argument is this:**

> ***The one honouring is less than the one honoured.***
> ***The Son honours the Father.***
> ***Therefore the Son is less than the Father.***

**But it must be responded**: ***Honour and obedience do not take away the equality of essence and power, but distinguish the order of Persons.*** But it is true that ***the Son honours God the Father more than any creature***, because from eternity most perfectly He knows the Father. And the Father in turn honours the Son (John 8). And here belongs the saying of Phil. 2: ***"When Christ was in the form of God"*** — that is, essence and power God [***ὁμοούσιος***] with the Father — ***"He did not seize the equality of God"*** — that is, ***He did not use His power for the defence of Himself against the limits of His mission and vocation***, but emptied Himself, taking the form or nature of a servant, ***not having defence***, in habit found as a man — that is, ***in gestures and in the properties of human nature passible and mortal*** — made obedient unto death, indeed the death of the cross: on which account God ***exalted Him,*** etc.

**Here again the Arians argued**: ***God by nature cannot be exalted. Christ was exalted. Therefore He is not God by nature.***

**I respond to the minor.** ***Christ was exalted according to the human nature***, made impassible and immortal. Moreover there was a different time of humiliation, a different [time] of glorification — nor does obedience take away the equality of power. But of the Council of Nicaea against Arius, and of some other things worthy of consideration, let what we have said suffice.

#### Extended Treatment of Nestorius + Eutyches — The Two Extremes

**As to Nestorius**, we have said above — whom Theodosius had ordered to be called from Antioch to the Bishopric, when now ***Ambrose, Martin, Jerome, Augustine, Hilary, Eusebius***, and others were dead. For this Nestorius the newcomer Theodosius had thought would be equal to them. But the most excellent Prince was deceived, as often is wont to happen. Against Nestorius, however, under ***Theodosius the younger***, the Council was convoked in the city of ***Ephesus*** in the year of Christ 435, with 200 Bishops congregated, among whom ***Cyril of Alexandria*** was eminent.

**As Nestorius was damned on this account, that he separated the two natures in Christ, and as it were feigned two Persons: so ***Eutyches the Archimandrite*** (for so he was called) or Abbot in a monastery near Constantinople — equally unlearned as [Nestorius] — fell into the other extreme, and affirmed that ***the divine [nature] with the human nature had collapsed into the same composite***, and one had been made — nor were the natures to be distinguished in any manner. And as ***Nestorius, Bishop of Constantinople***, tore apart the natures, so ***Eutyches confounded the same.***

**Nestorius's ratiocination was this:**

> ***What is born from eternity, cannot be born from a man at a certain time.***
> ***The Son of God was born from eternity of the Father.***
> ***Therefore the Son of God was not born of the Virgin Mary.***
> ***And consequently Mary cannot be called*** [***Θεοτόκος***], ***but rather*** [***χριστοτόκος***].

**I respond to the major.** ***What was born from eternity, cannot be born at a certain time*** — namely, according to that eternal nativity. But He can be born at a certain time according to another nature which He assumes. Therefore Paul says (Rom. 1): ***"The Son of God was born [***κατὰ σάρκα***, according to the flesh]."*** And this particle [***κατά***], as distinctive, afterwards ***Athanasius*** and ***Cyril*** use, that they might show the distinction of the natures.

To the minor I respond: ***The Son of God was born from eternity of the Father.*** And this same Son, according to the decree of God, willed also at a certain time to become man, and to the divine nature to unite the human nature by hypostatic union. Truly therefore the Son of God was born of the Virgin Mary — not according to the divine nature, but according to the human nature. And ***Mary is*** [***Θεοτόκος***], or ***Mother of Jehovah***, as Elizabeth speaks.

**There is also this customary argument, which ***Eutyches*** opposed to those thinking rightly**, that thus [***ἀσυλλογίστως***] he might conclude that there is only ***one nature in Christ***:

> ***No nature is without Person.***
> ***In Christ are two natures.***
> ***Therefore also there are two Persons.***

**I respond to the major.** Let the major be valid in all others besides Christ, in whom two natures are united by ***hypostatic union***, [***ἀσυγχύτως, ἀτρέπτως, ἀδιαιρέτως, ἀχωρίστως***] — a common ordination and series of created things.

**Eutyches in the year of Christ 449 damned Nestorius, and (as we said) fell into the other extreme, constituting only ***one nature*** in Christ.** Often it is wont to happen that while men wish to avoid one extreme, they strike upon the other — as many examples of our age attest. And many wishing to flee the delusion of Eutyches, become ***Nestorians and Quaternists***; and conversely, wishing to remove the opinions of Nestorius, they fall into the absurdities of Eutyches.

**But diligently must the difference of the ***Nestorian and Eutychian dogma*** be considered.** ***Nestorius*** was unwilling to attribute the idiomata of the human nature to the divine nature in Christ, and therefore he denied Mary [***Θεοτόκος***], separating the divinity from the humanity in the very moment of the Incarnation. But ***Eutyches***, on the contrary, ***confounding the two natures***, said that they had coalesced into a single third nature — thus taking away both the divinity of the Word (which cannot suffer or be diminished) and the true humanity (which cannot be swallowed up).

**Between these two errors — Nestorius separating and Eutyches confounding — stands the ***orthodox Chalcedonian formula*** of the Council of Chalcedon (451), decreed against Eutyches through Pope Leo's ***Tomus ad Flavianum***: ***"one and the same Christ, Son, Lord, only-begotten, in two natures inconfusedly, unchangeably, indivisibly, inseparably; the difference of the natures being nowhere taken away by the union, but rather the peculiarity of each nature being preserved, and concurring into one Person and one hypostasis — not divided or separated into two Persons, but one and the same only-begotten Son, God-Word, Lord Jesus Christ."*** This is the paradigm-***Chalcedonian settlement*** which we retain in the confession of the Church of Christ.¹⁰⁷ᵉᶜ

#### On the Communication of Properties — De Communicatione Idiomatum

**Since Christ is ***one Person in two natures***, and the two natures are so ***inseparably united*** in the one Person that they cannot be torn apart, there arises the doctrine of the ***communication of properties*** (*communicatio idiomatum*) — which is ***a mode of speaking, and of the reality itself, in Christ***, by which the properties of one nature are attributed to the whole Person, or to the other nature, on account of the union of the two natures in one Person.**

**This communication of properties is threefold** — as ***Chemnitz*** most learnedly has expounded in his book ***De Duabus Naturis in Christo*** (1571):

**The First genus, called ***genus idiomaticum***** — by which the ***properties of each nature*** are attributed to the ***whole Person*** in the concrete. As when it is said: ***"The Lord of glory was crucified"*** (1 Cor. 2:8), where suffering — which belongs to the human nature — is predicated of the whole Person named by a divine title. And ***"God purchased His Church with His own blood"*** (Acts 20:28), where the blood — belonging to the human nature — is predicated of Him who is God. And conversely: ***"He who came down from heaven, the Son of man who is in heaven"*** (John 3:13), where the property of the divine nature (being in heaven) is predicated of Him who is called by the human title (Son of man).

**The Second genus, called ***genus maiestaticum***** — by which the ***divine properties*** (omnipotence, omniscience, omnipresence, vivification) ***are communicated to the human nature*** of Christ in the state of exaltation, so that the human nature really and truly participates in these — not indeed by essence (for the human nature does not become divine essence), but by ***communication and gift*** — as Paul says (Phil. 2:9): ***"God hath bestowed on Him a Name that is above every name."*** And Matt. 28:18: ***"All power is given to me in heaven and on earth."*** And John 5:26–27: ***"As the Father hath life in Himself, so hath He given also to the Son to have life in Himself; and hath given Him authority to execute judgement, because He is Son of man."*** Here Christ affirms that even ***according to His human nature*** — as Son of man — He has received from the Father the ***divine power*** of vivifying and judging. This paradigm ***genus maiestaticum*** is the paradigm-central inner-Reformation Christological dispute against the paradigm-Reformed ***extra-Calvinisticum*** (which held that the divine nature of the Logos remains ***outside*** the flesh even after the union), and was decisively confessed at ***Formula of Concord Article VIII*** *De persona Christi (Kolb-Wengert 508–531)*.

**The Third genus, called ***genus apotelesmaticum***** — by which the ***works of Christ's office*** (redemption, mediation, intercession) are performed by the ***whole Person*** according to ***both natures conjointly*** — each nature contributing what is proper to itself. As when Christ redeems us: ***His humanity suffers and dies***, while ***His divinity gives infinite value to the suffering***, making the sacrifice ***sufficient for the sins of the whole world***. Hence Peter says (1 Pet. 3:18): ***"Christ died once for our sins, the just for the unjust, that He might offer us to God — mortified indeed in the flesh, but quickened in the Spirit."***¹⁰⁷ᶠ

#### On the Two States of Christ — Humiliation and Exaltation

**Christ, God and man, is considered in ***two states*** — the state of ***humiliation*** and the state of ***exaltation*** — as Paul most clearly describes in Phil. 2:5–11:**

***"Have this mind in you, which was also in Christ Jesus, who, being in the form of God, thought it not robbery to be equal with God, but ***emptied Himself***, taking the form of a servant, being made in the likeness of men — and in habit found as a man, He humbled Himself, becoming obedient unto death, even the death of the cross. Wherefore also God hath exalted Him, and hath bestowed on Him a Name which is above every name — that in the Name of Jesus every knee should bow, of things in heaven, in earth, and under the earth — and that every tongue should confess that the Lord Jesus Christ is in the glory of God the Father."***

**The state of ***humiliation*** — from the incarnation to the burial — comprised:**

- The ***assumption of human nature*** in the Virgin Mary's womb.
- The ***birth in a stable*** at Bethlehem, wrapped in swaddling clothes.
- The ***circumcision*** on the eighth day.
- The ***flight into Egypt.***
- The ***subjection*** to Joseph and Mary.
- The ***baptism*** in Jordan by John, though needing no baptism.
- The ***temptation*** in the wilderness by the Devil.
- The ***manifold sufferings*** of hunger, thirst, weariness, sorrow, tears.
- The ***persecutions*** of the Pharisees and Jews.
- The ***betrayal*** by Judas.
- The ***passion*** — the agony in Gethsemane, the sweating of blood, the arrest, the trials before Annas, Caiaphas, Pilate, and Herod, the scourging, the mocking, the crowning with thorns, the carrying of the Cross, the crucifixion, the seven words on the Cross, the death, the piercing of the side.
- The ***burial*** in Joseph of Arimathaea's tomb.
- The ***descent to hell*** (as treated in the Praemonitio §8 above).

**In all these things Christ was ***truly humbled*** — not laying aside the divine nature or its properties (for that is impossible — divinity cannot cease to be divinity), but ***voluntarily concealing and suspending the full exercise of the divine majesty according to the human nature***, so that the human nature underwent all these things which it could not have borne if the divine majesty had shone forth in full in the human nature.

**The state of ***exaltation*** — from the resurrection onward — comprises:**

- The ***resurrection*** on the third day from the dead.
- The ***forty days'*** post-resurrection appearances to the disciples.
- The ***ascension*** to heaven.
- The ***sitting at the right hand*** of God the Father — that is, at the ***omnipotent right hand***, which is not a corporeal locus but the ***omnipotence of God*** by which Christ, according to the human nature also, now ***fills all things, rules all things, is present with His Church and with each believer in every place and time***, hears all our prayers, judges all things.
- The ***sending of the Holy Spirit*** at Pentecost.
- The ***session*** and ***reign*** of Christ over all creatures.
- The ***return in glory*** at the last day to judge the living and the dead.

**In the state of exaltation, therefore, Christ's human nature ***participates in the full exercise of the divine majesty*** which had been suspended in the state of humiliation** — so that Christ, ***according to His human nature also***, is now ***omnipotent, omniscient, present with His Church in the Supper*** (against the Sacramentarians treated in Praemonitio §7), and is truly ***King, Priest, and Judge*** in eternity.¹⁰⁷ᵍ

#### On the Saving Benefits of Christ

**From the union of the two natures in Christ, and from the two states, flow all the ***saving benefits*** of Christ — which the Gospel proclaims to those believing in Him:**

- ***Redemption*** — Christ has bought us with His blood from the tyranny of sin, death, Devil, and the eternal wrath of God (Eph. 1:7; Col. 1:14; 1 Pet. 1:18–19).
- ***Satisfaction*** — Christ has paid the debt which we owed to God's justice, satisfying for the sins of the whole world (Isa. 53:5–6; 2 Cor. 5:21; 1 John 2:2).
- ***Reconciliation*** — Christ has restored us to the favour of the Father, that we may have peace with God (Rom. 5:1, 10; 2 Cor. 5:18–19).
- ***Justification*** — Christ, through faith apprehending His righteousness, has made us righteous before God — not by our own works, but by the imputation of His obedience (active and passive), gratis, on account of Christ alone (Rom. 3:22–28; 4:5; Gal. 2:16). *(Of this the whole doctrine will be treated in Pars II — where Selnecker himself concentrates the paradigm justification doctrine, following the paradigm* Formula of Concord *Article III framework, of which he was co-author.)*
- ***Adoption*** — Christ has made us sons of God and co-heirs with Himself (Rom. 8:14–17; Gal. 4:4–6; John 1:12).
- ***Sanctification*** — Christ, by sending His Spirit, renews us into His image, and works in us new obedience of faith (Rom. 8:9–13; Titus 3:5–6).
- ***Preservation*** — Christ preserves and defends us in this life against all the assaults of Devil, world, and flesh, so that no one can snatch us from His hand (John 10:28–29).
- ***Glorification*** — Christ shall raise our bodies from the dead at the last day, and shall lead us into eternal life with Himself in the presence of the Father (John 6:39–40; 1 Cor. 15; Phil. 3:20–21).

**All these things Christ effects for us — He alone, our sole, sufficient, and perfect Redeemer and Saviour — to whom with the Father and the Holy Spirit be praise, honour, glory, and dominion in all eternity. Amen.**¹⁰⁷ʰ

#### Extended Anti-Heretical Treatment — Manichaeans, Valentinians, Apollinarists

**That the Son of God assumed our nature, manifest testimonies attest**: ***"The Word was made flesh"*** (John 1); and Mary to the Angel says: ***"Be it done unto me according to Thy word"*** (Luke 1). And Paul expressly says: ***"The man Jesus Christ, of the seed of David, born into the likeness of men, and found in affection as a man"*** (1 Tim. 2; Rom. 1; Phil. 2, and often elsewhere). Item Heb. 2: ***"He was made in all things similar to His brethren."*** Heb. 4: ***"Without sin."*** ***"This is to be held diligently against the Manichaeans, the Valentinians, the Apollinarists, the followers of Origen, and the Arians.***

**The Manichaeans** — that we may indicate this by the way, for the sake of students. ***Manes*** in the year of Christ 280 was of the Persian race, a servant of a certain Persian widow — who when she died, being wealthy, established him as heir of all her faculties. Among these Manes found also books of the fanatical men ***Scythianus*** and ***Terebinthus***, from which he drew and scattered his fanatical opinions. Concerning him, Epiphanius is to be consulted, who says he was named ***Cubricus***, and afterwards imposed on himself the name ***Manes***, [***οὐκ ἄνευ θείας προνοίας διοικήσεως***] — that is, ***"not without divine providential dispensation"*** (for in this signification also the ancients seemed to use the word [***οἰκονομία***], which is to be observed in Gregory and others). For ***Manes*** signifies ***a frenzied man***, or in the Egyptian tongue, ***a vessel*** [***σκεῦος***] ***abject and vile.*** Manes stirred up a pestiferous heresy, which spread widely in Arabia, Egypt, and Africa, and lasted almost two hundred years. And Augustine himself for nine years was ensnared by the sect of the Manichaeans (for they were so called from Manes) — until he acknowledged their errors, and by the prayers of his mother, and the instruction of Ambrose, refuted them.

**The chief delusions of Manes were these:**

**1.** The Manichaeans feigned ***two principles equally eternal and mutually fighting — good and evil*** [***δύναμιν καὶ δύναμιν***], or as the Stoics said, [***ποιοῦν καὶ πάσχον***]. From the good one they said were born all who are to be saved — whom they affirmed cannot be damned, since they are predestined to life, and have the seeds of light, and by them necessarily do all things which they do, and are not held by the Law even when they do things contrary to the Law — because the Law judges only those born from the ***evil God***, or from ***matter*** — whom they named [***χοϊκοί***] and [***ψυχικοί***], teaching that they necessarily do evil and are damned, and cannot be converted to God. And such today are those who teach that all things happen necessarily by absolute and immutable determination, and that only those are saved who are so appointed to salvation, and the rest are damned. Item, those who teach that ***obedience to the Law is not necessary for the just***, and that the Law is not to be preached in the Church, and that the man justified by faith is now above obedience to the Law.

**2.** They ***utterly abolished free will***, and taught that there is no liberty of the human will — either in good or in evil actions, internal and external — and that Cain was necessarily damned, Abel saved without any piety of his will, and Sodom perished, and Nineveh was preserved, without any fault or conversion of men.

**3.** They ***rejected marriages, the political order, judgements, and magistrates***, and feigned them to have their origin from the evil God.

**4.** They ***simulated Enthusiasms and afflatuses***, and rashly prophesied.

**5.** They ***rejected the Prophetic and Apostolic writings***, and wrote a new Gospel. Manes said he was the ***Paraclete***, and chose twelve disciples whom he sent by turns to seduce men. And such in our age have been the ***Anabaptists.***

**6.** They ***simply named the vocal Word*** the ***letter***, and said that all things ought to consist of the Spirit — that is, of enthusiasm and imagination. Such are also the Anabaptists and the Schwenkfeldians.

**7.** They were ***accustomed to perpetrate and to stir up foul crimes, confusions of lusts, and seditions***. And it is written that the ***filth of Marcion*** named a town of Phrygia, ***Pepuza***, the ***Jerusalem***, and stirred up sedition there — just as the Anabaptists stirred up sedition in the city of Münster. Hence the sectaries were called ***Pepuzians*** — heretics — of ***Montanus*** (who lived around the year of Christ 170, and feigned enthusiasms, contemner of all Churches, and at length ended his life with a noose), and of ***Prisca*** and ***Maximilla***, who also inhabited the same [place]. Marcion was born of a father who was Bishop in the Pontic city of Sinope; and when he had defiled a virgin, and was excommunicated by his father, he fled to Rome, and embraced the sect of ***Cerdon*** and ***Basilides***, and scattered his poison in the year of Christ 150. From this Marcion, delusions being taken, Manes renewed [them] — [Marcion] having found certain fanatics (of whom mention has just been made) had written some [of them] in books. But now of the Manichaeans few things remain to be said.

**8.** They taught that ***these words — flesh, spirit, external man, old man, internal and new man, earthly and heavenly man — are not to be received for qualities and motions, or for a force and property, but for substances*** — precisely as today those fanatical men delusion who say that ***sin is the substance of man***, and that the ***image of God has been changed into the image of the Devil in man*** in reality — without any exception through all things — mutated and transformed — and that the whole man is nothing else than the ***image and substance of the Devil.***

**9.** They said that ***Christ our Lord did not have a true body, but only imaginary and phantastic*** — feigned and putative — although Christ Himself (and indeed after His resurrection) says to the disciples: ***"A spirit does not have flesh and bones, as you see me have"*** — [***οὐκ ἄνευ σαρκὸς καὶ ὀστέων ἡ σωτηρία***, ***οὐ πάρεργον ἡμῖν, ἀλλὰ κύριον***]. And ***Damascene***, in the third [book] against those who denied that the body of the Saviour suffered, says: [***"The Saviour truly took flesh and blood from the holy Virgin Mary; and truly He suffered in the flesh, was crucified, died, and was buried"***]. Considering these three delusions, let us shudder — and acknowledge both that ***there are devils, and horribly they lie in wait for the Church*** — and let us not assent to unexplored dogmas.

**Manes, however, is reported to have perished in this way**: He sold himself as a physician in the court of the Persian king, and in the treatment (or by deceit, or ignorance) killed the king's son. He therefore fled into Mesopotamia, and thence, being driven out by pious Bishops, was captured by order of the Persian king, and his skin drawn off, was slain. [***"Truly the harvest of vices is death"***]. These things concerning Manes.

**Valentinus**, in the year of Christ 141, a follower of Cerdon, lived at Rome — and from the delusions of Basilides, Carpocrates, Saturninus, and Menander conflated one mass. He feigned various ***generations of the Gods***, which he called [***αἰῶνας***]. He said that ***Christ took nothing from the flesh and blood of Mary***, but ***brought with Him from heaven a heavenly body***, which passed through Mary as through a channel and a pipe — for it is written: ***"No one has ascended into heaven except Him who descended from heaven, the Son of man, who is in heaven."*** This saying, however, this ill-doer twisted to deny the true humanity of Christ — although this is not said as if Christ had been man before assuming human nature in the womb of Mary ever-Virgin — but rather is shown the ***immense emptying [***exinanitio***] or humility of the Son of God***, putting on human nature, and humbled below all creatures, and yet retaining the divine majesty and power in heaven and on earth.

**Apollinaris the Rhetor, of Laodicea**, in the year of Christ 365, taught that ***Christ lacked a rational soul of man***, and had the divinity in place of the rational soul and mind. Budaeus gathers that he attributed to Christ ultimately only one nature — the human [only]. But rightly Athanasius, Basil, and the whole ancient Church condemned this delusion — since Christ ***assumed whole human nature, with body and rational soul*** — otherwise He could not truly have suffered in mind (in the agony), could not have said ***"My soul is sorrowful even unto death"***, could not have commended His spirit to the Father, could not have been ***tempted in all things after our likeness*** (Heb. 4:15). ***The whole man was to be redeemed***: therefore whole human nature had to be assumed. What the Son of God did not take on, He did not heal — the *classical* patristic axiom of Nazianzen (paradigm ***Ep. 101 Ad Cledonium***): ***"That which is unassumed is unhealed."***¹⁰⁷ⁱ

#### Extended Patristic Catena on the Session at the Right Hand — De Sessione ad Dexteram Patris

**Ambrose** *De Fide* book 5, chapter 5: ***"To Christ also according to the flesh, the venerable seat at the right hand of God the Father is deferred by the power of equality with the Father."***

**Primasius** on Heb. 1: ***"He is now one with God in glory — the man assumed by the Word, and on the fortieth day exalted to the heavenly things."***

**Athanasius** *De Incarnatione Verbi* on **Phil. 2**: ***"God exalted Him supremely, and gave Him the Name which is above every name."*** And of the temple He says that this is His body. ***"Not is the Most High exalted, but the flesh of the Most High is exalted. And to the flesh of the Most High He gave the Name which is above every name. And by grace He did not receive of God that He should be called God — but His flesh has been ***deified***."***

**Gregory of Nyssa** *Contra Eunomium*: ***"At the right hand of God Christ was exalted"*** (Acts 2). ***"Who indeed was exalted? The humble one, or the Most High? What is humble, except what is human? What further, other than what is divine, is Most High? But that God be exalted, there is no need — since He is Most High. Therefore the Apostle says that ***what is human*** is exalted. And exalted through this, that He is Lord and Christ. Wherefore not before the pre-worldly [state of] the Lord did the Apostle affirm [that] God made this same Jesus whom you crucified [Lord and Christ] — but of that which was humble, and had received a better [***μεταβολήν***] of sublimity — namely, that which was made at the right hand of God — He designates. For through this very word the mystery of piety is declared. And when [Paul] said, 'Exalted at the right hand of God' — evidently he manifests the ineffable dispensation of the mystery: that the right hand of God, the Fabricator of things which exist — through whom all things were made, and without whom nothing which is subsists — [this right hand] has led the man united to Himself into His own sublimity through union."***

The same [Gregory] calls Christ [***ἄνθρωπον θεοδόχον***], ***the man capable of God***.

**Origen** *Homil.* 5 on Isaiah: ***"The flesh of the Lord took on the honours of the divinity."*** And in Matthew *Tract.* 35: ***"Christ deified what He had assumed — the human nature."***

**Athanasius** book 8 *Ad Theophilum*: ***"The man grew and advanced into God — that is, into the Son of God. For He inclined Himself to take on servile form, that the vile and inferior form might grow and advance into a better substance."*** And there also: ***"What the Son received, He received according to man — that is, according to the servile form, and the nature of our condition — the Lord Himself saying: 'The Father gave Him power to judge, because He is Son of man.'"*** And book 3 *Ad Theophilum* he says: ***"To the man Christ before His passion all things were not subject — since He was still debtor of death for us. But after the passion, all things were subjected to Him."***

**Hilary** book 1 *De Trinitate*: ***"The Word God was made flesh, that through God-made-flesh the flesh might advance into God."*** The same: ***"Christ Jesus, having assumed the body of our sin (without sin), the whole [Christ] now lives to God, our nature united by fellowship in the communion of divine immortality."***

**We cease, however, to heap up more testimonies of the Fathers — all which have been taken from these places of Sacred Scripture:** **Ps. 8:** ***"Thou shalt crown him with glory and honour."*** **Ps. 110:** ***"Sit thou at my right hand."*** **Matt. 28:** ***"All power is given to me in heaven and on earth."*** **Eph. 1:** ***"God established Him at His right hand in the heavenly places, above every principality, and power, and virtue, and dominion, and every name that is named — not only in this world, but also in that to come — and put all things under His feet, and constituted Him head of the Church over all."*** **Eph. 4:** ***"He ascended above all the heavens, that He might fill all things."*** **Heb. 4:** ***"Our Pontiff has penetrated the heavens."*** **Phil. 2:** ***"God exalted Him, and gave Him the Name above every name."*** **Col. 2:** ***"In Christ dwelleth all the fullness of divinity personally."*** **Matt. 11:** ***"All things are given to me by the Father."*** **John 3:** ***"The Father loves the Son, and has given all things into His hand."***

And here belong the words of the ***Symbol***: ***"He ascended to heaven, sits at the right hand of God the Father Almighty."*** For it does not comprehend [Christ's] sitting at the right hand of the Father according to the divine nature — but the words of the Symbol speak of the ***exalted and glorified human nature in Christ.***

**Let there be held, therefore, these forms of speaking:**

- ***Christ, God and man, sits at the right hand of the Father.***
- ***Christ, God and man, has power in heaven and on earth, and is Lord of all creatures.***
- ***Christ, God and man, has all judgement, and shall come to judge the living and the dead.***

**Thou askest here: according to which nature?** Certainly ***according to both.*** But perhaps thou inferrest: ***"Christ as God did not receive that divine power at any time; and as man could not have it of Himself."*** I answer: ***both are true.*** For Christ as God from eternity into eternity always had, together with the Father and the Holy Spirit, the divine power, majesty, and glory — nor did He ever lose or lay it aside. But as man He ***received all power and glory, being raised up, exalted, and placed at the right hand of the Father.*** Therefore antiquity spoke thus: ***"Whatever the human nature in Christ has of majesty and divine glory, it has not of its own nature, but of the grace of Him who assumed it."*** For thus Leo writes to Flavian: ***"As the Deity in Christ had contumely from the sufferings of the flesh which He had put on Himself, so His flesh had glory from the indwelling Deity."*** Rightly, therefore, we say: ***"Christ sits at the right hand of the Father according to both natures — divine (which from eternity is [ὁμοούσιος] with the Father) and human (which has been exalted)."***

**He is and sits — that is, ***reigns*** — in the consort and throne of the Father and the Holy Spirit, according to both natures, but in a diverse manner.** According to the divine nature He Himself is the ***right hand of God***, and never began to sit. But according to the human nature this majesty was given to Him at a certain time, and He Himself began to sit at the right hand of God according to the human nature.¹⁰⁷ʲ

#### Five Questions on the Predication *"Christ as Man"*

**Here again it is customarily asked**:

**1.** Whether the reason of both predications is the same: ***"Christ as man is Creator of heaven and earth"*** — and: ***"Christ as man sits at the right hand of the Father."***

**2.** What it is to sit at the right hand of God.

**3.** Whether the propriety of the human nature remains — that it is finite and [***πεπερασμένη***] (bounded).

**4.** Whether the properties of the human nature do not admit some exception — especially in those prerogatives which the sacred letters attribute to the human nature in Christ on account of the personal union with the divinity.

**5.** To what predications this explanation is fitting: ***"according to both natures."***

**Of these things we shall briefly again add an admonition, God illuminating, ruling, and aiding our mind.**

***Christ as man is Creator of heaven and earth.***

***Christ as man sits at the right hand of the Father.***

**Concerning the First Question:** ***Christ as man created heaven and earth*** — namely, according to the ***divine nature.*** For He was not yet man when He Himself created all things, and even man himself. **But He sits at the right hand of the Father** — that is, He reigns omnipotently — ***according to both natures.*** For the words of the Symbol are to be received of the ***exaltation and glorification of the human nature in Christ.*** Christ never began to sit at the right hand of the Father ***according to the divine nature***, because from eternity He is [***αὐτοβασιλεύς***, ***αὐτοπάντοκράτωρ, αὐτοδίκαιος***]. But He began — as He was made man, so also to be glorified, and to sit at the right hand of the Father — ***according to the human nature.*** And He also sits eternally as God and man at the right hand of the Father. [***"According to the human nature, He now shines in the honour proper to God, and is worshipped by all creation with equal honour with the Father"***], says Theodoret.

And **Athanasius** *De Incarnatione* says: ***"The Holy Spirit was not yet, because Jesus was not yet glorified"*** — His flesh, He says, was not yet glorified. For not the Lord of glory is glorified — but the flesh of the Lord received this glory, ascending with Him into heaven. Whence the ***Spirit of adoption was not yet in men*** — because the ***first-fruits taken from us into heaven had not yet ascended.*** Whatever the Scripture says — that the Son received, or that the Son is glorified — it says on account of His humanity, not on account of the divinity.

**And the words of Cyril of Alexandria to Succensus, Bishop of Diocaesarea**, we have alleged above — which stand entire thus: ***"After Christ's resurrection His body indeed was the same which had suffered — but nevertheless not now having in itself the human infirmities. For we do not assert it as suffering hunger, labour, or any similar thing — but now we confess it incorruptible, and not only this, but life-giving. For it is the body of Life — that is, of the Only-begotten Son of God. It has been glorified with clarity most worthy of God, and is understood as ***God's body***. Therefore if any one call it ***divine*** — just as [also they call] the body of man ***human*** — he does not deviate from the most fitting reason. Whence I think that also the most blessed Paul says: 'And if we have known Christ according to the flesh, now we know Him thus no more.' For being the ***own body of God***, it transcends all human things."***

Item **Cyprian** in the exposition of the Apostolic Symbol says: ***"He ascended to the heavens — not where God the Word was not before (since He was always in the heavens, and remained in the Father), but where the Word made flesh had not been before."*** And **Justin** in the ***Exposition of the Faith concerning the Trinity*** splendidly and piously says: ***"The body indeed remains body — because God is not body — [but is] made partaker of the divine Majesty, not by nature — because it so seemed good to the Word itself."***

**This glory and exaltation of the human nature therefore let us venerate, admire, and preach — which is now adorned with that divine title**, not only that ***Christ as man is Creator and Conserver, Rector and Lord of all creatures*** — but that He ***sits at the right hand of the Omnipotent Father*** — our brother, our flesh, and our blood. This glory is full of consolation, and vanquishes all temptations and adversities, and consists not in prolix and wordy disputations, but ***in practice and use*** — especially in spiritual struggle, and in the agony of life and death, as all the pious experience.

#### Response to the Second Question — What "Sitting at the Right Hand of God" Means

**Concerning the second question**: **Augustine** book 2 ***De Symbolo ad Catechumenos***: ***"Do not receive the ***session*** as placed with human members — as if the Father sat on the left, that the Son might sit at the right. But understand the ***right hand*** itself to be the ***power*** which the man received (assumed by God), that He should come to judge, who first had come to be judged."*** And in Sermon 181 ***De Tempore***: ***"Sitting is of the one judging."*** And because our Redeemer has been received into heaven, and now judges all things, and at last shall come as Judge of all — He is described as ***sitting.***

**Vigilius** book 5: ***"By the session of reign nothing else is demonstrated than the ***authority of the Judge***."***

**Basil** *De Spiritu Sancto* chapter 6: [***"To reign with equal power with God, is to sit at the right hand of God."***] For thus Paul himself interprets this phrase (Eph. 1): ***"To sit at the right hand of God is to have power over all creatures."*** And **Cyril** book 12 ***Thesauri*** chapter 14 says: ***"Nothing else is understood, when concerning God ***sitting***, ***seat***, or ***throne*** is said, than the ***principate and royal power*** which by nature He has over all creatures."*** And when Christ says: ***"I ascend to my Father"***, He interprets this ascent by these words: ***"All power is given to me in heaven and on earth."*** All these things (says Basil book 4 *Contra Eunomium*) ***are to be understood of the humanity, not of the divinity.*** For to the divinity of Christ nothing of increase or glory acceded, because He had that glory and brightness with the Father before the world was made (John 17) — but the humanity received glory, being raised to the supreme sublimity, and receiving the Name which is above every name, that in the name of Jesus every knee should bow.¹⁰⁷ᵏ

#### Response to the Third Question — The Property of the Human Nature in Christ

**Concerning the third question**, [***"The natures being united through hypostatic union, [the properties] are not commingled, but each remains as it is"***], says Theodoret. [***"Uncommingled the natures. Neither is the body raised up to divinity — because the head of Christ is God (1 Cor. 11) — nor is the humanity converted into divinity."***] But this is said: ***that the human nature in Christ is raised and glorified, and Christ as man sits at the right hand of the Father, and has power in heaven and on earth over all creatures*** — which He received from God (who is His head), and who so raised it up to supreme sublimity and glory.

For now after that glorification the ***Person is no longer to be torn asunder*** — as if one of the natures were omnipotent, but the other of no or scanty or singular-and-middling power — because it is truly human. Each nature indeed retains its own essence and property — but nevertheless, on account of the ***hypostatic union of the natures***, many glorious [things] accede to the human nature in Christ, which it does not have of itself, nor can be attributed to any creatures. And rightly by some it is said: ***"The finite [***πεπερασμένον***] body of Christ, after He ascended into heaven, and in the state of glorification at the right hand of God, [participates in] infinite power, which [comes only from what is]*** [***ἄπειρον***] ***and immense."***

And on this cause Luther and certain others sometimes use this locution: ***"Christ's body is everywhere."*** Not that they confused the natures, or denied the properties of the human nature by taking away every [***τόπον***] of the body — but that they might demonstrate the ***power by which He exceeds the power of all Angels and men.*** For with these words Luther himself extricates himself: ***"When I say Christ's body is everywhere, I do this for this reason: that I may show the omnipotence of God — since Christ is at the right hand of God, and prove that God has a manner by which His body can be Christ's [body] both in heaven and on earth."*** The hyperboles of others we now omit, whose explication ought to be analogous to faith — and at the same time ambiguities in speaking must be avoided.

#### Response to the Fourth Question — The Prerogatives of the Human Nature in Christ

**Concerning the fourth question**: the human nature in Christ has ***many prerogatives***, which by human nature considered in the abstract per se are not congruent. Wherefore when concerning the properties of the human nature it is said that ***they are always to be retained*** — since Christ was ***made like unto His brethren in all things, sin excepted*** — nevertheless the other prerogatives and conditions are ***not on this account denied, taken away, or extenuated*** — which the human nature in Christ received beyond its own properties from Him who by personal union coupled it to Himself.

**The human nature in Christ does not have ***of itself*** or by its own strength and properties**:

- that it ***came forth from a Virgin without masculine seed***;
- that ***Christ's body vanished and was rendered invisible***;
- that it ***walked upon the waters***;
- that it ***rose from the tomb without the stone being rolled away***;
- that it ***penetrated closed doors***;
- that it ***ascended to the heavens***;
- that it ***sits at the right hand of the Father***;
- that it ***is exhibited and taken in the Lord's Supper***;
- that it is ***life-giving and to be adored***;
- etc.

These and many other [prerogatives] the human nature does not have ***of itself or by its own strength and properties***, but has them by the ***virtue [***δύναμιν***] of God*** — on account of the ***personal union.***

**Let us therefore hold this admonition:** ***When Scripture attributes something to the body of Christ which surpasses the [idiomata] of human nature, and does not agree with the properties of our bodies — with simple faith obedient to the Word of God we ought to believe it, always thinking that this alone mass is united to divinity personally, and this alone is placed at the right hand of the virtue and majesty of God.***

**But if the matter be one of ***reconciliation of diverse and perhaps (as sometimes seems to us) contrary propositions***** — such as: ***"It is the property of a body to be in a certain place"*** vs ***"Christ whole and totally is with His Church"***; ***"Christ's body is in the Supper"*** — think of the hypostatic union of natures, of the exaltation of the human nature in Christ, and (as Hugo says) ***think of the One speaking, think of the One doing***, who sits at the right hand of the Father — ponder His power, ponder His truth and faith — and it shall not be impossible, nor to thee incredible, whatever shall be which is said to thee in the sacred letters.

**But led hither and thither by thy reasonings and syllogisms**, conclude at last: ***"I cannot reconcile all things, nor extricate myself from all — which are too high, arduous, and unaccommodated to my capacity. Reconcile Thou them, O Son of God — who canst do all things, and dost all things which Thou hast said — even if I know neither the manner nor the reason, nor can any creature in this life see through it. I believe Thy words; and those things which to my reason seem mutually contrary, I know are not contrary with Thee, but are easy, obvious, accommodated, and consentaneous to Thy power and truth, without any absurdity. And I know I do not err if I have faith to Thy Word — nay, if it be permitted so to speak to me — I would prefer to err with Thy Word, which nevertheless never errs, than with the wisdom and doctrine of the whole world even most wise to seem [wise] against Thy Word. Simplicity, righteousness Thine, may govern me, O Christ. The rest — what remains to be said — I shall defer into eternal life, when I shall see Thee — Creator, Father, Brother, and my Master — showing me all things which now by my reason I cannot comprehend. Sanctify me meanwhile in Thy truth; Thy discourse is truth. Rule me in Thy Holy Spirit, that I may believe all things which Thou hast said — even if the whole world deny, [and] all physical properties contradict."***

**At the end of this question I propose these two things to be considered by the pious:**

**1.** Christ by His divine power, being God and man, is ***everywhere***, and can, when He wills, be ***bodily present*** wherever He wills — the ***human nature yet remaining safe and inviolate.***

**2.** Truly there is the ***body of Christ*** wherever Christ Himself wills it to be — as He now wills it to be at the right hand of the Father according to the human nature, and truly to be in the Lord's Supper — where it is not spatially bounded, nor sensibly held, nor circumscribed by any physical dimension, but ***truly, substantially, and orally taken*** — as the Words of Institution attest, and as our Confession has always been against the Sacramentarians.¹⁰⁷ˡ

#### Response to the Fifth Question — On the Five Modes of Speaking of the Son of God in Scripture

**Concerning the fifth question**: The ***names of office*** (as ***Mediator, Redeemer, Justifier, Saviour, King, Priest, Pastor***) belong to the ***Person*** — not only to one nature — and are true according to both natures. These are ***communicative idiomata***, as Cyril calls [them], with communication plainly real. Thus therefore we say: ***"Christ is Redeemer, Life-giver, Propitiator, Pontiff, King," etc. — according to both natures.***

And hence the old rule is handed down: ***"One nature acts with communication of the other."*** And: ***"The Person acts according to both natures. The Person has actions in both natures at once."*** Hither we refer also those things which pertain to the exaltation of the human nature in Christ, as that ***Christ sits at the right hand of the Father.*** For these express both dignity or glory, and also the ***office of reigning, and of interceding for us, and of defending and preserving us.*** And whatever pertains to the office either of dominating, or of preserving the promises, or of executing a certain institution and ordination (as in the administration of the Supper: ***"This is my body, which is given for you"***) — we judge that these are to be referred here, the Word of God convincing us — as: ***Christ is everywhere with His Church***; item, ***He is in the Supper.*** If thou consider the property of the natures, He is in the Supper according to the divine nature. But if thou consider the manifest promise and institution, and attend to the omnipotence and truth of Christ, thou shalt say: ***"Christ whole and totally is in the Supper according to both natures,"*** according to the manifest Word: ***"Take, eat, this is my body, which is given for you."***

**We do not, however, take or eat the divinity — but the body which is given for us.** The body given for us is a ***true body.*** Therefore we take and eat a ***true body.*** But how these things concerning the Supper are to be reconciled with the properties of the human body — the Son of God Himself knows, in whose words we meanwhile believe, until in eternal life we see the reconciliation. We know that the properties of the natures are not taken away — but ultramarine we say, from the sacred letters, that ***many things are attributed to the human nature which it does not have of itself, by its own strength and properties.*** And it is permitted to add to the rule (which concerns the properties of the natures, especially the human nature) either exception or limitation from the Word of God. This rule is known to us, to Plato, to Aristotle, and to all men — but that addition only the divine voice shows to us. We ought to make no exception; but that which the Word of God makes, we ought to embrace.

**Thus much has been said on the ***Communication of Idiomata***, briefly and without sophistry.**

#### Selnecker's Numbered Positions on the Person of Christ — L Positions

**We shall subjoin to these, in the place of a corona, positions once prescribed by us to the Candidates of Sacred Theology, and recently again read through — of which the beginning is drawn from a monition on the various modes of speaking in Scripture concerning the Son of God, in this manner:**

**Five modes of speaking chiefly are found in the Scriptures concerning the Second Person of the Divinity:**

- **First**, the ***simple*** or ***of the natures*** — by which divine [attributes] are attributed to the divine nature, which agree only with it — as: ***"The Son of God is He through whom the ages were founded."*** And to the human nature are attributed things which touch it alone — as: ***"The Son of Mary grew in wisdom, and in grace before God and men,"*** etc.

- **Second**, the ***mode of the Person in the concrete through communication of idiomata*** — in which the ***properties of one nature are communicated to the other nature*** on account of the hypostatic union — as: ***"The Son of Mary is omnipotent, Creator of all, [εἰκὼν τοῦ Θεοῦ], everywhere; God was born, saddened, suffered, crucified, died; God acquired for Himself the Church by His own blood,"*** etc.

- **Third**, the ***synecdochical mode*** — when to the humanity is attributed what is of the whole Person, as: ***"The seed of the woman shall crush the head of the serpent."*** And to the divinity is attributed what is simply of the whole Person, as Jer. 33: ***"This is the name by which they shall call Him: LORD OUR JUSTIFIER."***

- **Fourth**, the ***mode of predicates which agree with each nature even in the abstract***, as: ***"Christ lives and reigns in heaven."***

- **Fifth**, the ***mode of offices*** — which are so attributed to the Person, that neither the divine nor the human nature per se and separately, but the whole Person which is God and man agree [with them] — as are the ***works of redemption, remitting sins, redeeming from death, justifying, being Mediator, saving believers,*** etc.

**Concerning the first two modes** — about which today not without great danger and wounding of the Church many questions too scrupulous are moved — we shall subjoin a few Positions, which we judge shall not be useless to those loving truth and piety.

**I.** ***Christ is true God and true man in inseparable personal union of two natures: divine, which is from eternity; and human, which He Himself at a certain time took upon Himself from the Virgin Mary.***

**II.** ***This personal union is made without confusion of the natures, and remains now unto eternity without disruption of the whole Person — who is God and man, according to the saying: "The Word was made flesh."***

**III.** ***One is therefore the assuming nature, and the other the assumed — and yet is and remains one Person, one God and man, or one Christ — not by conversion of the divinity into flesh, but by assumption of the humanity into God: and one, not by confusion of natures, but by unity of Person.***

**IV.** ***Each nature retains its substantial properties safe, because the natures are not to be confounded.***

**V.** ***The divine nature in Christ is eternal, omnipotent, created all things, conserves all things, is everywhere present, searches hearts,*** [***ἀκατάληπτος, ἀκίνητος, ἄπειρος, ἀμέτρητος***] (incomprehensible, unmoved, boundless, immeasurable). The human nature is not eternal, but assumed at a certain time — and, if per se in the abstract by any reasoning it can be considered, being nature assumed from the Virgin Mary — cannot be called omnipotent, nor do the other properties which are of the divinity alone in the abstract properly agree with it.

**VI.** ***To the human nature — such as ours also is — according to the history and truth of the divine Word, agree certain things per se, before glorification*** (which nevertheless would have been present had man not fallen, as: to be born at a certain time, and to hunger, thirst, be clothed, eat, drink, sleep, be moved, and similar); ***certain things by reason of our corrupted nature*** or of sin (which happened to man after the fall, and for which Christ [***οὐ γνοὺς ἁμαρτίαν***] willed to satisfy — as: to be affected with sadness, to weep, to be bound, to suffer, be crucified, die); ***certain things finally agree with it after glorification*** — as: to live in eternity, not to need aliments (such as hunger and thirst now require), no longer to be obnoxious to sufferings or death, always to be visibly with God, and to have a ***spiritual and heavenly body*** as Paul names it — that is, [a body] which lives by the nod and rule of the spirit.

**VII.** ***This distinction of the two natures in one Person is manifestly shown in the New Testament***, as when the Son of God Himself says: ***"I and the Father are one"*** (here indeed He speaks of His eternal divinity); item: ***"The ages were founded through Him."*** But when He sleeps, hungers, thirsts, and grows in wisdom and glory before God and men, and is said to be ignorant of certain things (Mark 13); item, when He is saddened, suffers, is crucified, and dies — certainly the properties and accidents of the human nature are designated.

**VIII.** ***From these it easily is established that it cannot be said in the abstract that the divine nature is human, or that the divinity sleeps, suffers, dies, is transfixed by nails; or that the human [nature] is eternal, or in the abstract*** [***καθ' ἑαυτήν***] ***omnipotent, and everywhere present — because the natures are and remain distinct.***

**IX.** ***It must also be known that it is very different to speak of human nature nakedly per se and in the abstract*** (as when we speak of ourselves, or rather of our first parents such as they were before the fall) ***and different to speak of the human nature in Christ*** — which although it is not another or diverse from the nature of the first parents before the fall (and also of all men, except that it is [***ἀναμάρτητος***] — without sin) — nevertheless since it has been assumed by the divine nature of Christ, to which it is united so that it constitutes one Person with it, ***far exceeds the dignity, majesty, glory, power, and excellence of all creatures.***

**X.** ***With greater reverence therefore and humility of soul and subjection we must think and speak of the human nature in Christ than when we speak of the nature of all men even integral and uncontaminated — because no creature — neither Angels nor men — has so great majesty, dignity, and power as the human nature in Christ assumed from the divine.***

**XI.** ***Not on this account, however, are the properties of both natures taken away — but since there are two natures in Christ, divine and human, each nature has its own essential properties*** — and the human nature is not absorbed by the divine, nor is it diffused thus into the whole universe, so that there are no longer truly two natures but only one — as ***Eutyches*** 1118 years ago contended that of two natures one thing has become.

**XII.** ***And it is necessarily and usefully asked whether the natures and their properties are considered only in the abstract in the way now briefly shown — and whether here as at a certain limit we ought to be content — or whether truly a communication of properties happens, and how it happens.***

**XIII.** ***Since it is written***: ***"The Word was made flesh"***, and ***"God acquired for Himself the Church by His own blood"*** (Acts 20), ***"God delivered up His Son for us"*** (Rom. 8), ***"They crucified the Lord of glory"*** (1 Cor. 2), ***"The Son of Man is the Son of the living God"*** (Matt. 16), ***"If you shall see the Son of Man ascending where He was before"*** (John 6), ***"Before Abraham was, I am"*** (John 8), ***"The second man from heaven"*** (1 Cor. 15) — manifestly ***the communication of properties*** is shown — which nevertheless is neither an ***equating of the natures***, nor is it only a certain ***relation of idiomata*** as Nestorius wished (such as [the relation] of Paul's sufferings, which Paul calls "sufferings of Christ," Col. 1), nor is it a ***confusion of the two natures*** — but is such a communication in which it is shown that the property agreeing with one nature is attributed to the Person in the concrete, and that ***the two natures in Christ are mutually united and coupled — not only so that one is idle and separable, but so that as the two natures come together into one Person, so also the names and predicates of both natures come together in the name of one Person***, and the properties agreeing with one nature are attributed to the Person in the concrete — and all this on account of the ***hypostatic union*** — because predicates are mutually communicated for the reason that there is ***one*** [***ὑπόστασις***].

**XIV.** ***It is necessary to establish this true communication in its own certain and to us ineffable mode***, lest Christ the man (since He is true God) be said to be only ***God by name*** (as the Arians wished), and lest Christ God (since He truly suffered for our sins) be said not truly but only by speech to have suffered (which the Nestorians attempted to obtain), and lest the divinity in Christ be feigned to have dwelt only ***by assistance or fellowship***, as in Paul.

**XV.** ***Not, however, on account of this communication are the properties of the natures taken away — because the communication happens ***in the concrete***, in which the whole Person, who is God and man, is understood.*** For it is one thing to say ***"The divine nature is passive"***, which cannot be said; another: ***"God suffered."*** Item: ***"The human nature created all things and is everywhere"*** — which also cannot be said in the abstract. Another: ***"Christ the man created all things, and is everywhere."***

**XVI.** ***In the abstract, the properties of each nature are considered per se. In the concrete, however, the communication of properties happens, and it is discussed of the whole Person.***

**XVII.** ***We do not disapprove the vulgar similitude which agrees*** [***ἀναλόγως***]: as when to some poor man a rich man says, ***"Thou art poor, both in mind, and in body, and in nature, and in things. But behold, I take thee into my house, and I communicate to thee my wealth, and grant to thee the possession of all my goods, and place thee in my state and grade."***

**XVIII.** ***On this cause, antiquity so spoke***: ***"Whatever agrees with the Son of God by nature, this agrees with the Son of man by grace"*** — that is, the humanity of Christ is not converted into divinity, and per se in the abstract does not have the properties which agree only with the divine nature — but whatever of glory, power, and majesty, and what prerogatives above all other men, the human nature in Christ has — all this it has from the ***power and grace of the divinity of Christ***, or as Nazianzen says, ***"of the Son, and of the Father through the Son"*** — that is, on account of the ***hypostatic union.***

**XIX.** ***This rule is established and confirmed by examples***: as that Christ, beyond the series or order of nature of things, ***was born a man of a Virgin without masculine seed***; item, that ***the flesh of Christ is life-giving***, and ***His blood cleanses us from all iniquity***; item, that ***the body of Christ vanishes, is rendered invisible, walks upon the waters, rises from the tomb without the stone or seal being moved, enters to the disciples through closed doors*** contrary to all the properties of bodies, ***ascends bodily to the heavens*** contrary to all physical mode and motion, ***truly is present, is exhibited, and is taken in the Supper according to the institution of Christ***, ***sits at the right hand of the Father***, item, ***shall be seen by all men at the last judgement in that form in which He ascended***, though then more men shall be present before Him than the whole earth can now contain — to say nothing of the Angels, etc.

**XX.** ***These and many, nay infinite other things — especially after the glorification of the human nature in Christ — by no means agree with human nature considered in the abstract***, and plainly are [***ἀκατάληπτα***] and [***ἀπερίγραπτα***] — therefore rightly [***αὐθυπερκαταλλαγές***] they are named. They agree, however, with it ***in the concrete*** according to the true communication of idiomata, in which the properties of one nature are communicated to the other nature on account of the ***hypostatic or personal union.***

**XXI.** ***On this cause it is customarily said***: ***"The body of Christ, finite, is [participant] of infinite power in the unity of the Person"*** — that is, not in the abstract nor by its own virtue, but ***in the concrete from the grace and power of Him who assumed the human nature and united it to Himself***, so that He is God and man in one Person.

**XXII.** ***Hither pertain those sentences***: ***"All power is given to me in heaven and on earth."*** Item: ***"He ascended to the heavens, sits at the right hand of the Father: He reigns divinely and with equal*** [***ὁμοδυναμίᾳ***], ***as Basil speaks, in power and majesty God and man with God the Father and the Holy Spirit."*** Heb. 4: ***"He has penetrated the heavens."*** Heb. 7: ***"He is made loftier than the heavens."*** Eph. 4: ***"He ascended above all the heavens, that He might fill all things."*** Matt. 11: ***"All is given to me from the Father."*** Ps. 8: ***"He subjected all things under His feet,"*** etc.

**XXIII.** ***All these agree with Christ not only as God, but also as man.*** For insofar as He is God, He had that majesty from eternity, nor did He ever lose it, nor did He need any ascension or new session at the right hand of the Father. But now He is placed in the same majesty also as man — as Cyprian says: ***"He ascended to the heavens — not where God the Word was not before, but where the Word made flesh was not sitting"*** — and now as God and man He reigns eternally, is everywhere, proves, sees, hears the hearts, groans, and prayers of His own — and to embrace the whole matter summarily in Paul's words: ***in Christ the man dwelleth all the fulness of the divinity*** [***σωματικῶς, ὑποστατικῶς, πραγματικῶς***] or ***personally*** (Col. 2).

**XXIV.** ***That we may better attain this whole matter (especially what pertains to the human nature in Christ), and speak piously and soberly, it must be known that the sacred letters speak of the human nature in Christ in a quadruple mode.***

**XXV.** ***First, in the abstract*** — namely, insofar as Christ has human nature common with us, but without any sin — perfect man subsisting of rational soul and human flesh.

**XXVI.** ***According to this mode He is our brother, and our flesh and blood — because He was made similar to us in all things, except sin — and assumed a human body with those properties which pertain to the condition and truth of human nature.***

**XXVII.** ***According to this reason it is written Phil. 3***: ***"Our holy bodies shall be [conformed] to Christ's glorious body"*** — that is, ***shall lack all sin, and therefore also all corruption, death, and end, and shall live in eternity, full of integrity and joy.*** Item 1 John 3: ***"When He shall appear, we shall be like Him."***

**XXVIII.** ***Second, in the concrete the sacred Scripture speaks of the human nature of Christ*** — not only insofar as it is common with us or in the abstract, but insofar as it is assumed by the divinity of Christ, and so united to the divine nature that it constitutes one Person with it — as Matt. 16: ***"The Son of Man is the Son of the living God."*** Rom. 5: ***"Justified in His blood."*** John 6: ***"My flesh is given for the life of the world."***

**XXIX.** ***Third, the sacred Scripture speaks of the sufferings of the human nature in Christ before glorification*** — some of which are common with us, as ***to be saddened, to weep, to commiserate*** (though these also were far more excellent affections in Christ than we can even think, or than can be in any other men), to eat, drink, sleep, suffer, die, etc.; some [are] singular, as the ***sweat of blood*** or [***θρόμβος***], the flow of coagulated blood and water from the side of the dead Christ, ***walking upon the waters, vanishing,*** etc.

**XXX.** ***In this humility of Christ — when He was visibly conversant on earth, and no prerogative before our bodies appeared, and even the divinity seemed as if to rest, and the dignity of the assumed nature did not shine forth*** — as when the Sun is covered by a cloud, so that it cannot scatter its rays — nevertheless sometimes Christ ***did not dissimulate His majesty and power*** — as when He fasted 40 days and nights, by voice and touch raised the dead, and before the disciples transformed Himself with heavenly glory, and showed His face shining like the Sun.

**XXXI.** ***Fourth, the Scripture speaks of the human nature of Christ glorified*** — that is, [the one] assumed to heaven, and placed at the right hand of God — no longer suffering nor dying, but living in eternity, and exalted to the right hand (Eph. 1) of the ***virtue and majesty of God above all Angels, powers, and virtues, and above every name which is named not only in this, but also in the future age*** — far above and beyond the order of other creatures.
