```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

# Locus V

## De Hominibus — On Man¹¹⁵

*(pp. 299–320 of the imprint.)*

#### Man Created to the Image of God

**All things have been founded for the sake of man.** ***Thirdly***, ***man has been founded to the image and likeness of God*** — that is, adorned with the grace and gifts of God, as we shall shortly say. For ***Zelem*** signifies the ***image of a primary and archetypal thing***, as a shadow or adumbration. ***Demuth*** signifies ***conformity and likeness and [ὁμοίωσις]***. But ***image*** is a name of essence — namely of mind and will; ***likeness*** is a name of quality and of congruity with the archetype or divine mind.

**Fourthly, to man was granted dominion over all corporeal creatures.**

And although this excellence of man has beyond measure been enervated and prostrated by sin, yet each one ought to fit himself to the excellence of human nature. For ***all men and each single one*** who live are born into this world by the singular counsel and deliberation of God, as **Psalm 139** it is written: ***"Mine unformed [substance] Thine eyes have seen, and in Thy book were all my members written, which were to be formed on the days, when yet no one of them existed."*** Fifthly, therefore, [man] is also ***the end*** or the creature more excellent than the other corporeal creatures. And ***all are image of God, and have dominion over the remaining creatures*** — which is restored through the Son of God — so that each one of us who believes in the Son may say: ***"I am confident that neither death nor life shall be able to separate us from the love of God, wherewith He Himself loves us on account of Christ"*** (Rom. 8).¹¹⁵ᵃ

#### What is Called the Image of God

**Now what is called the image of God, is to be seen.** It is manifest that the proper signification of the word ***image*** is that ***the image is the expression or representation of some thing***, either in the soul of man himself, or even outside the soul or the cognitive part. In the soul of man there is an image, as when the human mind thinks something, then it conceives, paints, and delineates a certain ***image of the thing thought*** — as often as one thinks of a known and absent man, in the mind he conceives a certain image, which delineates for himself the figure, stature, gestures, morals, and other similar things of the absent man — as in the verses of Eobanus it is said:

> ***"As often as I meditate on thee, thy image is present to me,***
> ***Though thou be far from here, many miles away."***

Here the ***foundation*** is the absent man; the ***terminus*** is the likeness of that man conceived in the mind of the one thinking about him, representing the substance of the one absent. **Outside the soul** the image is, as when a painter or sculptor expresses the lineaments of the body whose image he paints. For then the ***foundation of the image*** is the statue or picture; the ***terminus*** is the likeness of the lineaments of the face, and of the whole figure of the body, referring and representing the form of the absent one, insofar as it can be done.

It is reported of the Emperor ***Theodosius*** that he was [***ὀξύθυμος***] — that is, as ***Horace*** says, ***easily provoked to anger, so also placable***. That he might as it were put a bridle upon his anger, he brought forth a law, which stands in the Codex ***De poenis***, that ***after the prince's mandates, punishments should be deferred for thirty days.*** This law preserved the ***Antiochene*** multitude, whom Theodosius had ordered to be slaughtered on account of the overturned statue of his wife Flaccilla — which the mob had cast down with ropes thrown around it. When one had come from the mandates already issued to the prefects, and had ordered that it be said to the Emperor that ***"he ought not on account of the image of a dead wife to violate living images of God,"*** the Emperor — who had already deflagrated with wrath, moved also by the singing of some Psalms, and irrigating the goblet with tears — retracted the mandates.

**Now the foundation of the image of God in man is God Himself, transfusing into us the likeness of His mind, wisdom, liberty, justice, and sanctity.** The ***terminus*** is the very likeness and congruity of the highest things in us with God, as we shall now say.

Of Tauler it is reported that with this voice he drove away the Devil and spectres: ***"I am the image of God. Thou art a spirit rejected by God. Therefore there is no fitness or fellowship between us. Depart therefore, and begone — thou hast no commerce with me."***¹¹⁵ᵇ

#### The Twofold Image of God — Natural (in the Son) and Gratuitous (in Man)

**These things concerning the term itself we have set forth — which Holy Scripture accommodates both to the Son of God and to man, but plainly by a diverse reason.** For there is a ***twofold image of God: Natural*** and ***Gratuitous.***

**The ***Natural*** image of God is in the Son of God alone, both essentially and by virtue.** ***Essentially***, for the Son of God is with the Father [***the effigy and character of the Father's substance***, ***the splendour of the paternal glory***] — and there is such a likeness of image in the Son and archetype, that the essence, Divinity, eternity, glory, and majesty of both is the same, as above we have discussed at length. ***By virtue***, indeed, the potency, goodness, will, and grace of the eternal Father in the works of God in Christ is manifested — as the Son of God Himself says (John 14): ***"Philip, he who sees me sees also the Father. I am the Way, the Truth, and the Life. No one comes to the Father except through me."*** And this is what Paul says to Colossians 1: ***"In Christ it hath pleased [the Father] that all the fullness [should dwell]"*** — namely, of Divinity and of redemption and of glory. And Colossians 2: ***"In Christ are all treasures of wisdom and of knowledge hidden."*** Item: ***"In Him dwelleth all the fulness of the Divinity [σωματικῶς]"*** — that is, ***truly and essentially and hypostatically.*** But of this image it is spoken elsewhere, and something has been said above.

**Now of the image of God in man is to be said, which is ***gratuitous***.** For man has been founded to the image of God ***by grace*** at a certain time.

The ancients set forth this distinction: ***The Son of God is called image born, and is not said to be to the image.*** For the Son is ***the eternal image, and equal to the Father.*** But ***man is a created image, not born, nor equal to the Trinity, but simply the image of the Trinity — not from nature but from grace.***

**And although Luther of holy memory most truly speaks that we, when we shall have spoken of that image of God in man to which man was founded, formed, and adorned, are speaking of a thing plainly unknown, which we have not only not experienced, but perpetually experience the contrary — and hear nothing except bare words, and can attain that image not even by intellect — nevertheless he establishes something for teaching's sake, both from that very ***antithesis*** which we carry about in ourselves, and from the saying of Paul interpreting the image, may be said.** For thus Paul speaks Eph. 4: ***"Put on the new man, who is created according to God in righteousness and holiness of truth."***

**The image of God, therefore, in which man was first founded, is both the likeness and congruity of substance with God, and the conformity and congruity of gifts with the will of God — or the integrity of nature endowed with true righteousness and sanctity, spontaneously and integrally satisfying the command of God — and the eminent dignity of human nature before other creatures, and the dominion or principate of man over all things of lower orders — and finally, immortality and perpetual life, without fear, without perils, without sadness and grief, without all adversities, and without end.**

#### The Fourfold Consideration of the Image of God in Man

**In this manner the image of God in man is considered quadrupliciter: first by reason of substance; second by reason of gifts; third by reason of order and eminence; last by reason of immortality.** Some add liberty of will to obey or not obey. But this liberty we ascribe to the gifts, as we shall now say.¹¹⁵ᶜ

#### The First — Reason of Substance

**The reason of substance includes in itself the essence of mind and will, and the elegance of body and members** — showing ***that God is, what sort God is, and what sort man was founded.*** For the human mind, and the notions implanted in the mind, show that ***there is another Mind — wise, eternal, good, just, and free — to which reverence and obedience is owed.*** The human body also, distributed with such variety and series of parts, is an illustrious testimony that God is, and what sort God is, and what sort man is — namely, ***a witness of wisdom and of the goodness of God the workman***, who has impressed upon us the traces of Himself. Whence the voice of ***Galen*** is celebrated, that ***the anatomy of the human body is our leader to the acknowledgement of God.***

**Many long ago contended that man was so far the image of God, that his body was fabricated to the idea of the body which the Son of God was to assume** — and therefore that the Son of God would have taken upon Himself human nature even if man had not fallen. Others also contended that ***the soul of man is of the essence of God***, and that God created the soul from His own substance, since it is written Gen. 2: ***"God breathed into the nostrils of man the breath of life."*** ***"Whoever, therefore,"*** they say, ***"breathes or exhales, sends forth breath from himself."***

Both opinions lack foundation and testimony of Scripture. What is objected — ***"He who breathes, sends breath from himself: God breathes into man, therefore He sent forth breath from Himself, or from His own substance, and consequently the soul is of God's substance, or is part of God"*** — the response is plain: ***God breathes into us the spirit of life***, that is, by His own breath ***effects that we live and have a rational soul, mind, and will***, and communicates to us His righteousness, life, and joy — ***as gifts, not as His own essence*** — because we are founded to the image of God, ***to represent the archetype, not having the essence of the archetype, but referring His image in us.***

**Wherefore when we say that God communicates to us His wisdom, goodness, justice, or even His essence, and pours forth rays of His light, wisdom, and justice into our souls, we understand ***gifts*** which at a certain time began in us, and were transfused into us by God — such that the rational soul (or mind and will) was created by God, not from God nor out of God, infused into us, and made conformable to the divine will, wisdom, light, and justice.** For if the rational soul were absolutely and simply said to be inbreathed into us ***from God***, and to be a part of God, as some have spoken, certainly the human race could not have fallen, nor could it be deceived by any reasoning. It would also be presupposed that ***matter*** existed, and it would be denied that the spiritual thing in us was founded from nothing by God. These things we add for the sake of admonishing that we may be more cautious in speaking of so great a thing — of which in this life we can attain and comprehend scarcely a shadow and idea.

**Augustine accommodates the image of God in man, considered by reason of substance, to the powers or forces of the soul** — as also above in the place on the Trinity we indicated. One is the ***most simple mind***, which has three properties: ***memory, cogitation, and will***. Memory, or the mind remembering or thinking on itself, signifies the ***Father***; cogitation, or intelligence of itself, and image of the thing thought, signifies the ***Son*** of the eternal Father; will signifies the ***Holy Spirit***.

But concerning this and other images in the article of the Trinity ***Nazianzen*** on the Holy Spirit piously speaks, saying: ***"When I have surveyed many things with my very self by thinking, and directed as it were the arrow of reason on every side and in every part, I could yet attain nothing of the lower things which might fittingly be compared to the divine nature. For although a certain slight likeness occasionally offered itself, yet a great part of it deceives — so that I am left below with the image. And, to speak summarily, there is nothing in which my thoughts fix a step, if I have measured the nature of God — which I imagine — to conceived images. I persuade therefore that pursuing images and shadows of this kind — as deceitful and almost far from truth — be permitted to depart."***

And so much of the reason of substance.¹¹⁵ᵈ

#### The Second — Reason of Gifts

**The reason of gifts in man comprehended the whole integral conformity and likeness of man with God** — by which man had the ***righteousness and sanctity of God*** (as Paul speaks Eph. 4), which he as it were represented in a mirror, and rendered to God spontaneously and from the soul in sanctity and justice. For there was ***the integrity of mind*** — a most perfect and most right action, understanding and judging rightly — and ***the action of the heart*** desiring things good and pleasing to God — and ***the obsequies of the body*** in all things which the mind judged and the heart commanded. For there was the most beautiful harmony and [***συμφωνία***] of all forces of mind, heart, and body with the will of God: nothing did the mind think, nothing did the heart desire, nothing did the body and its members execute, except what was in all things approved by God.

**In this way, if the image of God in man before the Fall be referred to gifts, that image is not only some relation and acceptation of the human race before God, but also a quality of second species or [***δευτεροενέργεια***], proceeding at last into [***ἐντελέχεια***]** — that is, it was the ***rectitude and excellent excellence of all the forces of man, of mind, will, heart, and even of the exterior members, congruent with the archetype or divine mind.*** For in the mind was ***light or illustrious knowledge of God***, wisdom and intelligence of all things of the whole of nature, and firm assent without any doubt concerning God, providence, promises, precepts, and divine threatenings. In the will was ***conversion to God*** — that is, universal obedience embracing true and ardent love of God and all virtues excited, flagrant, and directed to this end that the ***archetype*** might be known and celebrated. In the heart were ***affections and appetites*** obeying mind and will, so that there was in nothing dissension. In the body was the ***disposition, rectitude, and nature of all members*** such that it was borne spontaneously to the exact execution of all those things which the mind thought, and the will and heart commanded, and directed all things to the glory of God.

**Among the other perfect and excellent gifts, however, man had also ***liberty*** given to him by God to obey God, or even to choose and do otherwise.** For since God is the ***most free Agent***, He founded man to His own image, and gave him liberty conformable to Himself, as Sirach says (chapter 15): ***"God from the beginning created man, and left him in the hand of his own counsel. If thou wilt, thou shalt keep the commandments, and do what is pleasing to Him in faith. He hath set before thee fire and water; whichever thou now wilt, stretch forth thy hand. Man hath set before him life and death; and whichever he shall will, shall be given to him. For the wisdom of God is great, and He Himself is very powerful, and sees all things, and His eyes look upon those who fear Him, and He Himself knoweth every work of man. To no one hath He commanded to be impious, and He hath not given him licence to sin"*** — that is, ***He always and altogether prohibited the will of sinning, even if man had power of turning himself away from God by reason of primary liberty.***

***"It is one thing to be able, and another to owe and to will to do."*** ***Not all things which we are able to do, ought we to do.*** Whence on this matter Augustine speaks in ***De Ecclesiasticis Dogmatibus*** chapter 21: ***"The first man was committed to liberty of will immediately in his first condition, that with the vigilance of mind unharmed, and the custody of the precept assisting, he might persevere — if he willed — in that in which he was created."*** Item: ***"Most firmly hold, and by no means doubt, that the first men, that is, Adam and Eve, were created good and upright and without sin, with free arbiter, by which they could, if they willed, with humble and good will serve and obey Him — by which arbiter they could also, if they willed, by their own will sin."***

**But since God had founded man in liberty, and had adorned him with the power of living rightly or of departing from the norm** — that is, of understanding and doing good or evil (for this was required for the perfect knowledge of things, and for exact liberty, that the antithesis might be understood, and that from the extreme evil repudiated, that which was good and right should be done) — ***lest man abuse that liberty and power, or be deceived either by himself or from elsewhere***, God added His Word — precept and threatening — and most severely prohibited sin, lest man, if he should turn himself away from God, have any excuse as though he had not known that sin was prohibited to him — but that he might more admire the goodness of God adorning man with the gift of liberty, and the justice of God prohibiting sin — and might accuse himself, that he had both abused liberty and violated God's mandate, and had constituted himself the author of all evils. For man had, and knew, the good, and could have retained it by his own will. He lost the good ***knowingly and willingly.*** Wherefore the whole fault is man's, as below shall be said in [the locus] on sin.¹¹⁵ᵉ

#### The Third — Reason of Eminence

**The reason of eminence over other creatures comprised not only ***gifts and dignity*** by which man excelled and surpassed all animate things** — both in intellect, judgement, and senses, and in the form and rectitude of body, agility of members, and dexterity of all actions — but also ***dominion over all things of lower orders***, and the faculty by which man was able to use all creatures for his salvation, and be subjected to no creature, nor be less than the creatures. For this God wills, when He says: ***"Have dominion over the fish of the sea"*** — that is, ***usurp and exercise right and dominion over subjected things, and be rectors of earth, sea, and air, and of all creatures and animals.***

Of this eminence today we can say nothing, so miserably have we lost it. But there is no doubt that man's virtue was ***most robust***, his stature most beautiful, his members most perfect — so that his eyes surpassed the acumen of eagles and lynxes, the strength of body [surpassed] the strength of lions and bears — which Adam handled as we handle whelps. Now scarcely a shadow of this dominion survives:

> ***"The boar surpasses us in hearing, the spider in touch,***
> ***The dog conquers in smell, the lynx in sight, the ape in taste."***

And known are the verses:

> ***"Nine times the garrulous crow surpasses us in living.***
> ***And four times the stag exceeds the crow's ages.***
> ***The high-footed stag the raven thrice conquers, but him***
> ***nine times over the reparable Phoenix bird multiplies."***

#### The Fourth — Reason of Immortality

**The reason of immortality in man was such, that man could preserve perpetual life if he obeyed God**, and be free of all fear and sadness, and live in perpetual and most ardent joy, resting in the acknowledgement and cult of God.

**Accustomed is the sentiment of the Theologians:** ***"If Adam had not fallen, then with a certain finite number of saints — which God Himself would have constituted — man would have been transferred from animal life to spiritual life, so that no food, drink, or generation was any longer needed"*** — as we through Christ are certain of eternal life of that sort — as Matt. 22 it is written: ***"They shall not marry, nor be given in marriage, but shall be as the Angels of heaven."***

Let these things said concerning the image of God in man, or the first state of man before the Fall, or original righteousness, suffice. Now the definition of man and the admonition concerning the fourfold state of man, and concerning original righteousness we shall subjoin.¹¹⁵ᶠ

#### Definition of Man

**Man is a creature of God — founded by the singular counsel and deliberation of God — consisting of body and rational soul — formed to the image of God with free will — and constituted by the voice of God as governor of the remaining lower creatures — to this end, that he may know God, celebrate Him, love Him, and in Him trust and rejoice.**

#### The Four States of Man

**The sacred letters make mention of four states of man:**

**The first is the state of integrity and innocence ***before sin*** — when in human nature shone the image of God, obedience, and [***συμφωνία***] of all forces — of mind, heart, body, and members — toward God; and there was before God the approbation and acceptation of the human race, and of all the actions of man. For these are synonymous: ***the image of God in man, the first state of man before the Fall, the integrity of human nature, original righteousness*** — of which part is ***free arbiter before the Fall.***

**The second state of man is the most miserable, ***under sin and under the tyranny of the Devil***** — in which are deformation and corruption of the whole image, effected by the fall of the first parents, and derived by carnal propagation into the whole human species, and into all its individuals and each single one: ***darkness in the mind, ignorance of God, and horrid and various doubts concerning God's providence and will; in the will, aversion from God, vacuity of the fear of God, faith, love, and other virtues; carnal security, distrust, admiration of self, concupiscence, depraved inclinations; in the heart, [***anti-symphonia***] and contumacy of all affections against the will of God; and in the whole life many and great calamities and punishments of sins, dangers of diseases, poverty, and infinite other evils; finally death, eternal damnation** — unless reconciliation be made through and on account of the Son as Mediator. This state of man Paul expresses by the term ***old man***, unregenerate, who is retained in original injustice and sin, and is guilty of the wrath of God and of eternal death (Eph. 6: ***"Put off the old man"***; Rom. 6: ***"Our old man is crucified, that the body of sin may be abolished, that we no longer serve sin"***). Of this state, however, more copiously shall be said in the doctrine of sin.

**The third state of man is ***not under sin, not under the yoke of the Law's malediction, not under God's wrath, not under the tyranny of the Devil — but simply under God's grace through and on account of the Son of God***** — who takes away the sins of the world, satisfies the Law, placates the Father's wrath, destroys the works of the Devil, and ***restores and reforms*** the image of God in us deleted through sin — He, who is the ***eternal and essential image of the Father*** — both by the merit of His obedience and by the imputation of His righteousness, and by the efficacy by which He pours the Holy Spirit into our minds and hearts — as Paul speaks Eph. 6: ***"Be renewed in the spirit of your mind, and put on the new man, who is created according to God in righteousness and holiness of truth."*** Item Col. 3: ***"Lie not one to another, having put off the old man with his works, and having put on the new, who is renewed unto knowledge according to the image of Him who created him."*** In this state, therefore, man is ***raised up by the right hand of God*** while he believes in Christ — as Augustine splendidly speaks: ***"Since not, as of his own will man fell, so of his own will he can rise, let us with firm faith hold the right hand of God extended to us from above — that is, our Lord Jesus Christ."***

**The fourth state of man is either the ***glory of the pious after this life*** — when those departing hence believing shall live in eternity — or the ***eternal death of the impious***, in which body and soul shall be handed over to eternal punishments.

**Some add a space that is between man's migration from this life and the last day of the ultimate judgement** — while the body separated from the soul rests beneath the earth, and on account of sin putrefies; the soul, however, ***in God is glad and lives***, or, as pertains to the impious, ***is saddened and shudders***, feeling the judgement of God, until together with its body it be punished with eternal death and damnation. But this can commodiously be joined with the fourth state of man.

**Long ago in the schools they said that man in his first state had this — that ***he could die, and could not die***** — that is, was endowed with free will. In the second state after sin, that ***man can die, and cannot not die.*** For man retained the worse portion, which he chose by his own will — and lost the other, which by his will also he despised and cast off, and preferred to it the other extreme. In the third state is made the ***hope of the first lost good recovered and restored, and certainty, with the peril of the second state abolished.*** In the fourth state after the resurrection man shall have the ability ***not to die, and not to be able to die.*** Let these things concerning the fourfold state of man suffice.¹¹⁵ᵍ

#### Original Righteousness — Whether Connatural, or an Accident Separable

**Moreover, original righteousness is the integrity of the whole nature of man, and of all its parts, forces, and actions — congruent with the archetype (or the mind and will of God) — obedient to God in all and each — and pleasing and accepted by God.**

**In the schools it is disputed whether that righteousness was, as they speak, ***connatural*** — that is, either the substance and essence itself of man, or at least the nature and property of the essence — or whether it was only some ornament and gift outside of nature, and a ***separable accident***, which could accede or recede without the corruption and violation of the subject and nature. Concerning this dispute today also various things everywhere are heard, and often insipid and rude clamours.

**First, to the Monks and to certain scholastic doctors is the answer to be given** — who therefore deny that original righteousness was connatural (for we use the received term) so that in their way not only from this they might build up that ***natural [powers] remain entire*** — and this not nakedly only concerning substance, but concerning the qualities and properties and forces of human nature — but also they do not fear to affirm ***that man by his own powers, which he still has, can fulfil the Law of God.*** For thus the ***Sentences-men*** speak: ***"Man from the dictate of right reason can choose and do the good, and has the right light of reason; and when he can choose what is a lesser good — namely a creature — he can also choose what is a greater good, namely God the Creator; and when he does what is in him, God infallibly gives grace."*** Item: ***"The will of man from pure naturals can dispose itself, by doing what is in it, for the reception of grace, by an act conformable to right reason — which is morally good."*** Item: ***"The will, by removing the obstacle — that is, the purpose of mortal sin — and by a good motion elicited from the liberty of arbiter, can merit the first grace ***de congruo.*** And to merit the first grace that makes gracious, ***grace freely given*** suffices — or the general influence of God with an intellect rightly understanding and a will conformably willing."***¹¹⁵ʰ

**These monstrous words show that the Monks and others did not understand original righteousness — what the Holy Spirit in Scripture names it** — namely, the ***integrity of the whole nature, mind, heart, will, and of all parts and forces***, as above we have said. For they understood original righteousness only as an ornament of nature, an external gift and accident, which could without violation of nature again be taken away.

**Of the monstrous opinion of these, D. Luther speaks thus:** ***"See what follows from that sentiment, if you establish original righteousness not to have been nature, but a certain superfluous, superadded gift. If, as thou positest, righteousness was not of the essence of man — will not therefore also sin, which succeeded, not be of the essence of man? Is it not, therefore, in vain to send the Redeemer Christ, since original righteousness — as an alien thing from our nature — has been taken away, and integrals of nature remain? What can be said more unworthy of a Theologian?"***

**Then response must also be made to those who run into the contrary vices** — and who, while they wish to avoid the delirium of the Pelagians and Monks concerning ***integral naturals***, begin to deny almost all accidents of human nature, of mind, heart, and will — and confound substance and natural potency, and properties, or nature and accidents (or qualities of substance), into one chaos.

**To all these, since Luther himself responds, we shall add his response and words, which thus sound:** ***"Nature indeed remains, but corrupted in many ways — since trust toward God is lost, and the heart is full of distrust, fear, shame,"*** etc. On this account Luther often compares sin to leprosy infecting and corrupting the flesh.

**As therefore sin is ***the leprosy, corruption, depravation, and venom of the whole nature — not indeed nature itself; not the mind itself, but the disease and corruption of mind; not the heart itself, but the perversity of the heart; not the will itself, but the horrible perversion and contumacy of the will; finally, not the man himself founded by God, but the man made evil*** — so ***original righteousness*** was the most beautiful ornament of the whole nature and essence of man, of species and of individuals — not from without, but truly internal and external — a gift of God, which we can, for the sake of speaking in the schools, call either integrity, or property, or the entire and perfect form, or the natural potency of the whole man and of all his forces — although others may name it the essence of mind and will — which rightly understood (as we name the form of a thing the essence of the thing, and matter or substance) is neither absurd nor false.

#### Analogies for Original Righteousness — Sight/Blindness, Health/Sickness, Moonlight/Eclipse

**And although a similitude in the nature of things congruent with this form cannot easily be given** — yet at least for the sake of depicting, from the nature of positive and privative names one or another example can be taken, which can rightly be proposed to children.

- ***Sight*** is a certain sense of the eyes, perceiving light and colour with the eyes — it is not the eye itself, and yet is so in the eye that it can be said to be of the essence and nature of the whole and rightly-behaving eye. On the contrary, ***blindness*** is the ***privation of sight***, and the ***corruption of the integrity of the eyes*** — the eye remaining indeed, but no more suitable for the perception of light and colour.
- Item, ***health of body*** is the nature and form of the body behaving in right manner. ***Disease*** truly is an ***affect against nature***, vitiating the nature and actions of body, or of certain members.
- Thus the ***splendour and light of the Moon*** (for I shall not now say of the Sun, on account of the mysteries which thence are taken) is not indeed the Moon itself, but a quality of the lunar body, by which the Moon shines with light received from the Sun. ***The eclipse of the Moon*** is the privation and loss of light, by which the Moon previously illuminated shone.
- Item, ***sap in a tree*** and the ***vegetative virtue*** is the proper force and faculty (or quality) of the tree, by which in its own way it produces fruit and lives. ***A dry tree*** loses that force, and becomes useless for bearing fruit.

**Such and similar examples can be proposed to children and older students, that right judgements may be formed.** For such before the Fall was ***original righteousness in man*** — the ***proper force and faculty in man*** of rightly perceiving, thinking of God and other things, of obeying God, and of most rightly doing and perfecting all things — that is, it was the ***integrity of the whole essence of man***, and the rectitude and excellent excellence of all the parts and forces of man, of mind, will, and heart.

**Now after the Fall, not indeed the mind, not the will, not the heart, or the substance of man is utterly lost and reduced to nothing** — but, to use Luther's words, ***with nature remaining, the whole essence of man is horribly corrupted***, as leprosy corrupts the whole body — and ***trust toward God is lost, and the heart is full of distrust, fear, and shame*** — as also the experience of all men testifies, which teaches that we are born of unclean seed, and contract from the very nature of the seed ignorance of God, security, unbelief, hatred toward God, disobedience, impatience, and similar most grievous vices, which so have been implanted into our flesh that most widely through flesh, body, soul, nerves, blood, through bones and marrow themselves, in the mind and intellect, in reason, and in the will they are diffused — nor can they fully be taken away thence, nor are even acknowledged to be sin.¹¹⁵ⁱ

#### The Distinction between Substance and Its Corrupted Qualities

**The mind remains, the will remains, and the rational soul, remains also reason itself — which** (as long ago from Aristotle they taught) ***exhorts to the best*** — that is, as Luther most rightly expounds, to those things which are ***politically best*** (of which reason can judge — as are things subjected to reason: government of herds, building of houses, and the like) — and the animal life and substance of man founded by God remains — the general notions of God — that God is, and what sort — remain, even if they be very languid.

**But in the mind is ***horrible darkness*** ignorant of the essence and will of God, and doubting concerning God's providence, care, and will toward us.** Item, in the will is ***aversion from God***, not fearing God, not loving, not trusting; and there is ***carnal security, pride, admiration of self, concupiscence, depraved inclinations — manifold and innumerable.*** In the heart is ***contumacy of all affections against the will of God*** — on account of which evils man is ***enemy of God, hater of God, contumacious, averse from God, and guilty of God's wrath, and of eternal death*** — as Paul says: ***"We were by nature all sons of wrath"*** (Eph. 2).

**These things we say for this reason: to show that ***one thing is the substance itself of man, mind, will, and heart, and another its qualities, or [***συμβεβηκότα***]***.** For the question is not whether there be a mind, a will, and a heart in man, or what it is — but the question is ***what sort the mind is, what sort the will, what sort the heart, and what sort the man*** — namely, ***the mind corrupted, ignorant of God, doubting concerning God; item, the will depraved, averted from God; item, the heart contumacious and perverse.***

**But if any one simply say that original righteousness and original sin are the substance itself of man, what shall come to pass?** Man, original righteousness lost, would certainly cease to be man — because he lost the substance of himself, and no longer retained mind, or will, or heart, or anything which is of human nature. For, the substance destroyed, nothing remains which pertains to it. Moreover, when sin is called the substance of man, what shall come to pass? Either Satan himself shall be constituted the ***Creator of new substances*** — although creation is the proper work of divine majesty; or God, who alone is the ***Creator of all substances***, shall be called the author and creator even of sin — since sin would be a substance. Which is manifestly false, and in a certain manner ***blasphemous.*** What, that ***the righteousness of faith*** succeeding to our sin will no longer be ***imputation, nor consist by faith***, but will have to be called ***substantial in us***?

These things therefore are diligently to be considered — that we may study propriety of speaking, and that by the usage of absurd phrases we may not give occasion to absurd opinions.¹¹⁵ʲ

#### Manichaean Errors on Old-Man / New-Man Terminology

**The Manichaeans, when they read in Paul these words — *vetus homo, novus homo, caro et spiritus, exterior homo, interior homo*, and similar terms — did not receive them as epithets, but referred them to substance** — and therefore they constituted ***two founders and creators of substances***: the evil one of the ***old man***, and the good one of the ***new man***. But well known is the ***Hebrew metonymy***, in which the name of the ***subject*** is placed in the place of the ***adjunct***, and in which ***epithets signify diverse pursuits***. For the ***old man*** is he who is retained in sin, in original injustice, and on account of sin is subject to various calamities, to death, to the wrath of God, and to eternal damnation. The ***new man*** or reborn, is he who is converted and regenerated, who through faith in Christ has been absolved from sin, and receives the remission of sins gratis on account of the Son, and is given the Holy Spirit, through whom he is renewed to the image of God, and is preserved in the faith.

**The diverse pursuits of each man are described in Galatians 5.** For there is a ***perpetual struggle*** of the old and new man, as Paul testifies (Rom. 7). And rightly it is said: ***the old man can be alone; but the new man is never alone in this life, but consists of the new and the old man*** — as the perpetual struggle attests. For the ***new man*** through the Holy Spirit repugns the works of the flesh, or the ***old man.***

In the same manner, ***flesh*** is named the corruption of man, and carnal pursuits, and works of the flesh, which are described Gal. 5. ***Spirit*** comprehends spiritual motions excited through the Holy Spirit. ***Exterior man*** comprises everything which pertains to present or earthly life — as are riches, honours, good health, friendship, and other supports. ***Interior man*** is the conservation of heavenly life (2 Cor. 4).

#### Closing Prayer

**Now imposing an end to this disputation concerning man, we pray God that we — founded to His image, and delivered by the blood of the Son, washed and cleansed, and given the Holy Spirit — may be governed by Him, that we may remain palaces, temples, and vessels or organs of Him, and in true faith bringing forth true fruits may persevere constantly and unanimously unto the end of life. Amen.**¹¹⁵ᵏ
