# Locus VI

## De Peccato — On Sin¹¹⁷

### What is Sin?

*(pp. 320–360 of the imprint.)*

**Sin is a defect, or inclination, or action against the Law of God — offending God, condemned by God, and meriting the wrath of God and eternal punishments — unless remission be made through and on account of the Mediator Christ.**

The Hebrews call [sin] ***deviation, error from the way,*** (*chet, avon, pesha* — as also *hattath*), or transgression of the Law of the Word of God, in which we ought to have walked rightly. Augustine defines it as ***"to delinquish, as it were to flow away from something liquid — from the stability of the firmament and from righteousness."*** The Greeks translate [it as] ***ἁμαρτία and παράβασις,*** which they name ***παρανόμημα*** (against-law-doing), when they have called [the Law] ***νόμος.***

**John *(1 John 3:4)* defines** ***ἁμαρτίαν*** as ***ἀνομίαν*** — that is, ***everything which is done both against the Law of God and without Law and command of God*** — and it embraces the ***matter*** (defect, corruption, inclination, and action fighting with the Law of God) and at the same time the ***form*** or ***guilt*** (of which it is written Deut. 27: ***"Cursed is he who does not remain in all things which are written in the Law,"*** and let all the people say, ***Amen***).¹¹⁸

### Is Sin a Thing Created and Ordained by God?

**By no means. God is not the cause of sin, nor does He will sin, nor does He aid or approve it** — as His wrath against sin, the atrocious punishments, and the mission of the Son into flesh show. And in Ps. 5 it is expressly written: ***"Thou art not a God that willest iniquity."*** Therefore ***the blasphemy of the Stoics and of all who cast the cause of sin back upon God*** is to be detested. Item, the ***fanatical opinions of the Manichees*** — who feigned that there are ***two Gods and Creators, good and evil*** — of whom let students read Epiphanius book 2, tome 2.

### What Then are the Causes of Sin?

The ***tyranny of the Devil,*** and the ***will of man*** obedient to the Devil, and turning himself away from God freely — as in the Book of Wisdom is written: ***"Those things which God has established are salutary, and God has not made death, nor does He delight in the perdition of the living. But the impious call death upon themselves by hand and speech, and death has come into the world through the envy and hatred of the Devil, and they who are of his party imitate him."*** Certain therefore is the saying — and worthy of all acceptation — that ***God has neither created, nor ordained, nor wills, nor aids, nor approves sin.*** Job 34: ***"Far be impiety from God, and iniquity from the Omnipotent."*** And 1 John 3: ***"He who does sin, is of the Devil."*** And Rom. 5: ***"Through one man sin entered into the world."*** Let students read on this Basil the Great's ***Homilia quod Deus non sit causa malorum.***

**And although many inextricable things can be moved, gathered, and disputed by acute men against this true sentence — confirmed from all eternity — nevertheless, all trickery being set aside, let us defend the truth for the glory of God, and let us have consideration of our misery and imbecility — we who are dust, earth, and troublesome mass — and therefore let us be humble and modest, nor let us presume above the capacity of the human ingenuity.**

**We find ***five chief objections*** by which men sometimes wish to transfer the cause of sin to God.**

**The First is that ***God does not impede sin*** — although He could most easily impede it.**

**The Second is that ***He does not immediately punish sins*** — since the norm of justice exacts this.

**The Third is that ***He often seems to punish sins with sins.***

**The Fourth is that ***He permits many to live, persevere, and die in blindness*** — as the Gentiles, and others who without doubt would be converted and give themselves wholly to God if they had the Word — as Christ Himself in Matthew 11 attests concerning Sodom and Gomorrah.

**The Fifth is that ***Scripture often speaks of the induration of Pharaoh*** and of others — the origin and cause of which it seems to refer to God — as is gathered from Exodus, from Isaiah, from Malachi, and from the Epistle to the Romans.

#### Response to the First Objection

**We respond to the first question**: It is true indeed that God according to His eternal omnipotence could not only have impeded all sins, but also have brought it about that there be no cause of sin. But since He Himself is a ***most free agent***, and gave liberty to the rational creature, ***He does not do against the order instituted by wise counsel***, nor does He will the rational creature to be ***coerced*** to render true cults to God — but as He Himself gives His [***spontaneum***] to the human race, so He requires in turn ***spontaneous*** obedience from us — which could not be pleasing to Him if it were rendered coerced, and were led violently by external impulse. Therefore He does not impede all sins, though He could — because ***He requires spontaneous obedience*** — which, although drawn and ruled from elsewhere, is nevertheless not drawn unwillingly.

But he who sins and continues in sins, what else does he do than that he is ***unwilling*** to obey God, and contumaciously resists Him? Because therefore he resists God, God permits him to be led and borne by his own impulse, and constitutes limits for him — how far He shall bear his contumacy — and either He shall gradually correct it through the Word and the Cross, or shall at length punish [him] either in this life or with eternal punishments — unless conversion be made.

**Long ago acute men disputed of this same question,** and contended that ***what does not prohibit evil, is the cause of the evil coming to pass*** — as the cause of a fire is he who did not extinguish the beginning fire; the cause of a shipwreck is the pilot who did not draw in the rope. ***"For into whose power it was placed to prohibit — to him, the fault of what happened is imputed."*** But Clement of Alexandria responds in the same way as we have already responded: namely, ***God does not act violently with us, nor coerce anyone by force, but proposes the Word, through which He is efficacious in those hearing, learning, and apprehending it.*** Often also he repeats these words: ***"Vice or sin is neither [ἀκούσιον] nor involuntary, since it is often perpetrated by man from free will."***

**This also is to be observed: that we ought not to judge of God's omnipotence outside the Word.** God can do all things, and could in one moment take away and impede all evils, and no man's mind would be so hard that He could not by one nod bend, immute, and convert it. But God does not act violently, nor does He regularly coerce nature endowed with liberty to involuntary obedience — but requires ***spontaneous obedience and faith*** — which is (to use Clement's words) [***voluntary assumption, assent of piety, trust and expectation of things to come, and argument or certitude of things not seen***]. Wherefore God wills thee to hear the Word, and not to resist the Word heard — which if thou dost not do, He threatens thee with punishments — and thus in His own fear He represses the impulse to sins, and shows His benevolence toward men while He defers punishments. But if thou be not moved even by threats, at length He executes punishments — and thus in a certain order He proceeds in impeding sins: namely by ***Word, and by exhortation to good, and by dehortation from evil***, then by ***promises of obedience and piety***, and by ***threats of punishments***; afterward by the ***rod*** against the contumacious, and by ***corporeal punishments***; at last by ***death and damnation*** of those who are not converted to God. Therefore splendidly Clement says: ***"God is good, and did not — as the serpent immediately when it strikes bites — but often uses discourse before doing. 'My arrow shall consume them,' He says. Not even out of wrath does He inflict punishment, but considers what is just — namely, that on account of us it is expedient that what is just be passed over. But each one of us chooses punishment when he spontaneously sins — the fault, however, is his who chooses; upon God the fault cannot be conferred."***

**Thus therefore let this be the response to that first objection concerning God not impeding sin — namely:**

1. That God does not violently coerce the nature once endowed with liberty, nor draw the unwilling to piety, but sustains it such as it is.
2. Because He requires spontaneous obedience, which could not be pleasing to Him unless it were spontaneous.
3. Because according to His will manifested in the Word He acts with us. And the Word proposes to us ***promises and threats*** by which we ought to be moved to obedience — or to give punishments at length to God whom we did not obey. How, however, we can rightly obey God — since this is not [in the power] of our nature or of the strength of free arbiter — is shown afterwards in the doctrine of the Gospel.

**This response for informing the judgements of the younger suffices, since it is perspicuous and true.**¹¹⁹ᵃ

#### Response to the Second Objection

**Now to the second objection we respond nothing else than what Paul says (Rom. 2): ***"Dost thou contemn the riches of God's goodness, and patience, and longsuffering? [Knowest thou not] that the kindness of God leads thee to penance?"*****

**But ***why God does not immediately punish the impious*****: the first cause is the ***longsuffering of God giving long space for penance*** — as Gen. 6: ***"The days of man shall be a hundred and twenty years"*** — namely, conceded for penance. And Clement to the Gentiles splendidly writes thus: [***"For so many ages you have been ground down in impiety: you were infants, then boys, then adolescents, then men — but never good. Reverence at least old age, since you are at the setting of life, come to your senses [σωφρονεῖτε] — or at least at the end of life acknowledge God, that the end of life may take on for you the beginning of salvation."***]

**The second cause** is the ***testimony of future judgement***, in which all hidden things shall be made manifest, examined, and judged.

**The third** is that the ***impious are to be more gravely punished.***

**The fourth**, that ***God be celebrated by us on account of His longsuffering***, and that we live continually in exercises of true penance, mindful of the Davidic saying (Ps. 95): ***"Today if you shall hear His voice, harden not your hearts."*** Here belongs the celebrated saying of Augustine: ***"God allows things so foul to happen in opinions, cults, and morals, that His judgements may be everywhere tremendous, and in comparison of impure wickedness most pure discipline may be sweet, and honour may be attributed to Him — but fear or shame to himself who has not fallen in this evil (or with that one ruling), or from that ruling has risen up."***

**The fifth cause** is that ***the merit of the Son of God may be acknowledged***, on account of whom God spares the whole human race.

**Let us also consider the process of God, which He preserves in correcting and punishing us.** [***"For to whom Word, persuading and admonishing, does not medicate, to these threats shall medicate: to whom threats do not medicate, to these the rod shall medicate; to whom not even the rod medicates, these fire shall consume."***] From these things both ***justice*** and ***mercy*** are acknowledged.¹¹⁹ᵇ

#### Response to the Third Objection

**To the third objection — that God often seems to punish sins with sins**, adulteries with adulteries, homicides with homicides, seditions with seditions — according to the sentences: ***"By what any one sins, by the same is he punished"***; [***"By whatever things any one has sinned, by the same is he punished"***] — **the brief response is this: God wills the ***punishment***, whose right and execution is the work of God's will. God does not, however, will the ***sin*** — and the transgression of right belongs to men.**

**Add to this response ***two rules*** taken from universal experience.**

**The prior is:** ***When God punishes the sins of the impious, or even of His own, with punishments which themselves also seem to be sins*** — then He applies for the execution of the punishments ***impious and perverse men***, whose nature is nothing else than to sin — that is, ***vessels of wrath*** — who at length as a rod which the Father has used are cast into the fire, and which otherwise, even if they did not execute those punishments which God wills, would have to be cast into the fire. But one thing is the ***will and action of God***, another of these impious ones. For these obey their own evil desires — this improbity God turns to the executing of His judgements. Thus He punishes David through Absalom; the Judaic people through Asshur (Isa. 10); us He punishes through the Turk, and others who fill all things with manifold crimes, blasphemies, defilements, thefts, homicides, and foul lusts. Of these ***Ambrose*** says: ***"We find that some sins are punishments of other sins, as are vessels of wrath, consecrated for perdition."***

**I shall not be burdened to ascribe the words of Luther, by which certainly nothing better can extricate us from this objection:**

***"Satan and man, fallen and deserted by God, cannot ***will the good*** — that is, [what] is pleasing to God, or what God wills — but are turned into their [own] desires perpetually, so that they cannot but seek what is their own. This will and nature of theirs, therefore, thus averted from God, is not nothing. Nor indeed are Satan and impious man nothing, or have they no nature or will — although they have a corrupted and averted nature. That, therefore, which remains — which we call nature in the impious and in Satan, as a creature and work of God — is not less subject to the omnipotence and action of God than are all other creatures and works of God. Since therefore God moves and acts all things in all, He necessarily moves and acts also in Satan and the impious. He acts, however, in them such as they are, and such as He finds them — that is, since they are averted and evil, and are snatched along by that motion of divine omnipotence, they do nothing but averse and evil things.***

***"Just as if a horseman should drive a three-footed or two-footed horse — he drives indeed such as the horse is (that is, the horse walks badly). But what does the horseman do? He drives such a horse together with sound horses — the [defective one] badly, the [sound ones] well. Otherwise he cannot, unless the horse be healed. Here you see that ***when God works in the evil, and through the evil, evil things indeed happen*** — God nevertheless cannot do evil, although He does evil through the evil — because He, being good, cannot do badly — but uses evil instruments, which cannot escape the snatch and motion of His power. The vice therefore is in the instruments, which God does not permit to be idle, that evil things happen — with God Himself moving — no otherwise than if a smith should saw badly with a saw jagged and toothless. Hence it comes to pass that ***the impious cannot but always err and sin***, because being moved by the snatch of divine power, he is not permitted to be idle — but wills, desires, and does such as he himself is."***

**The other Rule is: ***Whatever*** rewards or punishments turn out for the pious for good, turn out with God thus disposing the event — even if in the middle there be manifold errors, whose cause is not God, but the perverse nature of men. And conversely: ***Whatever*** turns out for the evil unto evil, turns out with God from just wrath thus disposing the event — of whatever kind the middle things are, whose error and confusion is not to be ascribed to God, but to men.**¹¹⁹ᶜ

#### Response to the Fourth Objection

**I come to the fourth objection — somewhat more difficult than the previous — to which according to the Word of God we respond simply that the ***fault of blindness and of destruction is placed in man's sin*** — not only in original perversity, but also in actual sins, idolatry, impiety, ingratitude, and other sins.** Hence Luther says: ***"God wills all men to be saved, since with the Word of salvation He comes to all — and it is the vice of the will which does not admit Him, as it is said Matt. 23: 'How often would I have gathered thy children, and thou wouldest not?' But why the majesty does not take away or change this vice of our will in all — since it is not in the power of man, or why He imputes it to him, since man cannot lack it — this is not permitted to inquire; and even if thou askest much, thou shalt never find [out] — as Paul Rom. 11 says: 'Thou who art thou, who repliest against God?'"***

**Wherefore, why God has left the Gentiles for so many ages in their blindness, and even now leaves many peoples in the earth blind, and does not call them by His Word** — all this happens by the ***just judgement of God punishing sins***. But let us acknowledge the punishments of sin, and live in the fear of God and in true piety — nor let us either subtly or curiously dispute of the ***arcane judgement of God***, mindful of the saying of Prov. 25: ***"As he who eats much honey — it is not good for him: so he who is a searcher of majesty, shall be oppressed by glory."*** Whatever also we think, say, or dispute of this matter, let us always remember the voice of David: ***"Just art Thou, O Lord, and just is Thy judgement."*** Let us also be grateful, and acknowledge this immense benefit of God — that God has willed us to be citizens of His Church, and that in true humility we may serve Him — nor by our own ingratitude irritate Him, and provoke Him to disinherit and abdicate us. For here it is truly said: ***"More foully is a guest cast out than not admitted"*** — as Christ Himself shows the same Matt. 11: ***"Woe to thee Chorazin, woe to thee Bethsaida! For if in Tyre and Sidon had been done the virtues which have been done in you, they long ago in sackcloth and ash would have done penance,"*** etc.

**Although Augustine in his ***Questions*** on this matter and on that passage — Tyre, Sidon, and others — rightly and piously affirms that it is not to be disputed, nevertheless to those too anxiously inquiring, [the answer] must be [made]: that Christ speaks after the human manner, and accommodates the speech to the common capacity of the human mind — comparing the citizens of Bethsaida and their neighbours with the Tyrians and Sidonians, whom they themselves held as nefarious contemners of God, and fled as infamous on account of impiety, pride, luxury, and other vices. These He compares with the cities of Galilee, that He may more gravely reproach the ingratitude to these. He does not, however, speak of the ***arcane judgement of God*** — what God foresaw would happen, if He should endow the Tyrians, Sidonians, Sodomites, and others with the preaching of His Word — but only says what they would have done, insofar as from the thing itself it could be perceived, as if to say: ***"These were impious and perverse in doctrine, life, and morals. What wonder? For they did not have the Word of God, nor did they see miracles by which they might be admonished to repent. But you hear the voice of God, and see a huge abundance of miracles — [yet] without any progress, because you are swollen with an unhealable contempt of God and malice. Judge therefore who are worse, and whose judgement shall be more difficult."***

**That this is the sentence of the words of Christ, the context of the discourse shows.** This comparison has nothing of scruple. And what afterwards the contentious insist on and ask — ***why*** God did not give the Sodomites, Tyrians, and Sidonians as great light of Word and miracles as He gave to the Jews and Galileans — this is ***alien from the scope and sentence of the words of Christ.*** Whatever also may be said, we neither ***can*** nor ***ought*** to fly higher in inquiring and speaking than that — even if to the preserving of the Tyrians and the rest there was a remedy in the hand of God — nevertheless ***God was a just avenger in casting them off and destroying them***, and the fault is to be ascribed ***not to God***, but to ***the sin of men and to their perverse nature averted from God and adverse to God.*** And this ***immoveable and true rule always to be held: 'Why men perish, they themselves always find the cause — namely, sin — that they may impute nothing to God. But why they are saved, they in themselves find nothing, but they are saved gratis through and on account of the Son, from the mere mercy and goodness of God, without any works of the Law.'***¹¹⁹ᵈ

#### Reasons Why God Left the Gentiles in Blindness

**Let there be noted, by the way, the chief causes** which are wont to be adduced and proposed to children on this question — ***why God for so many ages left the Gentiles in blindness***.

**First** is the ***just wrath of God against sin***, and the ***just judgement against vessels of wrath***.

**Second**, that ***this very blindness might be the punishment*** of sins — even of actual [sins], idolatry, blasphemy, lust, and similar.

**Third**, that the ***calling of the Gentiles might be reserved for the incarnation of the Son of God***, who with His own voice should despatch the disciples into all the earth.

**Fourth**, that the ***Devil might be confounded***, who, when he thought himself already to be lord of the world, and to have all Gentiles in his own power, was confounded when he saw his kingdom destroyed by the preaching of the Gospel, and the Church of Christ gathered everywhere among the Gentiles.

**Fifth**, that ***every mouth might be stopped, and all men humbled*** — and, of themselves and their own merits, works, wisdom, virtue, and sanctimony of life, ***despair*** — and take refuge with the ***mercy of God alone***, who has mercy on those on whom He has mercy — only ***gratis***, through and on account of the Son — and thus fear the majesty of God, and reverently subject all things to His will, and give thanks to God for this immense benefit — that they live in the assembly of the called, and hear the doctrine of the Son of God and of the salvation of believers.

**Sixth**, that it might be ***manifest*** that all things ***lie subject to the will of God***, which never departs from the norm of its justice and rectitude, and which always sets forth examples of its justice, judgement, potency, and mercy (Rom. 9). Hence is that splendid voice of Augustine: ***"The supreme rule of justice is the will of God, so that whatever He wills, by the very fact that He wills, is to be held just. For He wills nothing which is not just."***

**It is established, however, that ***God has always called some out of the Gentiles, and converted them*** — as in Egypt through Joseph and the family and posterity of Jacob; in the Babylonian captivity through Daniel and others.** And often God willed the ***detraction of the ten tribes*** — which after Solomon were snatched from Judah — to profit the Gentiles: as in the kingdom of Samaria, and in Nineveh (where the family of Tobit was), and in the cities of the Medes (where the family of Raguel and Gabelus was).

**The Gentiles therefore are ***inexcusable*** in many ways:**

- On account of the ***sin and malice of nature***;
- On account of ***injustice*** in which they held the truth of God (as Paul speaks) — that is, they did not obey God even if they had the notions of the Law divinely implanted in themselves;
- On account of many, ***horrible, and foul sins*** which they committed willingly and knowingly;
- Finally, on account of the ***contempt of true doctrine***, which even when offered they repudiated, nor received, nor magnified, nor willed to be studious of it, nor sought it out, nor embraced it when present — but by their own negligence and ingratitude cast it away.

For these causes we read Matt. 10 that Christ said to His disciples: ***"Go not into the way of the Gentiles, and into a city of the Samaritans do not enter — but go rather to the lost sheep of the house of Israel."*** For to these — who had the true cults of God, of which for the causes now recited the Ethnics lacked — true doctrine of the Gospel first was to be revealed, according to the promise and covenant which God had specially made with the race of Abraham — until the time was mature for publishing grace and pouring it out on all Gentiles — which was to happen from the resurrection of Christ, when the veil of the temple was now plainly rent, and the wall of partition destroyed. The beginning, however, ought to be made from the ***elect people*** according to the promise — as Acts 13 Paul and Barnabas say: ***"To you the Word of God ought first to have been preached; but because you repel it, and judge yourselves unworthy of eternal life, behold, we turn to the Gentiles."*** Rightly did someone say: ***"If to any this human prohibition seems little, since Christ does not deign the Gentiles the participation of the Gospel — let him strive with God, who — the rest of the world excluded — sanctioned His covenant with Abraham alone in the seed of a woman — upon which this mandate of Christ depends."***

**Acts 16** — Paul and Timothy, passing through Phrygia and the Galatian region, ***were prohibited by the Holy Spirit from speaking the Word of God in Asia.*** When they had come into Mysia, they were attempting to go into Bithynia, and ***the Spirit of Jesus did not permit them.*** But why was it prohibited to the Apostles that in those places they preach the Gospel? It is customarily and rightly responded that ***God willed them to be sent elsewhere*** where greater fruit would be produced. But why, they say, did He meanwhile leave Asia, why Bithynia, why other Gentiles? Certainly God willed by ***just judgement*** that this be done, deferred, or omitted altogether. And ***the fault is of the Gentiles, not to be transferred to God.*** For by free will they turned themselves from God, and willingly persevered in sins and in their blindness. Nevertheless they ought to return to God, and study piety. But how could they do this, since they did not have the Word? So reason inquires. But of these things shortly it shall be said. Now here we stand, and say: ***"Just art Thou, O Lord, and just is Thy judgement."***

**Here reason exclaims: Therefore God is** [***φιλοπρόσωπος***] **[a respecter of persons]. Item, therefore the promise of grace is not universal, nor does it pertain to all men. Item, therefore the accusation and damnation of the Gentiles is unjust, since they did not know what God willed, being destitute of the Word of God.** Of these things very few we shall briefly subjoin.

**That with God there is no** [***προσωποληψία***] — Deut. 10, Acts 10, Isa. 20, Rom. 4, and often elsewhere is shown. With this sentence, that the ***exclusion of certain Gentiles*** and certain examples (as of Jacob and Esau, and the parable of Matt. 20) seem to fight — some appearance indeed of fighting is there for the judgement of our reason, and yet the fighting is not true. [***Prosopolēpsia***] contains in itself three [things], of which none can be imputed to God. First…¹¹⁹ᵉ

### §4. How many kinds of sin are there? — Quotuplex est peccatum?

**There are two: Original and Actual.**

**Original sin is the obligation to death and to other punishments on account of the fall of the first parents** — through whom we lost, in its chief parts, the image of God according to which man was created in true righteousness and sanctity — and we have been made ***like the Devil, unjust, blasphemous, and damned*** — so that in our nature there is ***extreme corruption***, or a ***defect and inclination fighting with the Law of God,*** or ***evil concupiscence against God and against neighbour*** — born with us, and meriting the wrath of God and eternal punishments — unless remission be made on account of the Son the Mediator.¹²⁰ᵃ

**Long ago in the schools they handed down this definition: Original sin is the ***lack of original righteousness*** — that is, of that righteousness which God gave by creating to man, and which He now requires of us in the Decalogue, saying: ***"Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thy heart, with all thy soul, with all thy strength — and thy neighbour as thyself."*** For the Lord requires of us a reckoning of the things given, as of servants, and requires of us ***true righteousness and sanctity — which is the image of God, which He gave to the first man*** — which since we no longer find in ourselves, we ought to acknowledge our sin and seek the grace of God in Christ, according to Rom. 3: ***"The Law is the acknowledgement of sin"*** — and Gal. 3: ***"The Law is a schoolmaster unto Christ."***

**The sacred letters often make mention of original sin.** In **Gen. 8** it is said of it: ***"The thought of man's heart is evil from his youth"*** — that is, the whole fabrication or mass is corrupt. **Ps. 14:** ***"There is not one just — namely by nature."*** For ***"we are by nature sons of wrath,"*** says Paul (Eph. 2). And ***"the sense of the flesh is enmity against God"*** (Rom. 8) — which, however sad and horrible to hear, pious minds in contrition experience and attest. As **David, Ps. 51:** ***"Behold, I was conceived in iniquities, and in sins did my mother conceive me."*** And Paul, **Rom. 7:** ***"There dwelleth not in my flesh good. O wretched man that I am, who shall deliver me out of this body obnoxious to death?"*** And Rom. 8: ***"By the offence of one, we all are dead."*** And celebrated and worthy of note is the voice of ***Doctor Staupitz***: ***"Ah, how grateful it ought to be to us, if we die — provided only that we may sometime cease to sin, and be freed from this prison-house of sins."***¹²⁰ᵇ

It is wont to be objected: ***"An alien sin damns no one. The sin of origin is alien, because it was perpetrated by the first parents. Therefore it does not damn us."*** The response to the minor is true: the evil of origin is ***not alien*** — as though it did not pertain to us — but is derived by propagation from Adam into all posterity, so that of all posterity it is their ***own sin*** — with whose stains we are all involved and contaminated, and are guilty — as Paul shows Rom. 5: ***"Through one man sin entered into the world, and thus into all men death invaded, because all sinned."*** And Augustine ***De Fide ad Petrum*** ch. 23 says: ***"Original sin is that by which human nature was changed into worse, so that not only in the first men themselves did death obtain reign through sin, but also into all men there passed the reign of sin and death."***

And rightly is given this similitude: as ***leprous parents generate leprous sons*** — who are contaminated both by the leprosy of the parents and by their own — so the posterity of Adam is stained and guilty both by the contagion of the first parents and by its own vice. For this grave delict infects not only the person but also the nature (to use Bernard's words), and occupies not only the race of men but each single one of that race — so that there is not one who escapes, not one from the first man even unto the last, from the sole of the foot even to the crown — just as if by one girder cast down, the other girders placed alongside it collapse, all together — and, the foundation broken, the whole building must fall in ruin.

**The greatest sin therefore was Adam's** — not only that he, although whole in nature and of the highest intelligence, cast off the Word of God — but that ***all the sins and damnations of men are from him.*** Nor could he have lived, and overcome so great a sorrow, had he not been sustained by the divine consolation and promise.

**Our nature and reason sometimes murmurs, that we all are cast into so great evils on account of the delict of one.** But let us acknowledge the wrath of God against sin, and give God the praise of righteousness, and give Him thanks for the deliverance through and on account of the Son — as Augustine says: ***"Original vice is sin, sickness, and the wrath of God, whose sons naturally we are — even if we had not our own sins — from which the Lamb of God, who taketh away the sins of the world, delivers us."*** Let us therefore look upon this Deliverer, and not murmur against God nor against our first parents, nor excuse ourselves as though we were not guilty or partakers in the fall of the first parents — but let us look upon ourselves and acknowledge our own sins which we have, which we feel, bear about, do, and accumulate — and let us know that ***we have a certain hope of greater glory through and on account of the Deliverer who takes away the sins of the world*** than if we were altogether innocent.

There is reported the speech of a certain notorious fool who, dying, when he heard it said to him that we all are subject to death because we all sinned in Adam, responded: ***"At that time I was not living, nor did I ever see Adam"*** — and at length, when it was said, ***"Ey, wir haben alle einen Leihkauf getrunken,"*** he burst forth in these words: ***"Bin ich denn dazumal so nechig gewest, so gesegne mir's der Teuffel."*** Similar examples can be recited many. But this happens from not considering the reason of God's righteousness. To those rightly instituted, and humbled by the Cross, and turning themselves to the Son of God, such thoughts (which sometimes vex even the not-wicked) can bring no scruple.¹²⁰ᶜ

### §5. What are the effects and punishments of the sin of the first parents, and of the whole posterity?

**First is the ***nakedness of men themselves*** — that is, the loss of the greatest gifts by which the nature of man was adorned, in soul and in body: in the mind, darkness ignorant of God, and doubts concerning God's providence and will; in the will, aversion from God, carnal security, and concupiscence (or proclivity and motion to sinning); in the heart, contumacy of all affections against the will of God — and in sum, the depravation of the whole human nature and of all its forces, and the loss of original righteousness.

**Secondly, ***guilt***, by which all men are guilty on account of the fall of the first parents, and are conceived and born in sins.

**Thirdly, ***the wrath of God***, which is a consuming fire.

**Fourthly, the ***fount and springhead of all sins, errors, and crimes*** — internal and external — against the first and second Table of the Decalogue. And here belong all the differences of sins: ***actual sins,*** all ***mortal, of omission*** (when we do not do what we ought to do), ***of commission*** (when either by thought, or by speech, or by deed we perpetrate what we are forbidden to do), ***sins of ignorance, of infirmity, and of malice.***

And here can be instituted a long catalogue of sins against the individual precepts of the Decalogue — as against the ***First Precept***: doubt of God, and of providence, and of doctrine; distrust, desperation, confidence, presumption, trust of human counsel, cult of idols, superstition, magic and whatever arises from it, oracles of the Gentiles and their interpretations, diabolical divinations, incantations, auguries, superstitious sortileges, prohibited divinations of every kind; hatred of God, murmuring, indignation; the stupor not feeling the flames of God's love; security, contempt of God, dissimulation or hypocrisy, pride, self-love, impatience, apostasy, pertinacity, scandals, and many others. Against the ***Second Precept***: intermission of prayers and negligence in prayer, Gentile invocation (Turkish, Jewish, and idolatrous — as of the dead, statues, saints), ingratitude for God's benefits, heresies, corruptions of doctrine, curiosity in non-necessary matters or inextricable questions, negligence in teaching, perjuries, impious oaths, calumnious interpretations of oaths. Against the ***Third Precept***: contempt of gatherings, of feasts, and of sermons in the Church, neglect of the use of the Sacraments (or unworthy use of them), transfer of feasts to other occupations, delights, and cares, abuses of those things which pertain to the ministry, tenacity in not helping faithful teachers and ministers of Churches and schools. Against the ***Fourth Precept***: [***astorgia***] (lack of natural affection), excessive indulgence, impiety of children, superstitious and impious obedience, contumacy, transgression of honest laws, sedition, tyranny or cruelty, confusion of grades, [***alazoneia***] (boastfulness), sloth, idleness, adulation, scurrility, rusticity, moroseness, irony, ostentation, ambition, envy, impudence. Against the ***Fifth***: injury, petulance, wickedness, immanity, [***prosōpolēpsia***] (partiality of persons), timidity, temerity, softness of mind, excessive pusillanimity, levity, inconstancy, obstinacy, Stoic difficulty of morals, disdain of present things, wrathfulness, hatred, desire of private revenge, homicide, slowness, [***mnēsikakia***] (memory of injuries), enviousness, livor, zeal of contention, dissimulation, feigned kindness, [comitasficta], suspicion, perfidy, distrust, excessive credulity, [***propeteia***] (rashness), courtly levity, ferocity, and other things which cohere with these. Against the ***Sixth Precept***: lust, whoredom, adultery, defilement, incest, impurity, illegitimate use of seed, illegitimate custom, divorce outside the case of fornication, unclean thoughts, obscene speeches, feigned chastity, prohibitions and vituperations of marriage, impudence, lasciviousness, wantonness or proclivity to depraved pleasures and to inflicting contumelies, excessive and rustic bashfulness; drunkenness, gluttony, crapula, [***heluatio***] (excess), luxuries or [***asōtia***], hypocritical and superstitious fasting, excessive abstinence harming health. Against the ***Seventh***: excessive coveting of another's goods, thefts of every kind — as frauds in contracts, unjust gain, usury, unjust contract, public expilations, violent ablation of goods, rapacity, private thefts, rapine of ecclesiastical goods, unjust wages and stipends (which are devoured by the slothful), sloth of the household, unnecessary mendicancy, injustice, prodigality and profusion, avarice and sordidness or [***aneleutheria***], inhospitality. Against the ***Eighth***: all lies in life and speech, and all sophistry in the disciplines, subtle trifles, heresies of every kind and noxious opinions, calumny, false delations (such as today there are in courts), perfidy in promises, oaths, contracts, and treaties, mockeries, jests by which snares are silently laid for the neighbour, [***eirōneia***] (dissimulation), masks under which evil arts are covered, sycophancy, boasting or [***thrasōn***]-like glorying, detraction, disparagement, levity, [***kenodoxia***] (vainglory), [***philotimia***] (love of honour), turpitude in speech, gestures, and life, duplicity, [***huponoia, hupokrisis***] (suspicion, hypocrisy), the kiss of Judas, courtly hypocrisy of benevolence, obscurity in speaking or unintelligible incomprehensible difficulty; Academic suspension feeling nothing certain; pertinacity, garrulity, futility or vanity opposed to honest taciturnity, silence in those things concerning which one ought not to have been silent, infancy in speaking, every abuse of eloquence and tongue, distortion and corruption of laws to evil things, excusation of evil actions, depravation of right, patronage in unjust causes, connivance of the magistrate at lies and cosmetics and other impostures. Against the ***Ninth*** and ***Tenth***: concupiscence and all disobedience and transgression. For ***concupiscence*** is named — not some ordered appetition in human nature which would have been even if man had not fallen (as hunger, thirst, delight, love, [***sygenē***] benevolence, hatred against the Devils) — but the ***vitious concupiscence*** [***δυσμετρία***] in all forces of man, fighting with the Law of God, and the confusion and depravation of appetitions in nature, meriting the wrath of God and eternal punishments, unless remission be made on account of the Son. For of this Paul says Rom. 8: ***"The sense of the flesh is enmity against God."*** This is truly a foul thing both to think and to hear.¹²⁰ᵈ

**Fifthly**, the ***tyranny of the Devil*** driving men to abominable crimes — homicides, incests, idolatries, and innumerable other flagitious deeds — as the history of all times attests, and our age abounds with examples.

**Sixthly**, ***evil conscience***, when sin is detected and accused by the Law — as Adam hid himself together with his wife from the face of the Lord, and hearing the voice of God, ***"Adam, where art thou?"***, trembled more and fled from God. Hence Sirach 21 says: ***"Any sin is like a sharp sword, and wounds incurably."***

**Seventhly**, ***the immense mass of most sad confusions and human calamities*** — maledictions, sweats and labours, poverty, exiles, manifold diseases, infinite misfortunes, sorrows, perils, hunger, wars, seditions, and whatever can be thought or said which is evil in every state of life, and in each one's household, soul, body, and things.

**Eighthly**, ***corporeal death***, as Sirach 25 says: ***"From a woman sin was made, and on account of it we are all subject to death."*** And Paul: ***"Death is the wages of sin."***

**Ninthly**, ***eternal casting-away and condemnation.*** Exod. 32: ***"Him who has sinned against me, I will blot out of the book."*** And Gen. 2: ***"By death thou shalt die, on whatever day thou eatest"*** — that is, ***thou shalt be deprived of eternal life, and cast away from my face.***

**From these things it appears why Augustine, Luther, and others use the term ***"bondage of the will"*** — not that the liberty is taken away which remains in those things which are subjected to our reason (or in external discipline), nor that the will is coerced to obedience — but that ***in spiritual righteousness the will must be freed by Christ, reformed by the hand of God*** — since man (to use Augustine's words) — ***whom half-alive robbers left in the way, wounded and pierced with grievous wounds*** — cannot so ascend to the summit of righteousness, as he could descend. Now we are slaves of sin, nor can we flee or take away sin, unless the grace of God succour us, teach us, rule us, aid, deliver, and vivify us — nor can we even those things which are of God and pertain to righteousness rightly understand or attain, perceive, and believe — or at least even inchoately begin true righteousness, much less perfect it — unless God prevent, come, assist, will, work, confirm, and perfect. For ***"the animal man perceiveth not the things which are of the Spirit of God"*** (1 Cor. 2). And the Son of God says: ***"Without me you can do nothing."***

### §6. Is Concupiscence, which is left in the Baptised and Reborn, truly Sin? — The Fomes Peccati Debate against Trent

**It is altogether sin, and not only ***punishment***, as the Monks taught, and the Council of Trent** — whose words I shall subjoin: ***"If any man denies that by the grace of Jesus Christ our Lord, which is conferred in baptism, the guilt of original sin is remitted; or affirms that not all which has the true and proper reason of sin is taken away, but says that it is only ***erased***, or ***not imputed*** — let him be anathema. In the reborn, however, God hates nothing, because there is nothing of damnation to those who are truly buried with Christ through baptism into death, who do not walk according to the flesh, but putting off the old man and putting on the new (who is created according to God), are made innocent, immaculate, pure, harmless, beloved of God — are made heirs of God and co-heirs of Christ — so that nothing at all delays them from the entrance to heaven. But that in the baptised remains concupiscence or the ***fomes*** [tinder] — this the holy Synod confesses and holds; which indeed, since it is left for the struggle, cannot injure those not consenting, and manfully repugning through the grace of Jesus Christ. Nay rather, they who shall have contended lawfully, shall be crowned. This concupiscence — which the Apostle sometimes calls sin — the holy Synod declares that the Catholic Church has never understood to be called sin, in the sense that it is truly and properly sin in the reborn, but because it is from sin, and inclines to sin. But if any thinks otherwise, let him be anathema."***¹²⁰ᵉ

**To these things what shall we say, when we are now with anathema stopped up on every side?** But it is written: ***"The malediction of the impious does not come"*** — that is, is not efficacious. We say therefore with the Apostle Paul Rom. 7 that ***concupiscence — which brings forth evil fruits in man even after baptism — is by itself a damnable sin***, since it is against the Law of God. And immoveable is the argument: ***whatever is against the Law of God, is truly sin. Concupiscence is against the Law of God. Therefore it is sin.*** For [***where there is no Law, there is no transgression***]. And the Law says: ***"Thou shalt not covet."*** And Paul: ***"I did not know sin except through the Law: for I did not know concupiscence, unless the Law had said, 'Thou shalt not covet.'"***

**It is to be known and observed by us that ***the grace of baptism is twofold — namely, remission of sins and regeneration***.** The remission is ***full***, and truly the guilt is taken away in baptism. But regeneration is only ***begun***, and makes its progressions through the whole life. For sin is not immediately in one day extinguished in us and rooted out through baptism, but there remain ***the remnants of sin***, which — since the guilt is taken away — are ***not imputed.*** Nevertheless sin remains in the nature, as Paul manifestly shows — not to speak now of the experience and feeling of all pious [men] — saying Rom. 7: ***"I find a law willing to me to do good, since evil lieth adjacent to me."*** Hence there arises a perpetual struggle in the pious, and hence the distinction of the old and new man is understood — which certainly by those who deny concupiscence or original sin to be truly sin after baptism, can by no means be acknowledged or understood.

Our adversaries suckle many gross and foul errors in this doctrine — such that at the Colloquy of Worms 1557, when on account of this article, concerning which they manifestly said absurd things, they excogitated the counsel of breaking off that [Colloquy], truly one judged that ***they vomited forth from the fomes.*** They say concupiscence signifies only appetitions, and that it is in the sensitive appetite, not in the mind.¹²⁰ᶠ

**The cause of this is ignorance of the signification of the word ***concupiscence*** in the sacred [writings].** For it is not spoken of appetitions in nature founded — as to desire food, drink, marriage, and other things — but when it is spoken of ***vitious*** concupiscence, it must necessarily be understood as the ***horrid depravation and [ἀπαξία] of all forces in the whole nature of man*** — as are, in the mind darkness and fog concerning God, doubts of God's providence and will; in the will, aversion from God, being without love and fear of God, and without ardent trust; in the heart, contumacy, rebellion, uncleanness, depraved inclinations — from which arise depraved affections and errant impulses fighting with the Law of God — namely envy, furies of wraths, of lusts, of avarice, and of lies. And there is no doubt that ***concupiscence, original sin, the thought of the human heart, corrupted flesh, sense of the flesh, perverse heart, rebellion, indwelling sin, law of sin and of members, old man, and the like are synonyms.***

Then they say ***concupiscence is a good thing*** — as also Pelagius wanted concupiscence to be neither sin nor evil, but a thing worthy of praise. Hence a certain Franciscan of Cologne wrote thus: ***"If it were commanded to flesh not to lust, it would be as if God should command the sun and moon not to shine, or fire not to burn."*** But (to use D. Philip's words) ***what more asinine could be excogitated than to feign that concupiscence is a good thing like the light of the Sun?*** For the substance created by God must be distinguished from the vice [***δυσμετρία***] which afterwards acceded. One thing is the light of the Sun, another is an eclipse.

Then they say original sin is [***ἀδιάφορον***] (indifferent). But it must be deplored that man, in so clear light of divine word, convinced also by all experiences, rushes into such folly, that against the truth he says that thing indifferent which he knows and experiences to be against God and against the Law of God, and to be indeed ***enmity against God.***

Beyond this, denying that concupiscence is sin, they say it is only ***guilt*** — as Occam and Eccius said (although Occam added that he was hindered by the authority of the Fathers, that he did not so speak). Eccius indeed at last so far inclined that he said original righteousness was only ***acceptation***, and original sin was ***guilt and imputation of sin*** — which otherwise is not truly sin. This is certainly to play and jest in so great matters, nay to do the highest injustice to God, as all the pious understand.

They add also that ***original sin is no longer sin but only punishment.***¹²⁰ᵍ

**The chief arguments which they have in this matter are almost these:**

**Eccius argues thus:**
> ***Nothing is to be affirmed in the Church, which fights with the sacred letters and the consent of the Church.***
> ***To assert sin after baptism is against the word of God, because it is written Ezek. 36: "I will pour upon you clean water, and you shall be cleansed from all your defilements": "He who said 'from all,' passed over none," says Jerome.***
> ***Therefore it is false to say that after baptism original sin is truly sin.***

**The minor is to be denied.** For Paul expressly says (Rom. 7): ***"I did not know concupiscence to be sin, unless the Law had said, 'Thou shalt not covet.'"*** Item: ***"Sin dwelleth in me."*** Item: ***"I see another law in my members, repugning the law of my mind, and captivating me in the law of sin which is in my members."*** And with this testimony of Paul agrees the experience of all the pious — who daily acknowledge, feel, deplore their internal uncleanness, root of all evils and punishments — do not extenuate, as the Monks extenuate, so that thence they might have easier access to those foul errors, that they affirm the reborn are just by their new obedience, when they do what is in them, nor does the internal disobedience oppose them, which is only ***fomes*** and ***punishment***, not sin. Against these errors let us hold the saying of Paul: ***"Sin dwelleth in me."*** And Job 9: ***"Truly I know that man is not just before God"*** — namely, by his own fulfilment of the Law. Item there: ***"I fear all my sorrows, because I know that Thou wilt not find [me] innocent."*** And Moses: ***"Before Thee the innocent is not innocent."***

That the adversaries interpret Paul as though he speaks of a man not yet converted, when he says ***there is sin in our flesh, and the sense of the flesh is enmity against God*** — this is plainly false — as the text itself shows, when Paul says: ***"I delight in the Law of God according to the interior man"*** — that is, insofar as I am reborn, and have true beginnings of faith. ***"But I find another law in my members"*** — that is, another government in my carnal nature, which by assiduous warfare impedes, languishes, and accuses me.

**Wherefore we conclude that Paul does not say ***"there is no sin now,"*** but says ***"there is no condemnation"*** — that is, the justified by faith please God on account of the Mediator Son, even if in them sins adhere, vitiated concupiscence, and depraved affections, which they repugn and with which they struggle so long as they live.** For regeneration is begun in this life, and through the whole life makes its progresses — until sin is utterly deleted and mortified, the body extinguished, which as Paul says is destined to death on account of sin — namely inhering [sin]. By no means therefore is the dignity of baptism extenuated, but exaggerated and commended. For sin is truly remitted to us on account of the Son. Truly we are washed from filth, so that they can no more injure us — so that even if they be not utterly taken away by one onset (since we still bear about flesh conceived and born in sins, and here have only the true beginnings of life), yet they be ***covered, not imputed***, and so be present as not to be obstacles — and be so felt as to be acknowledged, and that the dignity of baptism may be commended and become more known to us and more illustrious — [we] who now, even if in us sin adhere, are yet ***just by faith on account of the Son the Mediator***. And that thus Paul speaks is certain. That thus Augustine holds, is not obscure. That thus all the pious hold, speak, and daily in the struggle of the old and new man, and in the exercises of faith, invocation, hope, patience, and other virtues experience — is manifest to all who are not utterly hardened. Hence Augustine says: ***"I would say no one is without sin in this life — however much the Pelagians be puffed up and burst with insanity — not because anything of sin remains which is not remitted in baptism, but because in us remaining in the infirmity of this life daily [sins] do not cease to be made, which to those faithfully praying are daily remitted."*** And in *De Civitate Dei* book 13 he splendidly says: ***"He who thinks he lives here without sin, does not act so as not to have sin, but so as not to receive pardon."*** And 1 John 1 — not only of actual, but of inhering-in-nature sins — John expressly says: ***"If we shall have said that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us."***

**Another argument:**
> ***Form gives being to a thing. The formal [element] of sin is taken away in baptism. Therefore its very being is taken away, so that sin is no longer.***

**Response:** One thing is to speak of ***substantial form***, which if taken away, the thing is taken away wholly — as when the soul migrates from the body, the life of the body is finished. Another is to speak of ***accidental form***, which if taken away, does not take away the thing itself — as rightly in the schools it is said: ***"Often the foundations remain when the relations are extinguished; or the materials when the formal is taken away — as a stone in a field remains a stone, even if it no longer, as before, discharges the office of a boundary-marker."*** Since sin in man is an ***accident***, not the substance of man, there remains indeed the ***material of sin*** in man — that is, the disease itself, or vitious concupiscence — that is (as Bonaventura rightly notes), a ***defect and inclination against the Law of God***. But the ***formal — or guilt*** — is remitted — that is, the being-ordered-to-damnation or to eternal punishments. This guilt is taken away when the person is received, and sin is not imputed.

**Another:**
> ***The Holy Spirit is abundantly poured out upon us. Therefore He takes away every sin.***

**Response.** It is true that every sin is taken away, as regards remission. For of the remission of sins Augustine also speaks when he says: ***"The baptised lacks every sin, but does not lack every evil."*** As regards regeneration, however, the Holy Spirit in us vanquishes sin, and begins the new righteousness, and afterwards, regeneration consummated, takes away every sin — but in the meantime struggles with sin, as Paul attests Rom. 8: ***"We have the first-fruits of the Spirit. The Spirit aideth our infirmity."***¹²⁰ʰ

The same is the response to an argument almost similar: ***"Grace superabounds over sin. Therefore it takes away every sin, so that in the saints sin is not at all present."*** Response: Paul speaks of the ***remission*** of sins which is made gratis on account of the Son. And because he says grace superabounds over sin, certainly he confesses that sin is present and is in nature — but that grace is victrix, and far more powerful than sin. This is the supreme consolation of minds terrified by the sense of sin and of God's wrath. For thus the pious struggle out of death and hell, and learn that grace is more potent than sin doubly — by ***imputation*** and ***effect***. For God reputes just those believing in the Son, even if there yet adhere in them the remnants of sin; and the Son of God vivifies hearts, and by His holy Spirit takes away sorrows, restores joy of life to the heart — according to that: ***"Justified by faith, we have peace with God."*** Item: He excites good motions and represses sin; finally He raises the dead to eternal life.

**Another:**
> ***Where there is sin, there is no grace. If in the saints there is sin, the saints are excluded from grace. For grace and sin cannot be together.***

**Response:** Where there is sin — namely ***reigning***, and against conscience — there is no grace. For the Holy Spirit and grace are shaken off, and faith by which we are justified is lost, when sins against conscience are admitted, as Paul says: ***"If you shall live according to the flesh, you shall die."*** But in the reborn there is a disease which is called sin — which disease is not idle, and at the same time the Holy Spirit is present and represses sin — therefore Paul says: ***"If by the Spirit you shall mortify the actions of the flesh, you shall live."*** And in this manner Paul often joins together the old and new man: ***"The flesh lusteth against the Spirit, the Spirit against the flesh."*** For which cause some respond that ***sin and grace are not in the same excellent degrees at once in the same man, but in remiss degrees the disease which is sin and the grace of God are at once***. We add also this response, and merit turns the argument: ***where there is sin, there is grace*** — provided sin is to be remitted — nor can there be grace where there is no sin, just as there cannot be healing or medicine where there is no disease, nor can there be remission where there is no debt.

Eccius confesses that he does not understand the truth of this doctrine. Thus he barbarously enough says: ***"I confess the smallness of my ingenuity is not able to grasp that the same one is friend of God through grace, and enemy of God through fault."*** How ***truly*** this is said, need not be said — provided the term ***fault*** be rightly received, which is ambiguous, and sometimes signifies the ***disease itself, original sin and inhering***, sometimes it signifies ***guilt or imputation***, by which one is guilty — as when Augustine says: ***"The fomes after baptism is no longer fault"*** — that is, is not guilt. For the whole fault is remitted when through baptism man is received by God into grace — and therefore grace superabounds, which buries the whole of sin by condonation.

**Other objections and responses** — Eccius adduces the argument from ***"God hates nothing in the reborn (Rom. 8), therefore no sin is in the reborn"*** (response: God simply hates sin even in the reborn, but remits and takes away the guilt on account of the Son); and ***"Nothing hinders believers from entrance to heaven"*** (response: the merit of Christ's innocence removes all obstacle); and ***"Whoever puts off the old man and puts on the new is pure and innocent"*** (response: this speaks of consummated purity, valid only in the past-tense once regeneration is completed); and James's ***"Concupiscence begets sin"*** (Eccius argues from this that concupiscence is not sin — a very ridiculous argument, since if concupiscence begets sin as cause, root, and springhead of all other sins, how can it not be sin? Can an evil tree be good? — ***"Bad crow, bad egg"***); and ***"Sin is voluntary; original stain is not voluntary; therefore [it is not sin]"*** (response: the major is of civil errors and delicts; the minor concerning saints acknowledging their uncleanness is true — but Augustine's solution is true, that original sin is ***voluntary***, because we are delighted with it).

And ***"No commandment ought to be impossible; in the Law it is prescribed 'Thou shalt not covet'; therefore concupiscence can be avoided"*** — response: the major is of political commands, not of the divine Law, which cannot be satisfied in this infirmity of nature (although as to external works it is possible; but as to internal works, interior cleanness, obedience, and satisfaction it is impossible — by the vice of our depraved nature, which vice is shown to us in the Law — which would be wholly possible if nature were whole, as below of the Law shall be said). And ***"If you wish to enter into life, keep the commandments — but no one can keep 'Thou shalt not covet' understood of the disease of nature; therefore no one can be saved"*** — I concede the whole, insofar as our works and merits are considered. For no one is saved by works of the Law or by his own merits, according to the sentence of the Law: ***"Cursed be every one who does not remain..."*** Item: ***"All have turned aside."*** ***"There is one salvation — to hope in Thee and to have salvation, O Christ our God."***

**Now the chief arguments of the adversaries I have wished to note, that we may see on what foundations they lean, and how blind, pertinacious, and rash are the adversaries who dare to construct and superbuild so great a mass of errors upon this their assertion — that original sin after baptism is no longer sin — namely: that man can satisfy the Law of God, and be just before God, if he does what is in him, and has free arbiter of accomplishing and executing all things which God commanded.** But these being now laid aside, and the truth obtained from the condition of our nature, from the sacred letters, from the testimonies of the whole true Church of God, from the feeling and complaint of all pious [men] who ever were, are, or shall be — finally from death itself (which is the wages of sin) not only in adults, but also in infants even baptised — let us return to what remains to be said of sin.

### §7. What is Actual Sin? And of the Sin against the Holy Spirit

**Actual sin is the ***fruit of hereditary or original sin*** — that is, thought, word, deed, or omission fighting with the Law of God — in the mind, in the will and heart, and in the external members — so that man on account of it is guilty of the wrath of God and of temporal and eternal punishments — unless through and on account of the Son of God remission be made.** For thus it is written **Deut. 27:** ***"Cursed is every one who does not remain in all which are written in the book of the Law."***

**How many kinds of actual sin are there?** — Many differences are recited — taken not from the ***nature*** of sin (which is always the same) but from a diverse ***respect and consideration of sin.*** For of sins one is ***mortal***, another ***venial***. ***Mortal*** are all sins by their nature — interior and exterior, of origin and other — insofar as they are not remitted by God on account of the Son the Mediator. In this way all the sins of all men are mortal — that is, by their nature bring the merit of death. They are made ***venial*** when out of the sheer grace and goodness of God they are remitted on account of the Son to those doing penance — according to Ps. 32: ***"Blessed are they whose sins are covered."***

To these are subjoined other divisions:

- ***Sins of commission*** are called those where we do what we ought not to do.
- ***Sins of omission*** — when we omit doing, thinking, or saying what ought to be done, thought, or said.
- ***Sins of infirmity*** — which often arise from the weakness of our nature: fastidiousness in pious matters, sudden wrathfulness, desire of illicit things.
- ***Sins of ignorance*** not affected but probable — which are done from ignorance either of a thing one's own or another's — as if someone does not know those things which are of positive law (not natural, not moral, or divine — but of ceremonies, judgements, custom, morals). Of these Leviticus prescribes, and the punishments of those sinning through ignorance are mitigated.
- ***Sins of malice*** — which are done against conscience, of which Paul speaks Gal. 5.
- ***Voluntary sins*** are all civil and external delicts against laws known to us, which we do knowing and willing.
- ***Involuntary*** — those which the pious resist and dominate.
- ***Reigning*** or ***dominant sin*** — that which not doing penance is not remitted, and which still dominates in the unconverted and unregenerate man.
- ***Serving sin*** — which the pious repugn — is every internal evil remaining in the reborn, a certain darkness and depravity born with us, vitious affections, and various motions, manifold errors, yet not against conscience — which the pious resist, as Paul commands Rom. 8: ***"If you shall live according to the flesh, you shall die; if by the Spirit you shall mortify the actions of the flesh, you shall live."***
- ***Sins against conscience*** are all reigning sins which generate horrible effects and accumulate crimes — [and] they are and remain mortal so long as they dominate.

#### The Sin against the Holy Spirit

**Sin against the Holy Spirit is the ***voluntary renunciation and rebellion against acknowledged truth, born from hatred of truth, and final impenitence and desperation, repudiating the grace of God shown to us in Christ.*** It is also called ***blasphemy against the Spirit, irremissible sin, and sin unto death***, for which we are not to intercede.** ***1 John 5. Matt. 12.*** But of these things now enough.¹²⁰ⁱ

### §8. The Use of the Doctrine of Sin

*(Implicit in the pastoral-practical thrust of the entire Locus.)* The **use of the doctrine of sin** is fivefold, following the paradigm ***Melanchthonian Loci-methodology*** which Selnecker inherits from his *praeceptor*:

**1. Humiliation and self-knowledge** — that we may acknowledge our misery, corruption, and inability, and not be inflated with pride or presumption of righteousness by works.

**2. Contrition and salutary fear** — that our hearts may be terrified by the wrath of God against sin, and be crushed under the hammer of the Law.

**3. Faith in Christ's satisfaction** — that we may flee from the terror of sin to Christ alone, who ***bore our sins in His body upon the tree*** and by His obedience and passion has fulfilled the Law and made propitiation for us.

**4. Gratitude to God** for the deliverance shown through the Mediator, and for the daily remission and preservation.

**5. New obedience and sanctification** — that we may resist the reigning of sin, mortify the works of the flesh through the Spirit, and grow in the fruits of righteousness — awaiting the day when the body of sin shall be wholly destroyed, and we shall live in eternity without sin.¹²⁰ʲ
