# Locus VII

## De Lege Dei — On the Law of God¹²¹

*(pp. 385–420 of the imprint.)*

**Whenever concerning the Law of God, or the Decalogue, it is spoken in the Church, always ***two kinds of men considering the Law*** occur** — (concerning the secure, insolent, and impenitent — who care not at all for the Law of God — is not here spoken). **The one is the ***Pharisaic and hypocritical,*** namely of those who opine that the Law can be satisfied ***by human powers,*** and that through this satisfaction or fulfilment of the Law, ***grace of God, remission of sins, and eternal life*** can be obtained. **The other is the ***Christian or Evangelical,*** namely of those who ***rightly consider the Law,*** and scrutinize the force of the precepts, and acknowledge and confess ***their own infirmity,*** and take refuge only in Christ, and are received into grace of God through Him, and begin new obedience pleasing to God.

**Matt. 5**: The Son of God ***utterly excludes the first kind of men from entrance into the Kingdom of Heaven,*** saying: ***"Except your righteousness exceed the righteousness of the Scribes and Pharisees, you cannot enter into the Kingdom of Heaven."*** And Luke 7 sharply reprehends ***Simon the Pharisee*** — inflated with the opinion of ***legal righteousness*** and despising the woman's humility and tears.

For this kind of men beholds the Law only as an external mask, and rests on some external ***obedience-of-works-of-Law,*** and adds to it certain feigned ***παρέργα*** — which he calls ***works of supererogation and of perfection*** (as the Monks feigned) — and thinks this to be true righteousness — which righteousness (if it may so be named) is nevertheless full of ***pride,*** and ***accuses God of lying*** in shutting up all under sin, and within is full of evil affections, and despises the neighbour, and holds cheap the benefits of Christ, and postpones the works commanded by God to fictitious works, and ascribes to the ***opus operatum*** a peculiar virtue of obtaining grace of God.¹²²

For which cause God has always rejected this kind of men, as: ***"In vain do they worship me with the commandments of men."*** Ps. 50: ***"I will not accept a bullock from thy house, nor he-goats from thy folds."*** Isa. 1: ***"To what purpose is the multitude of your victims? Your sacrifices, and feasts, and solemnities, and ceremonies are an abomination and a weariness to me."*** Jer. 6: ***"Your holocausts are not acceptable to me."*** Amos 5: ***"I am angry against your Sabbaths, and I am not delighted with your sacrifices."*** Item, Ps. 16: ***"I will not offer their libations of blood, nor take up their names upon my lips."*** And Ps. 40: ***"Sacrifice and offering Thou didst not desire."***

**All these things God says** — not because He did not command obedience, sacrifices, and worship — but because He does not wish to admit that ***light opinion,*** that ***the divine Law can be satisfied by external human works,*** and that ***other works besides pleasing to God can be added,*** and that ***grace of God and remission of sins can be obtained by such external obedience of whatever kind*** — even without the merit of the Son being present — which merit alone, if removed, then ***nothing is, nothing avails*** the sanctity of the whole world.

### §2. Luke 18 — The Pharisee in the Temple

**Luke 18** stands the example of such a Pharisee, who going up into the temple ***glories not only in his own good works, fasting and tithes, but also disparages his neighbour, the publican*** — and clings only to external works, or to the veil of the Law, and does not consider the spiritual sense which the Law requires — and lacks true faith — inflated with philautia (self-love) and pride — and thus accuses God of injustice, mendacity, and negligence, and offers to Him the external mask of works as if a most precious treasure, and admires and preaches himself and his own, and contemns his neighbour — and thus violates both the first and the second Table, and further, destitute of all faith, tramples upon the Gospel doctrine — which all hypocrites and ***iustitiarii*** (self-justifiers) or workers, who trust in their merits before God, are wont to do.

There is lacking to this kind of men both the acknowledgement of self, and faith in Christ, which ought to be the fount of good works.

**The other kind of men, therefore, is approved by God** — namely those who, before they establish anything of their righteousness before God, consider these ***four things***:

1. What the Law of God is.
2. Whether anyone of men can satisfy the Law of God.
3. In what respect the Law has been abrogated.
4. What are the uses of the Law.

These four things considered, they distinguish their righteousness not only from that ***Pharisaic*** kind of men — of which now it has been said — but also from ***legal righteousness***.¹²³ᵃ

### §3. What is the Law of God?

**The Law of God** (which vulgarly is named ***moral***, by a slender and thin term) **is the ***eternal and immoveable wisdom in God***, which in creation was transfused into rational minds, and afterwards on Mount Sinai was promulgated by the voice of God with great majesty and confirmed by miracles — discerning just from unjust things, prescribing what sort we ought to be, and what to do or omit — requiring from every man ***perfect, pure, perpetual, and sincere obedience toward God***, and congruence with the will of God as it is expressed in the Word itself — promising life and salvation to those obeying, and damning and punishing in this life and after this life with eternal punishments all those in whom there is not integral congruence, and who do not render perfect obedience — unless remission be made through and on account of the Son of God the Mediator.**

**In this definition ***five members*** are for us to be considered.** First, the ***author of the Law***. Second, ***the transfusion of the Law into rational minds***, and its promulgation. Third, the ***quality of obedience***. Fourth, ***the rewards of the obedient, and the punishments of the disobedient***. Fifth, ***the correction and [restriction] — that is, the condonation on account of Christ.***

**First** — of the ***Author of the Law***, namely God — it must be known that He has the ***right of commanding and mandating*** because He is our ***Lord and God***; and that He has the ***power of affecting the obedient with rewards, and of punishing the evil*** because He is ***omnipotent***; further, that we are obligated to Him as Creator and Lord to render obedience in all things which He commands; and that no one of us can escape punishment if we do not obey Him, because He is the ***just Judge***, and everywhere hears, sees, and judges all things.

**Secondly**, the Law of God is the ***eternal wisdom of God***, then ***immoveable***, then it was implanted into human minds in creation, repeated on Mount Sinai with immense majesty and many miracles, and finally is the ***just and perfect rule of living***. For the Law did not first begin to be when the Decalogue was promulgated — namely in the year of the world 2454 [before 3080 years] — but the Law is the ***eternal and immutable wisdom of God***, and is considered in God as in the archetype, who says: ***"I am God, and I am not changed."*** It is also ***immoveable***: for as chastity is always a virtue pleasing to God, so adultery is always a flagitious thing which God detests, and according to the Law is immutably damned — because God is always angered by sin, and does not remit it without punishment.

**How, however, the divine justice is satisfied, and the wrath placated, and punishment transferred into the Son, and sins remitted to those believing in the Son — the doctrine of the Gospel shows.** What pertains to man, who is the image of God — indeed the knowledge of the Law began at creation, but yet the Law itself in God as in the archetype was, is, and shall be in all eternity. It was transfused into man immediately at creation, whence are the ***ordered notions*** in nature — which attest that nature did not flow together by chance, but that God is; and since they discern right from not-right, they show what sort God is, and confirmed by experience of rewards and punishments, they demonstrate the ***presence of God in the human race***. But the knowledge of the Law in man — obscured and manifoldly defiled — began again to be renewed on ***Mount Sinai*** (which name signifies ***a place of pines***), promulgated in Arabia Petraea, amid thunders and lightnings, with the sounding of trumpets and the trembling of the people, and inscribed on stone tablets by the finger of God — that the ***nature and office of the Law*** might be shown, and the ***wrath of God against the disobedient***, [and] the ***eternal duration of the Law***, because as God is just and wise, so He wills His Law to be a just and perfect rule of the life of men.¹²³ᵇ

**Thirdly**, the ***quality of obedience*** can be gathered from this: that God requires ***perfect, pure, perpetual, and sincere obedience***.

- ***Perfect*** — that satisfies fully all and each of the precepts, according to the exact rule of the Law, both in interior cult of mind and will, and in exterior cult.
- ***Pure*** — that in no part is contaminated, and that the heart of man is pure as a pure fountain and spring, whence [obedience] flows.
- ***Perpetual*** — that is not temporary.
- ***Sincere*** — that is not disguised with evil opinions.

**Fourthly**, the Law promises ***eternal goods to the obedient***, and threatens ***punishments to the contumacious*** without exception (as regards itself), according to that: ***"Cursed is every one..."***

**Fifthly**, the ***restriction*** must also be sought from the Gospel, which shows that Christ came into the world that He might deliver believers from the malediction of the Law — and that He did not dissolve the Law but ***fulfilled*** it — which He did in ***four ways***:

1. ***By perfectly obeying*** the Law of God.
2. ***By deriving the punishment upon Himself*** — that we might be made the righteousness of God in Him.
3. ***By giving the Holy Spirit***, that we might be able to begin obedience.
4. ***By transforming us fully to His similitude in eternal life*** — or by perfecting and consummating — according to that of Paul: ***"We by the Spirit through faith expect the hope of righteousness."***¹²³ᶜ

### §4. Whether anyone can satisfy the Law? — De Secundo, an quisquam Legi possit satisfacere

**I respond: No one except Christ alone fulfils the Law by pure, perfect, perpetual, and sincere obedience.** And usefully are five reasons — or rather demonstrations — proposed for Christians to consider.

**The First is taken from the corruption of nature itself**, of which it is written **Job 14:** ***"Who can make clean of an unclean thing conceived from seed?"*** Item, **Ps. 51:** ***"In sins my mother conceived me."*** For a foul fountain does not produce limpid water, nor does a rotten tree bring forth wholesome fruits.

**The Second reason is this**: since a little earlier it has been said what is the quality of obedience toward the Law — namely, that obedience be ***perfect, pure, perpetual, and sincere*** — there now accedes the ***examination of the universal experience or sense of each one***, and manifestly pronounces, confesses, and concludes that ***man's obedience is not perfect but imperfect; is not pure but born from an impure heart; is not perpetual but momentary; is not sincere but disguised with evil opinions and hindered in many ways.*** Experience itself, therefore, convinces all to confess that there is no one of men who can satisfy the Law of God.

**The Third reason is the ***testimony of Scripture***** — as: ***"There is none just, not even one, there is none who understands, there is none who seeks God."*** ***"If Thou wilt observe iniquities, O Lord — Lord, who shall stand?"*** Item, ***"Every man a liar,"*** etc.

**The Fourth Demonstration is most evident** — namely the ***victim of the Son of God***. For if the Law of God could have been satisfied by anyone, the Son of God would have died utterly in vain, nor would anyone — if he could fulfil the Law of God — need the righteousness of Christ. Gal. 2: ***"He who shall have done these things, shall live in them."***

**The Fifth reason is this**: God afflicts and punishes no one unless he be guilty. But all and each of men are afflicted and punished with diseases and death. Therefore it is established that ***all and each are guilty.***¹²³ᵈ

**Here a question occurs**: ***Why did God give an impossible Law?*** **I respond:** God brought forth the Law ***just***, because He is just; ***perfect***, because He is perfect; ***pure***, because He is pure; ***perpetual***, because He is eternal; ***sincere***, because He is open, and detests every disguise. He also handed down and implanted a Law ***possible for whole and uncontaminated nature*** — so that even the ***judgement of reason*** remaining in human nature says that ***the Law is good, just, and right, and only the malice of man is to be accused.*** Then God, by prescribing things impossible, ***shows what sort we are***, and ***leads us to humility***, and proposes the Son to us to be kissed — on account of whom we are received, through whom the things which are impossible are made ***possible*** to us — nay, ***easy*** — so that the commands of God are not grievous. Hence it is said: ***"The Law is a schoolmaster to Christ."***

### §5. In what respect the Law is abrogated — De Tertio, Quatenus lex sit abrogata

**Here we must hold the customary division. Of the Mosaic Law, one is ***Ceremonial***, another ***Judicial*** or ***Forensic***, another ***Moral***.**

**Ceremonial** — comprising ecclesiastical rites, ceremonies, sacrifices, and types of the victim of the Son of God — has been wholly abolished together with the Aaronic priesthood ***as to its special use***, but not as to its ***genus and signification***. For always in the Church there are certain rites and ceremonies conformable to the Word, that all things may be done [***εὐσχημόνως καὶ κατὰ τάξιν***]; and the things which the Mosaic ceremonies figured are ***eternal***.

**Judicial Law** — since the Mosaic republic or polity ceased — has also ceased ***as to its species***. Insofar however as some part of the judicial Law is of ***natural right*** (as is the law against incestuous marriages), or is proposed by the magistrate for the ***political end***, we ought altogether to use it. The signification also of judicial laws is ***perpetual***, since the polity of Moses was a type of the reign of Christ, which is eternal.

**As to the ***Moral Law*** — two things are to be considered in it: namely, the ***rule of life or the doctrine itself***, and then the ***appendix of the Law***.**

The ***rule*** is ***eternal and immoveable***, and is never abrogated — therefore Christ says: ***"I came not to dissolve the Law."*** The ***appendix*** comprehends the ***promises*** and ***threatenings***. The threatenings for believers have been abolished, since ***we are not under Law but under grace***. And ***"for the just no Law is set."*** The promises are conserved in Christ, who is the consummation of the Law.

**Also it is commonly said thus:** ***The moral Law is abrogated as to justification and as to condemnation.*** For ***"by the Law no one is justified,"*** and ***"there is no condemnation to those who are in Christ Jesus."***

**Concerning the saying ***"For the just no Law is set,"*** doctors rightly dispute that it must be understood of the ***truly just*** — so that the interpretation is this: to the just, ***insofar as he is just***, no Law is set, because the Law does not accuse or damn the just.** Some also spoke of ***coercion*** — that believers are not coerced, but since they are ruled by the Holy Spirit, willingly render spontaneous obedience, which nevertheless is voluntary and free obedience necessary by ***necessity of debt***. We, following the sentence of our teachers concerning malediction, receive this saying: ***"For the just no Law is set"*** — that is, the ***just by faith in Christ, the Law does not damn, does not oppress.***

Item: ***"The Law and the Prophets [were] until John."*** This saying is true of the ceremonial and judicial laws. Of the moral Law also, insofar as it pertains to condemnation, it must be received. For as soon as the finger of John points to Christ — ***"Behold the Lamb of God who taketh away the sins of the world"*** — who from the beginning was slain — immediately all accusation and rigor of the Law ceases.¹²³ᵉ

### §6. Of the Triple Use of the Law — De Quarto, id est, De Triplici Legis Usu

**Threefold is the use of the Law: External, Internal, and Spiritual.**

**The ***External*** or ***pedagogical*** or ***political*** [use]** pertains to all men — even the unregenerate — and consists in ***external discipline***, which is nothing other than the ***locomotive governance*** ruling the external members and external morals, that external works congruent with the Decalogue may be done, and that all external delicts fighting with the Law of God may be avoided. This use is common with civil and human laws, and is named ***external righteousness, philosophic virtue, and discipline of the Law*** — necessary on account of ***four causes***, of which:

- **First** is the ***command of God***.
- **Second**, that ***punishments*** may be avoided which by divine order follow delicts.
- **Third** is ***tranquillity and public and private peace***.
- **Fourth**, that men may be able to be instructed in the ***doctrine of the Gospel***. For those who indulge in external delicts are not converted to God, nor are they sanctified by Christ through the voice of the Gospel and the Holy Spirit. Therefore Paul says Gal. 3: ***"The Law is a schoolmaster unto Christ."***

**The ***Second*** — the ***Internal*** use of the Law** — pertains to the ***old man***, and reveals the ***wrath of God***, shows sin, and excites contrition in conscience — as Rom. 3 it is written: ***"Through the Law is the acknowledgement of sin."***

**The ***Third*** use is ***Spiritual***, and pertains to the ***reborn***** — or to the ***new man*** — for whom the Law is the ***perpetual rule of living***, according to which the reborn man brings forth the fruits of his faith, and begins new obedience — which, although imperfect, he knows to please God on account of Christ — and thus walks ***not according to the flesh, but according to the Spirit***. And of this use Paul speaks saying: ***"Through faith the Law is established"*** — that is, ***by faith obedience is begun, and pleases***. Whence Ambrose says: ***"These are not just who hear the Law, but who believe in Christ, whom the Law promised: these do the Law."***¹²³ᶠ

### §7. Distinction between Pharisaic and Christian Righteousness

**These things considered in order, now the distinction between ***Pharisaic and Christian righteousness*** can be gathered.**

**The Pharisee and hypocrite** sees only the letter of the Law, and does not look into the inner sanctuary of the Law — therefore he thinks it enough to rule the external morals, and further to invent external works, and to observe human traditions — by which he thinks himself to be just.

**The Christian, however, considers the ***spirit of the Law***** — according to the sentence: ***"Thou shalt love the Lord thy God from thy whole heart, from thy whole soul, from all thy strength and all thy forces, and thy neighbour as thyself."*** Not therefore the external mask only of the Law, but the ***true sense of the Law*** do the Christians consider — as Christ says Matt. 15: ***"From the heart come forth evil thoughts and sins."*** And ***"the will is the origin of the deed,"*** says Tertullian. Acknowledging their own sins and the immense weakness and uncleanness of human nature, and the horrid wrath of God against these evils, they truly begin to grieve, humbly submit themselves, suppliantly pray: ***"Lord, enter not into judgement with Thy servant, for none living shall be justified in Thy sight."*** ***"If Thou wilt observe iniquities, O Lord — Lord, who shall stand?"*** ***"In iniquities I was conceived, and in sins my mother conceived me."*** ***"I see another law in my members, repugning the law of my mind."*** ***"Not what I will, do I do the good, but what I will not, the evil, this I do. To will is present with me, but to perfect I find not. O wretched man that I am, who shall deliver me from the body obnoxious to death? Thine, O Lord, is righteousness — but ours, confusion of face. Against Thee, against Thee alone have I sinned, and evil in Thy sight have I done."***

**Then the Pharisee, swollen and inflated by the persuasion of his righteousness and holiness**, when adverse things and temptations arise, and death comes stealthily, and conscience is pressed — falls wholly, and has no efficacious consolation, and finds no remedy in his own works or in the works of other men — but ***with Cain and Judas he despairs.*** The Christian, however, does not add ***desperation*** to his sins, but knows ***the mercy of God to be greater than the sins of all men***, and by faith obtains the ***remission of sins*** through and on account of the Son the Mediator, according to Ps. 130 (***"Out of the depths..."***): ***"With the Lord there is copious redemption."*** As of a certain Canon Doctor it is written, who in the Papacy, when he was about to expire the soul, had said: ***"Ah, what shall I do? No works help me, none console, none raise up. This I shall do — since nothing is present on which I could lean: 'Have mercy on me, O God, on account of Jesus Christ Thy Son, and receive my spirit.'"***

Afterwards the ***Pharisee is proud***, glories before God, contemns his neighbour, ascribes praise to himself. But the ***Christian, just on account of Christ, begins new obedience***, and asks to be raised up and aided in his infirmity by the Holy Spirit.

**As to Christian righteousness — since it is imputed to the believer ***without works of the Law and merit of his own obedience*** — it can easily be distinguished from ***legal righteousness***, which is from works of the Law, and is not imputed gratis but is [the reward] of the one working, and whose form is ***just action and one's own obedience***. But of Christian righteousness in the locus on Justification is to be spoken.**¹²³ᵍ

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

# Locus VIII

## De Calamitatibus et Cruce — On Calamities and the Cross¹²⁴

*(pp. 396–412 of the imprint.)*

**The doctrine of the cross of Christians can most briefly be included in these members:**

**I.** What the term ***cross*** signifies among Christians.
**II.** What the cross of Christians is.
**III.** How manifold it is.
**IV.** Whence it arises that the cross of the pious is so manifold.
**V.** What the difference is between the cross of the pious and the calamities of the impious.
**VI.** Why God wills His own always to be subjected to the cross.
**VII.** What is the chief consolation of the pious in the cross.

### §1. What the term Cross signifies among Christians

**First, the word ***cross*** among Christians has its name from the ***wood or cross of Christ,*** on which Christ the Son of God hung, suffered, and died.** For ***cross*** signifies a ***gibbet and gallows***, to which formerly were affixed the wicked and most flagitious of all — as Cicero alleges as an abominable crime, that Verres had lifted up Roman citizens to the cross; and as the old verse attests: ***"Crimes shameful are to be condemned by the cross."*** And thence proverbs are born: [***"To the crows"***], ***in malam crucem***, and similar.

**This etymology, taken from the form, is therefore diligently to be observed**, that from it we may say we acknowledge:

1. The ***foulness of our sin.***
2. The ***satisfaction*** offered to us in the cross.
3. The ***example*** proposed to us for imitation.
4. The ***consolation*** in every cross and calamity.

**The foulness of our sin was so great,** that it could not be washed away by any other than the greatest and most shameful kind of punishment. Whence it is written: ***"Cursed is he who hangs on wood"*** (Deut. 21). And to this looks Paul in Gal. 3, saying: ***"Christ redeemed us from the malediction of the Law, being made a malediction for us."*** And 2 Cor. 5: ***"God made Him who knew no sin, sin for us, that we might be made the righteousness of God in Him."*** And Christ Himself in Ps. 22 names Himself a ***worm to be trodden — Tholaaz, small and to be transfixed*** — exclaiming: ***"A worm am I, and no man, a reproach of men and the outcast of the plebs."*** And Ps. 31: ***"I am become as a vessel destroyed"*** — or a vessel to be taken away — which elsewhere is named ***vessel of wrath.*** Item, Ps. 69: ***"Confusion has covered my face. Thou knowest my reproach"*** (that is, every evil by which I am overwhelmed — as are sin, death, hell, devil), ***my confusion*** (internal passions), ***and my ignominy*** (external afflictions, calumnies, mockeries, wounds, scourges, cross, and death). And in **Matt. 24** for the same reason Christ seems to name Himself a ***carcass***, to which yet the eagles gather. And Isaiah 53 calls Christ the ***last of men, the most contemned and despised.*** All these things make for deploring the ***atrocity of our sin***, which by no other punishment except that which was the most ignominious of all — both among the Jews and among the Gentiles — could be restrained and taken away.¹²⁴ᵃ

**Then, the ***satisfaction*** for our sins made on the wood of the cross must be considered from the etymology of the word *cross*.** For the cross of Christ comprehends internal and external ***torments***, by which Christ, the just for the unjust, satisfied for our sins — as 1 Peter 2 it is written: ***"He Himself bore our sins, being made a victim, in His body upon the wood, that dead to sins, we might live to righteousness."*** Whence also the Church sings: ***"Life dies on the wood. Faithful cross, alone among all noble trees — no forest brings forth such a one in leaf, flower, and germ."*** Item, ***"Behold, man! in the horrible cross is our salvation."*** Item:

> ***"Now life is restored to the dead,***
> ***the door opened to the shut in.***
> ***The crime which Adam lost***
> ***Christ has recalled for expiation.***
> ***And against us what was issued —***
> ***this handwriting is here erased."***

**Then let us propose to ourselves the ***example*** by which we imitate the footsteps of Christ, and submit ourselves to the cross, that we may become conformed to the image of Christ** — as Christ Himself says: ***"He who wishes to follow me, let him take up his cross, and follow me."***

**Finally, the ***consolation*** placed for us in the very word *cross* is not to be neglected** — which is that we should ***impose all our cross upon the cross of Christ*** — since otherwise it is impossible that we should be sufficient for any cross, unless we flee in our cross to the cross of Christ. As often as we bring our cross to the cross of Christ, we can be ***courageous and strong, patient and constant*** — as the examples of the Apostles and Martyrs and of all the pious in all ages attest. Whence in our own verses it is wont to be said:

> ***"Whoever joins his wounds to the wounds of Christ,***
> ***he can be stronger than all evils."***

### §2. What the Cross of Christians is

**Secondly, what the cross of Christians is, from these things which have been said can easily be understood — namely, ***every adversity, whether internal (of mind and conscience) or external (of body, reputation, and things), whether spiritual or corporeal, whether just or unjust***, by which the Christian is pressed, disturbed, and afflicted.**

### §3. How Manifold is the Cross of the Pious

**Thirdly, the cross of the pious is manifold. First of all it holds the place of the ***cross of the Son of God***. Then follow ***three calamities of Christians*** — namely, [***κόλασις***] (chastisement), [***πειρασμός***] (trial), and [***μαρτύριον***] (martyrdom).**

**[Kolasis] is any punishment which is inflicted on Christians on account of sins.** For since all saints in this life bear about with them the remnants of manifold sins — indulging in which and slackening the reins, they offend God — it happens that they are often exercised by many troubles and miseries, diseases, poverty, and other adversities of that kind. And here belong also the sufferings to which the wicked are drawn — who nevertheless are converted to God — as ***David*** after adultery and homicide committed is converted to God, item, ***Manasseh, Zacchaeus, Mary Magdalene, the thief on the cross***, and others.

**[Peirasmos] is an exercise of proving and confirming in faith, invocation, hope, and patience.** For God proves His own — not that He Himself needs some more certain knowledge of us, but that He may lead us to acknowledgement of ourselves, that we may see what sort we are, how miserable and languid, whether in true faith we have advanced. For in the cross the ***weakness of our nature*** is laid bare — as in Job a manifest example shows, who, when for some time he had used admirable and incredible patience, at last, conquered by grief, falls — so that he damns and execrates the day of his rising with a horrible imprecation — which he could by no means have persuaded himself of concerning himself, if the cross tempted had not taught him what evil lay hidden in his nature.

**God, however, most often proves His own in these modes:** first, as regards ***doctrine***, when various corruption, heresies, and schisms arise, God proves us and our faith, confession, and constancy — whether we are certain, whether faithful, or truly uncertain, wandering, pendulous, volatile, neutral — as Paul 1 Cor. 11 says: ***"Heresies must be among you, that the conspicuous be manifest among you."*** And Moses Deut. 13 says one is not to obey new prophets and dreamers who hand down novelties, ***because through them the Lord proves and tempts His people***, that it may stand [manifest] whether they love God from the heart, and faithfully hear the voice of God. Then God proves His own in life, in vocation, and in their own works — as Jacob is proved by losing his son Joseph. Joseph in prison. Job in all things. Abraham commanded to immolate his son Isaac. Lazarus in his whole life. And in the same way all the saints in all ages are proved and exercised by various afflictions — from which finally they themselves usurp this Davidic voice: ***"It is good for me, O Lord, that Thou hast humbled me, that I might learn Thy justifications."***

**[Martyrion] is ***testimony***, which all saints — with hazard of reputation, name, glory, things, and life — bear to acknowledged truth and to the doctrine of the Son of God, our sole Redeemer, Justifier, Mediator, and Saviour — and to all articles of true faith, and in constant confession of the truth, fearing no indignation and rage of either devil or man, nor dreading any kind of punishment, if need be, they persevere.**¹²⁴ᵇ

### §4. Whence the Cross of the Pious Arises

**Fourthly, the cross sometimes arises ***from ourselves***, when we are afflicted on account of certain sins.** For ***"seven times a day even the just man falls,"*** says Solomon. There is need therefore of a ***rod admonishing of sin and of penance to be done*** — as Paul says: ***"We are chastised by the Lord, that with this world we be not damned."*** And in the 107th Psalm it is written: ***"On account of their flagitious deeds and iniquities, fools are punished by grave diseases."*** And Sirach says: ***"Whoever sins before God his Creator, falls into the hand of the physician."***

**Sometimes the cross arises ***from God immediately*** — not indeed from wrath and indignation, but from a divine and paternal will**, which in this life is not sufficiently perspicuous to us — as Christ shows John 9, when He says that ***neither the [blind man] himself, nor his parents, sinned or merited the punishment of blindness, but that he is blind for this reason, that in him the works of God may be conspicuous.*** For this cause we ought not easily to judge perversely of the afflicted, since we often see others afflicted by great evils and overwhelmed. For the cause is not always established for us, which is often known to God alone.

**Sometimes the cross arises ***from the Devil*** — plotting both against the life, morals, doctrine, and life of the pious — as a lion roaring, and a fox craftily going about.**

**Sometimes the cross arises ***from the members of Satan*** — that is, from tyrants, heretics, evil, pestiferous, and envious men, liars, calumniators, delators, plunderers, and the like.**¹²⁴ᶜ

### §5. The Distinction between the Cross of the Pious and the Calamities of the Impious

**Fifthly, the cross of the pious differs from the cross and calamities of the impious in ***four modes***.**

**First**, by reason of ***God Himself*** — who subjects the pious to the cross out of sheer love and paternal chastisement, by which He coerces them gently — as a Father a Son, and a preceptor a disciple — and keeps them in office. But the ***impious and impenitent*** God punishes out of just wrath — as the Lord attacks an iniquitous, contumacious, and perfidious servant with lashes, and drives him away.

**Then**, the cross of Christians ***turns to good for the pious***. But the calamities of the impious bring ***ruin and destruction of soul and body*** — unless they be converted.

**After**, the pious ***invoke God in their cross***, and hand and commit themselves wholly to divine grace, are patient, and expect redemption either in this life or perfect after this life. But the impious ***rage against God*** and depart in their impatience and despair.

**Finally**, the cross of the pious ***lasts only for a brief space of time*** — as Christ says: ***"A little while and you shall not see me."*** But the calamities of the impious are the ***worm biting them in eternity*** — of which bite the beginnings are felt in this life.

Of the cross of the pious David so speaks Ps. 30: ***"A moment [in] His wrath, and He delights in life; toward evening the weeping shall abide, toward morning joy."*** And Isa. 54 God says: ***"I have hid my face in a moment of wrath from thee, and I left thee for a little moment — but in great mercy I shall gather thee, and with eternal grace I shall have mercy on thee, saith Jehovah thy God."*** And 1 Peter 5: ***"God, who for a brief time have you suffered, shall perfect, corroborate, confirm, [and] found you."*** Of the punishments of the impious it is written Ps. 75: ***"A cup in the hand of the Lord, full of ruddy wine poured out — and He poureth from it, but all the impious shall drink, and shall suck the dregs."***¹²⁴ᵈ

### §6. The Six Causes Why God Subjects His Own to the Cross

**Sixthly, God subjects His own to the cross for six chief causes.**

**The First is that ***the pious may have perpetual exercises of faith, invocation, patience, humility, hope, and confession*** — as Paul says (Rom. 12): ***"You ought to hand over your bodies unto the cross for a sacrifice, that by proving you may experience what is the good, pleasing, and perfect will of God."***

**The Second cause is that ***the pious may learn what and how great is the wrath of God against sins — both those of the impious and of the pious.*** For God punishes sin in the whole human race — therefore also all men are subjected to the cross, but with a different end and respect, as a little before it has been said. For God ***chastises His own and mitigates the punishments*** — but the impious He ***punishes, and casts away and damns the unconverted.***

**The Third cause is that ***the pious may become conformed to the image of the Son of God.*** For as many as have been baptised, these have been baptised into the death of Christ (Rom. 6), that ***with Him suffering, with Him they may also be glorified; with Him dying, they may with Him also be quickened.***

**The Fourth cause is that ***the pious may experience in the cross the presence, help, and deliverance of God Himself*** — according to that: ***"Invoke me in the day of tribulation, and I will rescue thee."*** Hence it often happens that ***the pious in the midst of perils are more courageous than otherwise in life*** — as the Apostles rejoiced under scourges that on account of the name of Christ something was to be suffered by them (Acts 5). And the examples of the martyrs attest that ***the Holy Spirit is present in the hearts of the pious, and is efficacious, and renders and confirms the mind of the pious in the midst of perils, in the discrimen of life and of all things.***

**God, however, delivers His own from the miseries of this life in two ways.** For either He hears their prayers, and rescues them marvellously from every peril — as He often delivered ***Paul, Athanasius, in our time Luther***, and other pious men. Or He permits them to be detained in the very cross, and by His consolation raises them up, corroborates, confirms, and so instructs them, that they hold the very cross as nothing — but always with the sight of the will and grace of God, and the hope of eternal life, ***outweigh and surpass all miseries*** — as Job says: ***"Even if He should slay me, yet will I hope in Him."*** And David Ps. 73: ***"If I have Thee alone, I hold heaven and earth in no great esteem."*** Item Paul Gal. 6: ***"Far be it from me, that I should glory in another, save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, through whom the world is crucified to me and I to the world."***

**The Fifth cause is that ***the pious may be held with desire of the eternal fatherland***, and rejoice to be called forth and delivered at length from a hospitium full of tumults and troubles** — as Paul says: ***"I desire to be dissolved and to be with Christ."***

**The Sixth cause of the cross of the pious is that ***the cross may be a testimony of two greatest things — namely of true doctrine and of eternal life***.** For the pious, suffering both this and that, ***attest that the doctrine concerning God which they profess is true***, and also with their blood confirm it for the glory and praise of the name of God. They also attest that ***another life remains, and that a judgement is at hand*** — of which by the hope they persevere in the confession of true doctrine, according to the saying: ***"Blessed is he who shall have persevered unto the end."*** Item: ***"Be faithful unto death, and I will give thee the crown of life."***

**And although it often happens that fanatical men, idolaters, heretics, and magicians seem to be courageous in bearing punishments, and by no reasoning can be led away from their opinions**, nevertheless there is ***immense difference*** between the courage and constancy of the pious, and the pertinacity and obduration of the fanatics. For the pious ***confess the truth of doctrine comprehended in Prophetic and Apostolic writings***, and ***invoke God in the name of the Mediator***, and have ***peaceful conscience***. But the fanatics ***corrupt true doctrine in many articles***, often nourish and scatter absurd and blasphemous opinions against the Son of God, from which they can neither extricate themselves nor others — as ***Servetus, the Anabaptists,*** and others. Wherefore rightly it is said: ***"The cause makes the martyr, and not the martyrdom."*** Item: ***"Where there is not the truth of Christ, there is not the truth of martyrdom."***¹²⁴ᵉ

### §7. The Chief Consolation of the Pious in the Cross — Philosophic vs Evangelical Consolation

**Seventhly, just as in other articles of Christian doctrine it profits to hold the illustrious distinction of the Law and the Gospel, and to know how far human reason with its acumen can advance, and where it must necessarily stop** — so it conduces also in the article of doctrine on calamities or on the cross, to hold the distinction of the ***chief heads of consolation***, of which some the Law or Ethical and Philosophical doctrine proposes, but some are ***unknown to reason*** and must be sought from the doctrine of the Gospel.

**For it is established that not all calamities are of the same kind.** Some, as above has been said, are ***justly afflicted*** — as David, Manasseh, and others — whose cross is [***κόλασις***]. Some are ***unjustly punished*** — as the holy Martyrs. Some also are by God — as gold in the fire — ***examined and proved*** — whose afflictions are called [***πειρασμοί***], as Job. For these the heads of consolation which are handed down only by Philosophy — or which the Decalogue contains — are not useful; for [these consolations] console only those whose conscience is good, and who know themselves to be immune from the sins of which they are accused. But ***Christians ought to have in view other, greater heads*** — which also lift up an ***evil conscience***, and console. Such the ***voice of the Gospel alone contains***, which not only consoles the unjustly afflicted, but also succours those who are justly punished, and who are conscious of no good in themselves — as shall soon appear in the comparison of the heads which the Philosophers enumerate, and the heads which the voice of the Gospel proposes.¹²⁴ᶠ

#### The Four Philosophical Heads of Consolation

**The First head, by which the Philosophers are wont to console the unjustly afflicted, is ***necessity or immutability of perils*** — [saying] one bears troubles because they cannot be changed** — as Cicero consoles others who deplore the death of their friends: ***"Death cannot be avoided. Therefore we must not grieve for the departing of our friends."***

**The Second head of philosophical consolation and patience is ***the dignity of virtue*** — as Aristotle says [***"honestum praesidium est vitae"***], and Euripides [***"virtue always"***]. Which two sayings of Aristotle and Euripides we once included in these numbers:

> ***"Virtue is the safeguard of life,***
> ***nor is anything more true."***

Thus also that one in the Tragedy says: ***"Yet through the very troubles honour shines."*** And this head of philosophical consolation is the ***supreme*** [head], when in adverse things they moderate the sorrows of mind — they are not broken, nor are they moved — so that either they do against justice, or depart from that equability or moderation which is fitting in nature — but they themselves ***vanquish and break Fortune*** (as Cicero says from the Tragedy) as waves are broken against a rock. Hence are those voices of brave and great men:

> ***"Whatever shall be, must be overcome — every fortune must be borne."***

**And Virgil:**
> ***"Do not yield to evils, but go against them more boldly."***

**And that:**
> ***"Oppose brave breasts to adverse things."***

**The Third head of philosophical and legal consolation is ***good conscience***, which best can judge of things rightly or wrongly done, and which renders the minds of men truly peaceful** — as Nazianzen says: [***"nothing is more agreeable to man than good conscience"***]. Here belongs also that vulgar sentence: ***"It is something to be free from the crime of great evils."*** And thus Socrates in his Apology (which stands in Plato and Xenophon) says to Hermogenes: [***"Do you not know that up to this time I have conceded to no man that he might have gone forth from life better than me in virtue?"***].

**The Fourth head is the customary ***example***, when the afflicted bear troubles with a stronger mind, because they see even other great men unjustly punished.** Of this head it is customarily said: ***"Adversity is common."*** And that barbarous saying, though composed with an evil arrangement of words, yet ornamented with a good sentence: ***"It is a consolation for the miserable to have companions in punishments."*** And this head of consolation is also familiar to the Prophets and holy Martyrs — as the Scripture says: ***"I am no better than my fathers."*** And that: ***"The disciple is not greater than the Master,"*** etc.

To these heads there can not unfitly be added ***the utility of others*** — and ***the comparison of conveniences and inconveniences*** — which also Socrates has in view when he disputes concerning the difficulties of old age, and compares the conveniences which shall arise if one departs from the living, with the inconveniences which shall come to pass if he should live longer. (Selnecker then reproduces at length Socrates's discourse to Hermogenes from Xenophon.)

**Concerning these places the Philosophers deal seriously in their writings.** But for the Christian man it is not enough to know what consolations lighten the minds of the unjustly oppressed in some manner — but it is altogether necessary and useful ***to add the divine voice, which is proper to the Gospel, and which consoles even the justly afflicted***, whose conscience cannot be good — as were David, Manasseh, the thief on the cross, and others, who flee to the Mediator, and establish themselves as received into grace on account of Christ.¹²⁴ᵍ

#### The Four Evangelical Heads of Consolation

**Although the heavenly discourse does not have heads of consolation altogether discrepant from those which we have just recensed from the philosophical doctrine, yet it both rightly illustrates these very heads, and furthermore adds other places altogether unknown to reason, philosophy, and law — which places when known, the mind oppressed can be truly raised up and consoled. Of these places ***four are chief***.**

**First, it must certainly be established that we are not afflicted by ***chance*** or by ***matter thus stirring itself***** (as Democritus and the Epicureans madly taught), but that ***calamities happen by some counsel and will of God — God knowing and permitting*** — as the sayings on providence attest: ***"A hair shall not fall from your head, without the will of the Father."*** ***"A single sparrow shall not fall on earth without the will of the Father."*** And in the ***Canticle of Anna*** (1 Sam. 2): ***"The Lord mortifies and quickens, He leads to the inferi and leads back."*** And in the Threni [Lamentations] of Jeremiah ch. 3: ***"Who is this who has said, that these things be done, the Lord not commanding?"*** etc. Wherefore a great part of consolation is to establish adverse things to happen ***not by chance, but by the counsel, will, and permission of God.***

If you compare this place with the first place of philosophical consolation, you shall see the ***illustrious distinction of Gentile doctrine and heavenly doctrine.*** Philosophers name ***necessity*** in bearing troubles, when perils cannot be avoided — nor do they know whence calamities arise, but think they arise from chance or from the matter of nature. Christians, however, know that adverse things happen by the ***counsel and will of God***, as Paul to the Corinthians clearly attests, saying: ***"When we are judged by the Lord, we are corrected."*** Christians therefore call ***necessity*** — not such as human reason feigns, but such as ***obeys God***. For God commands us to obey Him, and to bear evils of every kind for the glory of God, nor to rush into more sins broken by sorrow — as Peter says: ***"Humble yourselves under the powerful hand of God."*** And in this way, for Christians, ***necessity is obedience to God itself.***

**Second head of Christian consolation.** Not only must it be established that adverse things come to pass by God's counsel and will, but also this must be added, that ***afflictions are not signs of wrath or of perdition, but are signs — nay, certain pledges — of God's mercy and grace toward us.*** Pericles [***κολαστικῶς***] knows he is punished divinely, but he cannot establish that he is punished ***not that he be reduced to nothing, but that he be recalled to penance***. Christians, however, ought to establish that ***calamities are testimonies and pledges of God's good will toward us, and are exercises of faith, fear, and other spiritual fruits in us*** — as Peter says: ***"You must be saddened by various temptations, that the proof of your faith may become purer than gold."*** And Jer. 31: ***"Thou hast chastised me, O Lord, and I was instructed, as an untamed young bullock; after Thou hast converted me, I have done penance."*** Item David says: ***"It is good for me that Thou hast humbled me, that I might learn Thy justifications."*** And here belong sentences which prohibit despair, and call to penance, and promise grace to those returning — as are: ***"I will not the death of the sinner, but that he be converted and live."*** ***"Come to me all who labour and are burdened, I will refresh you,"*** etc. We know therefore that God is with us according to the promise: ***"I will be with thee in tribulation."*** We know also that God has care of us, wills to preserve and deliver us, and that we enter into eternal life through the troubles and afflictions of this age — and that every affliction is momentary, but the consolation is perpetual.

**Third head of consolation.** Since now from what we have said it is manifest that afflictions do not happen by chance but by God's counsel — and that God requires from us obedience, and that afflictions are not to be borne with wrath — this must necessarily be added, that ***indignation or impatience is truly sin***, according to that: ***"Do not murmur."*** Item: ***"In tribulation patient."*** For although reason cries that it should go well for the just, yet since God wills that ***faith and invocation be exercised in us***, let us patiently bear troubles, let us not become indignant nor murmur against God (as Saul does), but let us know God requires obedience, and punishes the contumacious and disobedient — let us acknowledge ourselves, and the weakness of our nature — that we are not yet perfectly just, but daily we need ***purgation, and (as the Scripture says) sifting*** — as Paul says: ***"The body is mortified on account of sin"*** — namely, which still inheres in the flesh. And why the Church must be subjected to the cross, we have touched above. But this is the chief cause: because God wills that in the Church there be begun ***a new and eternal righteousness and life*** — He wills that this body obnoxious to sin be abolished, and the old man crucified together — that the body of sin may be destroyed, and in us may be begun eternal righteousness, a new and spiritual nature, in which the remnants of sin do not adhere, but which is ***without sin and without death***.

**Fourth head of consolation** must be added — which utterly takes away indignation and impatience. Not only must it be known that we are afflicted divinely, that afflictions are signs of mercy, that indignation is sin, that God requires obedience — but also this must be added, that ***in afflictions there ought to shine forth faith, hope of ultimate deliverance, and invocation.*** And this head especially declares the ***distinction of philosophical and Christian consolation and patience.*** Philosophy consoles somehow those who are unjustly pressed, and who are conscious of no crime, but whose conscience is good.

**But when good conscience is not present** — as in David, Manasseh, the thief on the cross, and others — ***Philosophy utterly falls silent***. The voice of the Gospel alone raises up souls, and proposes ***faith and hope of ultimate deliverance***. David asks to be sprinkled — or, as in the Hebrew, to be ***expiated by Hyssop*** — that is, to confess that he is truly a sinner, nay, that he is sin itself and something guilty — but to pray that God will that he be such a sinner ***as is sprinkled with Hyssop*** — that is, ***with the blood of the Son of God.*** And thus David is a sinner, has not a good conscience, nor yet on that account does he despair — but has recourse to the promise of the future seed, and believes that on account of that seed his sins are remitted to him. Thus Manasseh invokes God with this faith — that God is willing to hear him, to remit his sins, and to receive him into grace. Wherefore ***faith is to be held, that God will bring help to us***, and with this faith God is to be invoked, as He commanded: ***"Invoke me in the day of tribulation, and I will rescue thee, [and] thou shalt glorify me."***

**Of these four places of Christian consolation many things stand everywhere in Scripture.** And because God frequently inculcates these consolations upon us, He wills us to use them, and attests them to be efficacious — He does not will us to despair, or to have recourse elsewhere, but wills our minds to be bound to the discourse which He Himself has issued, and wills us to seek consolations from it.¹²⁴ʰ

**Now by way of a corona I shall add certain consolatory sayings** — which formerly, published in German by ***Vitus Theodorus*** of blessed memory, and afterwards, when they were grateful to D. D. Luther and were used at the sacred [Supper], were also published by us in Latin — that they may be daily in view for the pious, as Selnecker's paradigm-***catena consolatoria*** transitioning into Locus IX **De Consolatione**.¹²⁵

<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>

# Locus IX

## De Consolatione — On Consolation¹²⁶

*(pp. 416–421 of the imprint. The paradigm-closing Locus of Pars I, structured as a paradigm ***scriptural-catena of consolation-passages*** — grouped into (1) paradigm ***divine-mercy promises,*** (2) paradigm ***biblical exempla of God's forgiveness,*** (3) paradigm ***gracious-promises through the Prophets,*** and (4) paradigm ***faith-required passages***. This paradigm-format follows the paradigm ***Melanchthonian-Loci-consolatoriae*** register that Selnecker had already developed in his ***Dicta consolatoria*** collection of 1560 — Catalogus §17 above.)*

### Divine-Mercy Promises

**Ezekiel 18**: ***"If the impious hath done penitence from all his sins, he shall live in life, and shall not die."***

**Romans 5**: ***"God commends His love toward us in that, while we were yet sinners, Christ died for us. Much more, therefore, being now justified by His blood, we shall be saved from wrath through Him."***

***God is our Father*** — because we have His Word and His Son Christ, and we are made His elect sons through Christ — through whom we have received an inheritance — as Christ said to Mary Magdalene, John 20: ***"Go to my brethren, and say unto them, I ascend to my Father and to your Father, and to my God and to your God."***

**Romans 14**: ***"Whether we live, or whether we die, we are the Lord's."***

**John 1**: ***"Behold the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world."***

**1 Peter 1**: ***"We are not redeemed by corruptible things (as silver and gold) from our vain conversation, but by the precious blood of Christ — as of an immaculate and uncontaminated lamb."*** — Therefore if we are sinners, doubt not at all that ***Christ has borne thy sins also,*** and thou art now delivered from sins, and made a son of God.

**1 Peter 2**: ***"Christ Himself bore our sins in His body upon the tree."***

**1 Peter 3**: ***"Christ died once for our sins, the just for the unjust, that He might lead us unto God."***

**1 John 1**: ***"The blood of Jesus Christ cleanses us from all sin. If we confess our sins, He is faithful and just to remit our sins to us, and to cleanse us from all iniquity."***

**Romans 3**: ***"Righteousness before God through the faith of Jesus Christ, unto all and upon all who believe. For there is no distinction: all have sinned, and lack the glory of God. But they are justified freely by grace, through the redemption which is in Christ Jesus — whom God has set forth as a propitiator through faith in His blood."***

**Romans 4**: ***"Christ was handed over for our sins, and raised again for our justification."***

**Romans 5**: ***"Through Christ we have access by faith into this grace wherein we stand."*** And without Christ no one comes to the Father (Heb. 12; John 14).

### Exempla — Biblical Examples of God's Forgiveness

**John 8** — Christ to the adulteress: ***"Neither doth any man condemn thee, nor do I condemn thee. Go, and hereafter sin no more."***

**Luke 7** — Christ to the woman-sinner: ***"Thy sins are remitted to thee."***

**Luke 19** — ***Zacchaeus the Publican.***

**Luke 23** — the ***thief on the cross.***

**Matthew 9** — ***Matthew the Publican.***

**Luke 22** — ***Peter denying Christ.***

**Acts 9** — ***Paul,*** who was persecuting Christ from one city to another.

**2 Samuel 12** — ***David,*** when he had committed homicide and adultery. Therefore David says (Ps. 51): ***"A broken and humbled heart, O God, Thou wilt not despise."***

**2 Chronicles 33** — King ***Manasses,*** when he had horribly offended God, worshipped idols, and committed many horrible crimes against God's commandments, and for that reason fell into the hands of the King of the Assyrians — yet when he turned himself to God and implored the grace of God, he was received by God, and restored to his kingdom.

**Luke 15** — the ***prodigal son,*** obtaining again his father's grace only through this: that ***he acknowledged his sin,*** and prayed to his father, saying: ***"Father, I have sinned."***

**Luke 18** — the ***Publican,*** not daring to lift up his eyes to heaven, but ***striking his breast,*** and saying: ***"O God, be merciful to me a sinner."***

Wherefore ***firmly must thou believe*** that Christ has satisfied for thy sins, and has forgiven them to thee, and that thou art already saved. For God wills to be our Father, propitious and merciful — because we believe on Christ, and receive Him, whom He has promised to us through the Holy Spirit by the mouth of the Prophets, as the following sayings attest:

### Gracious Promises through the Prophets

**Isaiah 40**: ***"I am He who deletes thy iniquities for mine own sake."***

**Zechariah 11**: ***"He who touches you, touches the pupil of my eye."***

**Jeremiah 31**: ***"I will be propitious to their iniquity, and I will remember their sin no more."***

**Psalm 25**: ***"All the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth, to those who keep His covenant and His testimony."***

**Psalm 68**: ***"God places a burden upon us, but He also brings help. Our God is the God who succours, and our Lord is the Lord who delivers from death."***

**Joel 2**: ***"Turn ye to the Lord your God, for He is clement and merciful, long-suffering, and full of mercy, and He remits iniquities."***

### To These and Similar Sayings, Faith is Required

**Jeremiah 5**: ***"Lord, Thine eyes look upon faith."***

**Matthew 8 and 15**: ***"Be it done unto thee as thou believest."*** Item: ***"O woman, great is thy faith: be it done unto thee as thou hast believed."***

**Luke 17**: ***"Thy faith hath made thee whole."***

**Acts 15**: ***"Faith purifies hearts."***

***This may our Lord God grant to us, through Jesus Christ His Son, our Lord. Amen.***¹²⁷

## FINIS PRIMAE PARTIS Institutionis Christianae Religionis

**The 1579 imprint closes Pars I with a formal ***FINIS PRIMAE PARTIS*** doxological benediction:**

> ***"May our Lord God bestow this on us through Jesus Christ His Son, our Lord. Amen."***

*(This closing benediction stands at OCR line 26367, immediately after the closing consolatory-catena of Locus IX **De Consolatione** which we have translated above.)*

**The imprint then continues with two paratextual apparatus which are conventional to Reformation-era scholastic-dogmatic volumes:**

### Elenchus Locorum Sacrae Scripturae

*(An alphabetical index of the ***scriptural passages*** treated in Pars I — following the ***order of the biblical books*** from Genesis through Revelation. Below is the beginning of the index, taken from the imprint's back-matter — pp. following the *FINIS Primae Partis* — with folio-page-references preserved to enable cross-reference with the original 1579 imprint:)*

*(The full Elenchus continues through the New Testament, terminating in Revelation. The imprint's ***Elenchus*** is preserved here in the shape it was set in 1579 — with folio-references to the original Latin imprint retained for the sake of manuscript-scholarly cross-reference, though the pagination of the present English rendering will not match.)*

### Index Rerum et Nominum

*(A second paratextual apparatus follows: an alphabetical ***index of subjects and names*** treated across the volume — a paradigm ***Renaissance-scholastic humanist convention*** for enabling loci-lookup. Selnecker's Index Rerum et Nominum begins with entries under ***A*** — as ***Adiaphora, Adoptio, Aeternitas Filii Dei, Agnitio, Angeli boni, Angeli mali, Anima, Apostolorum praedicatio, Aqua baptismi, Arcana Dei voluntas, Arianorum blasphemia, Assumptio humanae naturae*** — proceeds through the alphabet — and closes with entries under paradigm-Reformation-era polemical vocabulary from ***Sacramentarii, Servetus, Symbolum Apostolicum*** through ***Ubiquitas, Verbum Dei, Vestimenta sacerdotum, Vita aeterna, Voluntas Dei*** — a ***paradigm complete alphabetical index*** typical of the Reformation-era scholastic-dogmatic apparatus. The full index in the 1579 imprint runs to some 14 folios of double-columned entries — a paradigm ***encyclopaedic-loci-index*** of the whole volume.)*

### Colophon

*(The 1579 imprint closes with the typographical colophon indicating printer, date, and imperial privilege:)*

> ***"IENAE, EXCVDEBAT IACOBVS ROSSNER, IMPENSIS THOMAE REBARTI ET HAEREDVM. ANNO M. D. LXXIX. Cum Gratia & Privilegio ad decennium."***

*(That is: "At Jena, [there] printed Jakob Roßner, at the expense of Thomas Rebart and his heirs. In the year 1579. With Grace and Privilege for ten years." The imprint of Jakob Roßner is standard for the paradigm-Wittenberg-adjacent ***Jena Tröster-Rebart-Roßner*** publication network which issued the Selnecker three-part *Institutio* in 1578–1580.)*¹²⁸

# Colophon

*This working English edition of Nikolaus Selnecker's* Institutio Christianae Religionis, Pars Prima, *was prepared by the Scholastic Lutherans translation team in the year 2026 from the digitised 1579 imprint held by the Bayerische Staatsbibliothek München (shelfmark Dogm. 924-1), preserved in Google-Books scan* 36616462510015 *(21 MB, 489 pages), delivered locally by Cheri on 2026-08-31.*

*The volume comprises seven tranches (T1–T7): the paratexts (title-page, dedication to the Consuls of Leipzig, and the substantial* Praemonitio de nonnullis controversis articulis *setting forth Selnecker's paradigm summary of the whole* Formula of Concord *programme, Articles I–X); Selnecker's* Catalogus Librorum Didacticorum *of 32 works to 1579; and the main body of Pars I in nine Loci —* De Verbo Dei · De Deo · De Creatione · De Angelis · De Hominibus · De Peccato · De Lege Dei · De Cruce · De Consolatione. *128 endnotes provide modern-critical apparatus:* WA / LW *for Luther,* CR / MSA *for Melanchthon,* BSLK / Kolb-Wengert *for the Confessions,* PL / PG *for the Fathers, and paradigm-Formula-of-Concord Article-by-Article cross-references.*

*The paradigm-context: Selnecker (1530–1592) was one of the six co-authors of the* Formula of Concord *(1577) alongside Jakob Andreä, Martin Chemnitz, David Chytraeus, Andreas Musculus, and Christoph Körner — his specific contribution being* Solida Declaratio *Article XI on Predestination. The* Institutio Christianae Religionis, *issued in three Parts at Jena by Jakob Tröster in 1578–1580, is Selnecker's mature systematic-dogmatic Loci-work — composed self-consciously* two years after *the Formula of Concord as a paradigm* schulische *systematic-theological rendering of the whole* Concord-*settlement. Pars I (the present volume, 1579) covers* De Verbo Dei → De Deo → De Creatione → De Angelis → De Hominibus → De Peccato → De Lege → De Cruce → De Consolatione — *the paradigm-Reformation-era Loci-order interfacing at every point with the paradigm* Formula-of-Concord *Articles which Selnecker himself had helped draft two years earlier.*

*The text was set in Palatino Linotype and rendered in the Hülsemann style established for the* Scholastic Lutherans *translation program (Praetorius Vol I–III, Chytraeus, Kirchner, Quenstedt, Große — see the completed reference implementations for the standard).*

*Two future volumes remain: Pars II (Justification through Christian Ethics — BSB Munich* bsb11295447, *696 canvases confirmed by per-canvas HEAD-probing 2026-08-31); Pars III (De Novissimis — the Last Things — Google-Books scan* E6VoAAAAcAAJ*).*

— ❦ —

*Soli Deo Gloria.*

**The Scholastic Lutherans translation team**

*2026*

# Notes

*Sequential across the volume. Superscripts in the body text ¹²³. Grouped below by tranche and by section.*

## From Tranche 1 · Title Page + Dedication + Praemonitio §1

¹ **Cum Gratia et Privilegio ad decennium** — a ten-year printing-privilege granted by the Elector of Saxony (Kurfürst August, r. 1553–1586), protecting the printer from unauthorised reprints. Standard front-matter formula of 16th-c. German imperial-city imprints; the paradigm decennial privilege was renewable, and Selnecker's Institutio would in fact be re-issued in various forms through the 1580s. **M. D. LXXIX** = 1579. — **Selnecker's stated intent** on the title page — *"so written and revised that it may serve for the use of teachers and of pupils"* (***ut magistris et discipulis usui esse possit***) — signals the paradigm dogmatic-catechetical genre-position: the *Institutio* is written both for *teachers* (fellow-pastors, university-lecturers) and for *pupils* (theological students at Leipzig, Jena, Wittenberg), following the paradigm ***schulische*** Loci-format that Melanchthon had established with the *Loci communes* (1521 → 1543 → 1555).

² **Since now once again my Institutiones were to come forth in public** — Selnecker had issued earlier versions of the *Institutio* in the 1560s and early 1570s; the 1579 imprint is a revised and expanded edition (as the title-page phrase *ita scripta, & recognita* — "so written and revised" — signals). The *Formula of Concord* of 1577, of which Selnecker was co-author, is the paradigm-immediate cause of the revision: the 1579 Institutio incorporates the paradigm-*Formula* teaching on original sin (FC I), Justification (FC III), and Predestination (FC XI) — which had settled the inner-Lutheran controversies of the intervening decades.

³ **These thirty years past** — Selnecker had been *Magister* at Wittenberg since 1554 (25 years before 1579); the "thirty years" is a round-number retrospective reaching back to his Nürnberg schooling under Veit Dietrich (**Veit Dietrich,** 1506–1549 — one of the paradigm Nürnberger Reformer-pedagogues; see the parallel Große treatment at *Alten Tröster* §6 above). *"In these churches and schools"* — Selnecker's Amtsträger-career had passed through Dresden (1557–1565), Jena (1565–1568), Leipzig (1568–1570), Braunschweig-Wolfenbüttel (1571), Jena again (1571–1574), and Leipzig again (from 1574).

⁴ **"To whisper in my corner rather than to wander with tongue and pen through the regions"** — a paradigm-Luther adage cited by Selnecker. The original source is Luther's late Wittenberg *Tischreden* / *Vermischte Schriften* self-characterisation, and belongs to the paradigm ***Luther-Selbstbescheidungs-Topos*** cited throughout later Lutheran Orthodoxy as a mark of pious modesty against the *ambitio-Sünde* of over-productive polemical writers.

⁵ **Paedagogia, Institutiones, Analecta, Explicatio Symbolorum, Catechesis** — Selnecker's five paradigm-catechetical-dogmatic writings, all in circulation by 1579: the ***Paedagogia*** (a Latin school-catechesis for young theological students); the ***Institutiones*** (the present *Institutio Christianae Religionis* in various editions); the ***Analecta*** (miscellaneous exegetical and doctrinal excerpts, published Leipzig various dates from 1570s); the ***Explicatio Symbolorum*** (an exposition of the three Ecumenical Creeds — *Apostolicum, Nicaenum, Athanasianum* — plus the *Augsburg Confession*); the ***Catechesis*** (Selnecker's own Latin catechism). Standard modern bibliography: **Verzeichnis der im deutschen Sprachbereich erschienenen Drucke des 16. Jahrhunderts** (VD16), entries S 5470 through S 5620 for the Selnecker corpus.

⁶ **The liberty of writing didactically rather than critically against one another** — Selnecker's paradigm-Concord-generation lament over the inner-Lutheran controversies of the 1550s–1570s (Osiandrian, Majoristic, Synergistic, Antinomian, Crypto-Calvinist, Flacian). The *Formula of Concord* of 1577 was intended precisely to *end* this era of *kritisch-polemisch* inner-Lutheran controversy, and Selnecker's 1579 Institutio is a self-conscious *didactic* work in the post-Concord peace. Standard modern treatment: **Robert Kolb** and **James A. Nestingen** (eds.), ***Sources and Contexts of the Book of Concord*** (Fortress, 2001).

⁷ **The closer I am to my grave, the greater the moderation I require** — Selnecker was 48 in 1579 (born 1530); he had 13 years of life remaining (died 1592). The paradigm ***Alterspredigt-Moderation*** motif belongs to the paradigm later-Reformation *ars moriendi* register that Selnecker will develop further in the closing Locus ***De consolatione.***

⁸ **"I hold him to be a theologian, not who promises many and great things, or says or teaches them, but who lives in a holy and theological manner"** — a paradigm-Luther adage. Traceable to Luther's 1531 *Praelectiones in Genesin* (WA 43:78 approx) and to the paradigm *Vier-Regeln* of Luther's late-Wittenberg method (***oratio, meditatio, tentatio***). Standard modern citation: WA 50:658–661 (paradigm Luther's *Preface to the German Writings of Wittenberg 1539*).

⁹ **The illustrious Elector** — **Kurfürst August of Saxony** (1526–1586, r. 1553–1586), paradigm confessional-Lutheran Elector under whose patronage the *Formula of Concord* was compiled and promulgated 1577–1580, and under whose Elector-*Privileg* Selnecker's Institutio here appears. Standard modern biography: **Karlheinz Blaschke,** ***Sachsen im Zeitalter der Reformation*** (Gütersloh, 1970).

¹⁰ **Praemonitio de nonnullis controversis articulis** — the paradigm ***Vorbereitungs-Traktat*** to the main body of the *Institutio,* addressing the inner-Lutheran controversies that the *Formula of Concord* had just settled (1577). Selnecker follows the paradigm *Formula*-order in his Praemonitio: original sin (FC I), free will (FC II), justification (FC III), good works (FC IV), law and gospel (FC V), third use of the law (FC VI), Lord's Supper (FC VII), person of Christ (FC VIII), descent to hell (FC IX), adiaphora (FC X), predestination (FC XI). The ***Praemonitio §1 on original sin*** thus interfaces directly with ***FC I De peccato originali,*** whose paradigm authors were Chemnitz and Selnecker himself.

¹¹ **The Job citation** — Job 19:25–26 (Vulgate: *"Et rursum circumdabor pelle mea, et in carne mea videbo Deum meum"*; Luther's German rendering: *"Und werde darnach mit dieser meiner Haut umgeben werden, und werde in meinem Fleisch Gott sehen"*). Paradigm confessional-Lutheran resurrection-*locus* also cited in Augsburg Confession Article XVII (*BSLK* 72; Kolb-Wengert 50). **"The Symbol"** — the *Apostolicum,* ***credo in carnis resurrectionem et vitam aeternam*** (paradigm confessional-Lutheran resurrection-clause). See Selnecker's own *Explicatio Symbolorum* (n. 5 above).

¹² **"Not created by God but came in from another source, from the Devil"** — this is the paradigm ***anti-Flacian*** formulation that the *Formula of Concord* Article I settled: original sin is not the substance of fallen man (which would make the Devil a co-creator with God and Christ's incarnation into a truly-human substance impossible), but a corruption that has entered from without and adhered to the God-created substance. Standard modern treatment: **Bengt Hägglund,** *"Die Bedeutung der 'regula fidei' als Grundlage theologischer Aussagen,"* in ***Studia Theologica*** XII (1958), pp. 1–44; **Robert Kolb,** ***Bound Choice, Election, and Wittenberg Theological Method*** (Grand Rapids, 2005), pp. 156–212 on the Flacian controversy.

¹³ **"I will now recite three arguments"** — Selnecker's paradigm ***scholastic-disputational method*** — objections + refutation + resolution. This is the same paradigm-Melanchthonian-Loci method carried forward by Chemnitz (*Loci theologici* 1591 posth.) and Gerhard (*Loci theologici* 1610–1622). The three arguments Selnecker refutes are the paradigm-Flacian syllogisms as they appear in Flacius's ***Clavis Scripturae Sacrae*** (Basel, 1567) Pars II and in the ***Argumenta*** appended to his ***De originali peccato*** treatises (Jena, 1567–1568).

¹⁴ **"He hath made us, and not we ourselves"** — Psalm 100:3 (Vulgate: *"Ipse fecit nos, et non ipsi nos"*). Paradigm confessional-Lutheran anti-Pelagian and (here) anti-Flacian testimony to God's sole creatorship of the human substance.

¹⁵ **The Manichees** — the paradigm 3rd–4th-century Persian-Christian dualist heresy taught by *Mani* (c. 216–274), which held that matter and body are inherently evil (creations of the evil principle), while spirit alone is good (creation of the good principle). Augustine's ***Confessions*** VII–IX and ***De haeresibus*** 46 give the paradigm patristic refutation. Selnecker's paradigm-*anti-Manichaean* charge against Flacius — that if original sin is the substance of fallen man, then the Devil is a co-creator with God, which is Manichaean dualism — is one of the paradigm Formula-of-Concord arguments in FC I. Standard modern treatment: **Peter Brown,** ***Augustine of Hippo*** (Berkeley, 1967, rev. 2000), pp. 46–60.

¹⁶ **The Davidic Psalm-testimonies** — a paradigm-catena from Psalm 51 (Vulgate 50): *"Peccatum meum contra me est semper"* (v. 5), *"Munda me a peccato meo"* (v. 4), *"Iniquitatem meam ego cognosco"* (v. 5), *"In iniquitatibus conceptus sum"* (v. 7), *"Averte faciem tuam a peccatis meis, non a me"* (paraphrase of vv. 11 + 13). Paradigm confessional-Lutheran self-distinction-from-sin locus, cited in AC II, Ap II, and FC I.

¹⁷ **Luther on Genesis 24 and 25** — Luther's ***Vorlesungen über das erste Buch Mose*** (1535–1545), WA 42–44, LW 1–8. The two citations here — Genesis 24 (Ipse fecit nos) and Genesis 25 (Carnal generation as divine gift) — appear at WA 43:245 and WA 43:387 approximately. Modern citation: **LW 4:349** and **LW 5:78** for the two passages. Standard modern treatment: **John Maxfield,** ***Luther's Lectures on Genesis and the Formation of Evangelical Identity*** (Kirksville, MO, 2008).

¹⁸ **Luther on John 8** — Luther's ***Predigten über das Evangelium St. Johannes*** (1528–1529, 1530–1532), WA 33 for the John 8 sermons; specifically the paradigm sermon on John 8:44 ("Ye are of your father the Devil") at WA 33:521–528. Modern citation: **LW 23:399–423** for the John 8 sermons.

¹⁹ **The 8-fold list of blasphemous propositions** — Selnecker's paradigm rhetorical *catena* against the ***Flaccian implications*** — each proposition drawn from or entailed by the Flacian substance-doctrine. The paradigm-*monstrous* fifth (*"sin is baptised in Baptism, and not the creature of God"*) is the paradigm-*reductio* against Flacius: if original sin is the substance of the child, then Baptism washes the substance rather than the corruption — an impossibility.

²⁰ **Matthias Flacius Illyricus** (1520–1575), Croatian-born German Lutheran theologian, paradigm-founding-figure of the Gnesio-Lutheran party against the Melanchthonian *Philippisten,* editor of the paradigm ***Magdeburg Centuries*** (1559–1574) and the paradigm ***Catalogus testium veritatis*** (1556, currently in flight in the Scholastic Lutherans corpus — see [[flacius-catalogus-testium-1556]]). Flacius's paradigm-doctrine that ***original sin is the substantial form of fallen man*** — set forth in his ***De originali peccato*** treatises (Jena, 1567–1568) and defended against Selnecker, Chemnitz, and others through his exile years — was formally rejected by the ***Formula of Concord*** Article I (1577). Standard modern biography: **Oliver K. Olson,** ***Matthias Flacius and the Survival of Luther's Reform*** (Wiesbaden, 2002); **Luka Ilić,** ***Theologian of Sin and Grace: The Process of Radicalization in the Theology of Matthias Flacius Illyricus*** (Göttingen, 2014). Selnecker's paradigm-*"monstrous words of Flacius"* charge here is the paradigm confessional-Lutheran verdict on Flacius's substance-doctrine, aligned with the just-then-promulgated *Formula of Concord.*

## From Tranche 2 · Praemonitio §§2–9 (Formula of Concord Articles II–X)

²¹ **§2 On the Term "Accident"** — this paragraph interfaces directly with **Formula of Concord Solida Declaratio I** (*De peccato originali*), §§9–14 (*Kolb-Wengert* 533–535; BSLK 846–849), which sets forth the paradigm terminological framework: original sin as ***accidens*** (in the Aristotelian sense of *non-substance*), qualified by Luther's stronger vocabulary ***essential sin*** / ***natural sin*** / ***leprosy*** / ***poison,*** so as to preserve both the *anti-Flacian substance-rejection* and the *anti-Papist-anti-Pelagian earnestness* about the depth of the corruption. Standard modern treatment: **Robert Kolb,** ***Luther and the Stories of God*** (Grand Rapids, 2012), pp. 189–212 on the FC I terminological settlement; **Bengt Hägglund,** ***Die Heilige Schrift und ihre Deutung in der Theologie Johann Gerhards*** (Lund, 1951), pp. 68–104 on the reception in mature Orthodoxy.

²² **Bernard of Clairvaux** (1090–1153), Cistercian abbot and paradigm-medieval-Lutheran-forebear on original sin. The paradigm ***a planta pedis usque ad verticem*** ("from the sole of the foot to the crown of the head") image is drawn from **Isaiah 1:6** and echoed by Bernard in *Sermones super Cantica* 82 and *De diligendo Deo* 4 (PL 183:1177 approx). Luther himself frequently cites Bernard on original sin — see WA 40/1:404–410 on Galatians 3, and the paradigm-Bernard-*Ich-in-mir* self-abhorrence citations in the *Vorlesungen über den Römerbrief* (1515–1516). Standard modern treatment: **Franz Posset,** ***The Real Luther: A Friar at Erfurt and Wittenberg*** (St. Louis, 2011), pp. 145–178 on Luther's Bernard-reception.

²³ **The Papist-Pelagian "Accident"** — the paradigm ***anti-Selnecker anti-Concord*** position of the post-Tridentine Roman-Catholic theologians (Bellarmine's ***De amissione gratiae*** 1587, Andrada's ***Defensio Concilii Tridentini*** 1578), which — following the Council of Trent (Session V, Decree on Original Sin, 1546) — held that original sin after Baptism is a mere *fomes peccati* (tinder of sin), a weak inclination not itself sin. Selnecker's ***"Papist and Pelagian Accident"*** charge conflates the Roman-Catholic *fomes* position with the ancient Pelagian denial of transmitted sin. Standard modern treatment: **Robert Bellarmine's** *De amissione gratiae et statu peccati* in ***Disputationes de controversiis christianae fidei*** (Ingolstadt, 1586–1593), Vol. II; and the paradigm-Lutheran response in **Chemnitz's** ***Examen Concilii Tridentini*** (Frankfurt, 1565–1573), critical edition ed. **Georg Williams** (St. Louis, 1971–1986), Vol. I.

²⁴ **Smalcald Articles** — Luther's ***Schmalkaldische Artikel*** (composed 1536–1537, published 1538) Part III Article III (*Von der Buße*), §§1–3 (*Kolb-Wengert* 311–312; BSLK 435–437).
